# Beyond Work: Preparing a Basic Income for Australia's AI Transition

Prepared by Simon, Founder, Mai Software, Sydney, New South Wales. Revised 31 October 2026 after an external critique.

**Working draft.** This paper is updated regularly; its figures and recommendations may change between revisions. This version: 31 October 2026.

About this submission: Mai Software is the body responsible for this submission and its analysis, and Simon prepared it as its Founder. The author's full name and postal and email contact details are on the lodgement cover sheet. Parliamentary committees remove personal contact details before they publish submissions, so they are not repeated here [S1].

*Conventions.* Dollar amounts are Australian dollars (A$) unless marked US$ (United States dollars), € (euros), £ (pounds sterling) or C$ (Canadian dollars). Sources are cited as [S1], [S2] and so on, and listed in Section 11. Exhibits marked [CHART] or [TIMELINE] are drawn as charts or timelines; their data are lodged with the supplementary files. Each table says which design its figures were run on. "P\*" is the recommended Stage 1; "Part 1" is the design the 22 October 2026 paper recommended, which this revision rejects; "the targeted package" is the strongest targeted package. Figures from model runs that rest on assumptions made for this revision, or that have not yet been through the arithmetic checker, are marked provisional.

<a id="changes"></a>

## Changes in this revision

This version supersedes all earlier versions, including the paper of 22 October 2026. An external critique of that paper made ten criticisms. The author accepts five in full and four in part; the remaining one, that winners were headlined before the funding taxes (criticism 5), holds for the online calculator rather than the paper. The main change is that the recommendation now follows from the paper's own comparison.

1. **Targeted welfare called basic income.** Accepted in part. The paper is retitled. Stage 1 is called what it is, a targeted floor, and the universal stages are called a conditional pathway (Sections 1 and 5).
2. **It recommended about 0.56 million more people in poverty.** Accepted. Stage 1 is now P\*, the design the paper's own figures favour over Part 1. The targeted package is offered as Parliament's alternative if it weights poverty above work incentives, and the price of preferring P\* to it is stated. Part 1 is shown as the rejected option, with its price (Section 5).
3. **Its own appraisal found Stage 1 destroys value.** Accepted in part. The summary now says every Stage 1 design's net benefit is negative on the complete accounting and spans zero on the paper's method, and ranks them, gives each design's break-even distributional weight, and says the case rests on cutting poverty, weighed against work incentives and fiscal sustainability, not on a positive monetised benefit (Sections 1 and 6).
4. **The universal destination is blocked or unaffordable.** Accepted. Parliament is asked to legislate the tests (an AI need test, a randomised trial and a PBO certificate), not rates. Stage 3 is described, not authorised (Section 5).
5. **Winners were headlined before the funding taxes.** Accepted for the calculator. The paper now leads with results after the funding taxes and gives each household decile's share of the five taxes (Sections 1 and 6).
6. **"No current recipient loses" is narrow.** Accepted. The guarantee is restated as what it is, that no one's payment falls under today's rules, and the households worse off after housing costs are counted (Sections 5 and 6).
7. **The Budget advance is borrowing.** Accepted. The summary calls it borrowing and gives the amount. A joint stress test gives the odds of an earlier levy, and a phased start that avoids the borrowing is costed (Sections 1 and 6).
8. **The default dividend adds machinery.** Accepted in part. The dividend options move to their own chapter with their own tests, and none is recommended. The central fiscal path is rebuilt on the recommended design with no dividend (Sections 6 and 10).
9. **Precision exceeds predictive power.** Accepted in part. Uncertain dates and rates are given as ranges, and each table names the design it was run on. The calculator's fixes are listed separately.
10. **The easy objections were answered best.** Accepted. "Why legislate any universal pathway?" is now the first objection, followed by "Why not the targeted package?" and "Who pays?" (Section 8).

Three corrections were made while answering the critique.

- **P\* as first costed was not at Part 1's budget.** The variant the 22 October paper reported (A$6,973 a child, with automatic enrolment) cost A$6.2bn more than Part 1 in 2028-29 on the fiscal model's basis. The recommended P\* is re-costed at Part 1's budget: A$6,750 a child, the floor and Rent Assistance +75%, without automatic enrolment. On that basis it leaves 10.5% of people in poverty after housing costs, not 10.1%; 0.20 million fewer people in poverty than Part 1, not 0.32 million; and A$1.5bn to A$1.8bn a year more net benefit on the complete accounting than Part 1, not A$2.4bn to A$3.2bn (Table 5.7).
- **The OpenResearch trial's fall in labour-force participation is 3.9 percentage points**, the figure in the trial's latest revision [S82], not 4.2. At 3.9 points the trial's work-effort cap binds with probability 0.79 to 0.84, not 0.86 to 0.91, so a universal Stage 2 would pass with probability 0.16 to 0.21, not 0.09 to 0.14, and Part 1's expected saving as Stage 2's base rises from A$0.05bn to A$0.25bn to A$0.09bn to A$0.37bn once. The paper uses the 3.9-point figures throughout.
- **"No current recipient loses" did not hold after housing costs.** On Part 1, 1.11 million recipient households were worse off once higher rents and interest rates were counted (Table 6.21).

The paper also adds newer evidence on AI and jobs (Section 2). Table 8.6 gives each criticism, the answer and where the paper changed.

## 1. Executive summary

Australia should fix the adequacy of income support now with a targeted floor. Among the designs of equal cost it compares, the paper chooses P\*, weighing poverty against work incentives and fiscal sustainability. It should also legislate the tests a universal basic income would have to pass, so the country can move quickly if AI displaces work at scale. It should not legislate a universal rate or a dividend yet, because on current evidence neither pays its way. The case for a basic income is insurance against a shock whose probability no one can yet estimate.

After the five taxes that pay for it are counted, 34.2% of adults live in households better off under the recommended Stage 1 in 2028-29, or 32.4% by A$5 a week or more, against 32.5% under Part 1; before the taxes, 65.3% do. Of households, 35.4% are better off after the taxes and 62.1% gain before them (Table 6.14, provisional). The taxes fall mostly on the top income decile, which bears 52.9% of them (Table 6.15). Poverty after housing costs falls from 14.2% to 10.5% in the model.

The recommended Stage 1, called P\* in this paper, starts on 1 July 2028 and has three parts:

- a floor for working-age payments at the poverty line, A$569.81 a week (A$32,570 a year in 2028-29);
- a child payment of A$6,750 a child, paid with each family's maximum Family Tax Benefit (FTB) and withdrawn at 18 cents a dollar of parents' income above A$72,631, with no cliffs; and
- a 75% rise in the maximum rates of Commonwealth Rent Assistance.

It is recommended over Part 1, the design the 22 October paper recommended, because the paper's own comparison favours it. At the same budget it leaves 0.20 million fewer people in poverty than Part 1, less poverty in every group shown and in every robustness run, and A$1.5bn to A$1.8bn a year more net benefit on the complete accounting (A$0.8bn to A$3.1bn across the robustness runs). Part 1 paid a supplement to middle-income families so that a universal Stage 2 would later pay it to every child; that saves at most A$0.09bn to A$0.37bn in expectation, far less than its price (Table 5.7).

The targeted package is Parliament's alternative if it weights poverty above work incentives and fiscal risk. At the same cost it cuts poverty further, to 9.0% against 10.5%, and leaves 0.40 million fewer people in poverty than P\*. It is ahead of P\* by A$2.0bn to A$4.3bn a year on the paper's method and by about A$10.7bn a year with the Green Book's weights; on the complete accounting it is between A$1.9bn a year behind and A$2.3bn ahead. On average it also treats working families better: second earners' mean participation tax rate is 28.9% against 33.0% under P\*. P\*'s advantages are at the extreme and in the funding. Under P\* 1.3% of higher earners in families with children face an effective tax rate of 60% or more, against 14.2% under the package, and no second earner does, against 6.8%. And the package needs A$108.1bn of levies to 2045-46 against A$36.5bn, with an 87% against a 74% chance of a levy before 2044-45 (Tables 5.7, 6.9 and 6.12; provisional). Preferring P\* is a value judgement that those advantages are worth the price. Parliament may judge otherwise.

Five facts weigh against the recommendation, and Parliament should know them.

1. **The Budget advance is borrowing.** The five taxes fall A$20.7bn short of P\*'s cost in 2028-29 (provisional), and the Budget borrows the gap, repaid with interest by 30 June 2032 (Table 6.6). Starting the child payment a year after the floor would avoid the borrowing, at the price of a year without the child payment, which would have paid families A$26.4bn above the Family Tax Benefit they keep (the budget saves A$37.3bn) (Table 6.7).
2. **Every Stage 1 design has a negative net benefit on the complete accounting.** On the paper's method, which counts the economic cost of the taxes, the ranges span zero: P\* −A$12.1bn to A$3.3bn, the package −A$10.1bn to A$7.6bn and Part 1 −A$12.8bn to A$1.9bn a year in 2028-29. On the complete accounting, which also counts Stage 1's own behavioural costs, P\* scores −A$23.3bn to −A$2.3bn in 2028-29 and the targeted package −A$25.2bn to nil (both provisional), and Part 1 −A$24.8bn to −A$4.1bn. Part 1 ranks below P\* on every reading. The case for Stage 1 rests on cutting poverty, weighed against work incentives and fiscal sustainability, not on a positive monetised benefit. P\* breaks even at a distributional weight of 1.5 to 3.3 on the paper's method, or 2.3 to 4.9 on the complete accounting; the package at 1.3 to 2.4 or 2.0 to 3.8; and Part 1 at 1.6 to 3.9 or 2.8 to 6.1; against the 1.5 to 2.0 the paper uses (Table 6.12, provisional).
3. **The guarantee covers payments, not income after housing costs.** No one's payment falls under today's rules. But after higher rents and interest rates, 0.70 million recipient households (1.65 million people, 16% of recipient households) are worse off under P\*, by A$770 a year on average, mostly Disability Support Pension households (0.28 million) and Age Pension households that own or are buying their home (0.26 million), whom Rent Assistance does not reach. P\*'s Rent Assistance rise removes 37% of Part 1's 1.11 million (Table 6.21, provisional).
4. **The five taxes fall short each year from 2037-38.** Surpluses kept from the 2030s fill the gap until a levy on market income from 2043-44 or 2044-45 on central yields, depending on how Rent Assistance grows, totalling A$19.8bn to A$36.5bn to 2045-46 (provisional). A joint stress test puts the chance of a levy before 2044-45 at 48% to 74%, and before 2034-35 at 37% to 59% (Table 6.9, provisional). P\* carries the same funding risk as Part 1.
5. **The universal stages will probably never start.** Stage 2 would need every income-tax rate 15.6 to 25.5 points higher (15.6 on the smallest displacement that meets the need test, up to 25.5 on slower paths timed to the trigger), and at the trial's latest estimate the work-effort cap stops it with probability 0.79 to 0.84. A full basic income at the Age Pension rate (Stage 3) would need about 30.9 points, and no rate passes its funding test on any modelled path (Section 6).

The universal stages therefore become a tested, pre-committed pathway. The Act would set the AI need test, the randomised trial and the Parliamentary Budget Office (PBO) certificate a universal payment must pass, and no rates. Stage 3 is described but not authorised. No dividend is recommended now; four options are set out in Section 10, each with its tests.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

P\*'s and the package's figures are provisional. Stage 2 and 3 figures were run with Part 1 and the wealth-tax fund's dividend on the smallest displacement that meets the need test and have not been rerun on P\*. P\* beats Part 1 in every poverty group across three random seeds and three calibrations of the household model (Table 5.7).*

- In the model P\* cuts child poverty from 15.6% to 6.6% and poverty in sole-parent households from 31.9% to 14.8%. Poverty in households relying on the Disability Support Pension falls from 63.6% to 52.6%, but at 67 and over it rises slightly on the relative line, from 11.1% to 11.6%, because the line rises with the median (Table 5.7).
- Second earners keep less under P\*'s taper than today: their mean participation tax rate is 33.0%, against 28.9% under the package, 32.9% under Part 1 and 27.3% under current rules, but none faces 60% or more (Tables 5.7 and 5.8).
- The paper's earlier choice, Part 1, would have kept 0.20 million more people in poverty than P\* and 0.56 million to 0.61 million more than the package, to save at most A$0.09bn to A$0.37bn once, in expectation, if a universal Stage 2 ever starts.

### The problem

AI's early effects on jobs are uncertain: there is no aggregate displacement yet, but hiring of young workers into the most exposed jobs has slowed in the United States [S2, S26, S225, S226]. JobSeeker pays a single adult A$412 a week against a A$570 poverty line [S3, S4].

### Key risks

1. Revenue shortfall: the five taxes must yield 100.2% of their costings for no levy before 2044-45 under P\* (99.7% under Part 1); a joint stress test gives a 48% to 74% chance of a levy before then (Table 6.9).
2. Housing: after housing costs, 0.70 million recipient households are worse off under P\*, and on Part 1's run higher rents and interest rates lifted poverty by 0.3 to 0.7 points (Tables 6.21 and 6.32).
3. Early AI displacement: with no fund to lend, the levy in a severe shock reached 1.96% of market income in 2034-35 and 6.17% by 2045-46 on Part 1's run (Table 6.51).

### Decisions needed now (Table 9.1)

1. Pass the Australian Basic Income (ABI) Act with P\* as Stage 1 by 31 December 2027, or the targeted package if Parliament weights poverty above work incentives.
2. Legislate the five durable taxes, the AI need test, the randomised trial and the PBO certificate; legislate no universal rate and no dividend.
3. Refer this paper to a joint select committee reporting by 30 September 2027, and ask the PBO to cost P\* (Recommendation 12a).

## 2. The case for change

Government should act now on the safety net, which already leaves millions in poverty, and prepare for AI, which is advancing quickly but has not yet displaced workers across the economy. That difference is why the paper proposes a targeted floor now and only tests for anything universal.

### AI capability is rising quickly

One benchmark shows AI capability growing quickly and steadily. METR (Model Evaluation and Threat Research), a non-profit research organisation, measures the length of software task an AI agent can complete with 50% success. That length doubled about every 7 months from 2019 to 2024 [S6], and METR's January 2026 update puts the doubling time at about 4.3 months since 2023 [S7]. These are METR's own benchmark measurements, not peer-reviewed studies (Table 2.1). Figure 2.1 plots them against each model's public release date [S8, S9, S10, S11, S12, S13, S14].

**Figure 2.1 [CHART]. Length of software task that frontier AI systems complete with 50% success, by model release date, March 2023 to April 2026 (minutes, log scale)**

![Figure 2.1: AI task length by model release date](charts/fig2_1_metr_time_horizon.png)

*Source: METR, Time Horizon 1.1, January 2026, for the seven models shown [S7]; release dates from each developer's announcement [S8, S9, S10, S11, S12, S13, S14]; Claude Mythos Preview plotted at its announcement on 7 April 2026 [S15], with the lower bound of at least 16 hours from a secondary report of METR's March 2026 evaluation [S16]. Data: charts/data/fig2_1_metr_time_horizon.csv [S5]. Note: the measure covers mainly software tasks.*

The inputs to AI are growing at a similar pace: training compute for frontier models has grown about 5 times a year since 2020 [S17]. The price of running a model at fixed capability has fallen 9 to 900 times a year [S18]. Anthropic reports that its Claude Mythos preview, announced in April 2026, solved 93.9% of tasks on a standard software-engineering test, against 80.8% for Claude Opus 4.6, on its own scoring [S15, S19].

These measures have limits: the time-horizon measure covers mainly coding, a 50% success rate is not reliable unsupervised work, and models still do worse on messy real-world tasks [S20]. A randomised trial in early 2025 found experienced developers took 19% longer with AI tools [S21], although a 2026 follow-up points to modest speed-ups [S22].

### Many jobs are exposed, mostly through augmentation

The International Monetary Fund (IMF) estimates that about 60% of jobs in advanced economies are exposed to AI, half likely to benefit and half to face lower demand [S23]. The International Labour Organization puts 34% of employment in high-income countries in exposed occupations [S24]. Jobs and Skills Australia (JSA) finds that about 4% of the Australian workforce is in occupations highly exposed to automation and 31% to augmentation. In its illustrative modelling, clerks, receptionists and bookkeepers lose most while nurses and construction labourers gain [S25].

### No aggregate displacement yet, but an early warning in entry-level hiring

There is no sign yet that AI is cutting employment across the economy. The Yale Budget Lab finds the mix of occupations in the United States not yet changing with AI [S29, S226], and Danish data rule out effects above 2% [S227]. Treasury's 2026 Intergenerational Report finds no significant Australian effects yet [S30], and most forecasts reviewed by the Productivity Commission (PC) expect a net jobs gain, though the PC calls the job effects "highly uncertain" [S32].

The early warning is in entry-level hiring. Stanford researchers find that employment of 22 to 25 year olds in the most AI-exposed United States occupations is about 19% below that of less-exposed peers, mainly through less hiring, a pattern they call descriptive, not causal [S26, S225]. In Australia the evidence is mixed. The Department of Employment and Workplace Relations (DEWR) finds that the most-exposed fifth of Australian occupations grew 5.6% from November 2022 to February 2026, against 9.5% for the least exposed, a gap that may partly reflect earlier trends [S2]. It also finds employment of 20 to 24 year olds growing slightly faster than for older workers, and the youth unemployment gap below its pre-COVID range [S2, S27]. SEEK data link AI with more early-career demand, not less [S31]. In September 2026, youth unemployment was 11.1% and overall unemployment 4.7% [S28].

This is why the paper does not ask Parliament to pay a universal income now. It asks Parliament to fix the adequacy of today's payments, which is a problem now, and to legislate the tests that would show whether AI has begun to displace work at scale.

### Productivity and pay: mixed evidence

Evidence on whether technology's gains reach wages is mixed, and the paper does not rest its case on it. The Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) found that market-sector labour productivity rose about 65% from 1994-95 to 2021-22 while real hourly compensation rose about 47% [S33], though output per hour fell in two of the three years to 2024-25 [S34]. The PC finds the long-run gap concentrated in mining and agriculture [S35], and the Reserve Bank of Australia (RBA) finds labour's share of income drifting down since the 1970s [S36] (Figure 2.2).

**Figure 2.2 [CHART]. Market-sector labour productivity and real hourly compensation, Australia, 1994-95 to 2024-25 (index, 1994-95 = 100)**

![Figure 2.2: labour productivity and real hourly compensation](charts/fig2_2_productivity_compensation.png)

*Source: Australian Bureau of Statistics, 2022, for both series to 2021-22 [S33]; productivity extended to 2024-25 using ABS market-sector gross value added per hour worked [S34]. Data: charts/data/fig2_2_productivity_compensation.csv [S5]. Note: compensation is deflated by the Consumer Price Index; no matching compensation series is published after 2021-22.*

### The safety net already leaves millions in poverty

The Australian Council of Social Service (ACOSS) and the University of New South Wales (UNSW) found that 3.7 million people (14.2%) lived below the poverty line in 2022-23, up from 12.4% in 2020-21 [S37]. They included 757,000 children, or 15.6% of children under 15, the rate to which the household model in Section 6 is calibrated. Their headline line is 50% of median equivalised disposable household income, A$584 a week for a single adult in 2022-23, with poverty measured after housing costs; it is re-set each year, so it is a relative line [S38]. Poverty is highest among people relying on working-age payments: 76% for JobSeeker, 73% for Youth Allowance and 71% for Parenting Payment [S39]. The Melbourne Institute's poverty line for a single adult not in the workforce was A$569.81 a week in the March quarter of 2026 [S4], against a JobSeeker single rate of A$412.45 [S40]. In June 2024, 42% of Rent Assistance income units were in rental stress after that assistance [S41].

Public support for a response is conditional. The Lowy Institute found that 64% of Australians think AI's risks outweigh its benefits [S42]. An Australian National University (ANU) poll found 55% favoured a basic income, but only 38% backed the same payment for everyone regardless of work [S43]. No later poll was found, and the paper treats that division as a reason for a vote on each stage.

| Melbourne Institute poverty lines (2026) [S4] | Single adult not in the workforce: A$569.81 a week, March quarter 2026 | Statistical index | **Moderate.** Updated by income growth from a 1973 benchmark, not a measured headcount |
| Australian Institute of Health and Welfare (AIHW) housing assistance data (2025) [S41] | 42% of Rent Assistance income units (538,000) in rental stress after that assistance, June 2024 | Official administrative data | **Strong** |
| Deloitte Access Economics for Carers Australia (2020) [S55] | Carers provide about 2.2 billion hours of care a year, worth A$77.9bn | Consultancy modelling for an advocacy body | **Moderate.** Replacement-cost valuation; sensitive to the assumed hours and wage |
| Robodebt Royal Commission figures, read out at the report's release (2023) [S56] | About A$1.76bn in alleged debts against about 526,000 people; about A$971m to run and unwind | Official inquiry, reported at a ministerial press conference | **Strong** for scale; figures taken from the press conference, not the report itself |
| Spies-Butcher, Phillips and Henderson (2021) [S57] | A lower-rate basic income would also need about 12 more points on every income-tax rate | Modelling published as commentary | **Moderate.** Not peer reviewed; a different design and year |
| Future Campus (2026) [S58] | No official AI-adjusted employment projections | Commentary (secondary) | **Weak.** Used only to note a data gap |

*Source: as cited in each row.*

### The policy problem

The policy problem is that Australia's income support ties security to paid work and means tests while AI begins to change who gets hired, with no automatic way to share AI's gains if paid work becomes scarcer. The evidence has limits: the latest poverty headcount is for 2022-23, because the ABS did not release its 2023-24 income survey [S45]. No official series has compared productivity with real pay since 2021-22, and Australia has no official AI-adjusted employment projections [S58].

### Objectives

The proposal has seven numbered objectives, each with a measurable indicator, a baseline and a target date (Table 2.2). The poverty targets were set on the 22 October design's first-round rates with Stage 1 continuing, rounded up to the next half point; P\* is modelled below them (10.5% for all persons and 6.6% for children in 2028-29; Table 5.7), so the recommended design meets them with more room. Objective 1(a)'s 12.0% by 2030-31 and 12.5% by 2034-35 uses the pensioner-income variant nearest Department of Social Services (DSS) data (Table 6.23). Higher rents and interest rates could put them out of reach: in the central housing scenario child poverty is 8.1%, above the 7.0% target (Table 6.32). Objective 2's targets are what the model supports, though it fits that group poorly, and objectives 3(a), 6(b), 7(a) and 7(b) apply only if Parliament later legislates a dividend (Section 10).

**Table 2.2. Objectives of the proposal, with indicators, baselines (2022-23 to 2026) and targets (2029 to 2040) (per cent unless stated)**

| No. | Objective | Indicator | Baseline (year) | Target and date | Data source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Reduce poverty | (a) Poverty rate after housing costs, all persons, on the relative line ACOSS–UNSW publish (50% of each year's median equivalised household disposable income); (b) the same for children under 15; (c) the same for people in sole-parent households | (a) 14.2% [S37]; (b) 15.6% (2022-23, the latest available, and the household model's baseline; no 2023-24 official survey was released) [S38, S45]; (c) 34% (2019-20, the latest by household type) [S59] | (a) 12.0% or lower by 2030-31 and 12.5% by 2034-35 (set from the pensioner-income variant nearest DSS data, Table 6.23); (b) 7.0% or lower by 2030-31 and 8.0% by 2034-35; (c) 19.0% or lower by 2030-31 and 20.0% by 2034-35 | ACOSS–UNSW Poverty and Inequality Partnership; ABS income surveys |
| 2 | Lift people out of poverty on working-age payments | Poverty rate in JobSeeker-reliant households, relative line | 76% (2022-23) [S39] | 55.5% or lower by 2030-31 and 53% by 2034-35 (indicative, because the household model fits this group poorly) | As above |
| 3 | Give every adult a secure income floor | (a) If the dividend is legislated, share of eligible adults paid it; (b) share of working-age payments whose maximum rate is at least the floor; (c) share of recipients with a payment suspended or cancelled under the activity test in a quarter | (a) and (b) 0% (2026); (c) 38% suspended and 4.3% cancelled, Workforce Australia, January to March 2024 [S60] | (a) 98.5% or more from 2029-30, with 80% of non-lodgers enrolled; (b) 100% from 1 July 2028; (c) no higher than the 2024 rates (38% suspended, 4.3% cancelled) in any quarter from 2028-29, so the higher floor brings no rise in suspensions | Services Australia; Australian Taxation Office (ATO); Department of Employment and Workplace Relations |
| 4 | Pay no current recipient less, and count who is worse off after housing costs | (a) Share of protected recipients, and of families now receiving Family Tax Benefit, paid less than under current rules (the cash guarantee); (b) recipient households with lower income after housing costs than under current rules | Not applicable | (a) 0% in every year from 2028-29; (b) reported each year by payment and tenure (modelled: 16% of recipient households in 2028-29, Table 6.21), with Rent Assistance reviewed if it rises | Department of Social Services (DSS); AIHW; national audit |
| 5 | Keep Australians in work | (a) Employment-to-population ratio; (b) participation effect in the embedded trial; (c) share of earning parents with an effective marginal tax rate above 50% | (a) 63.6% (September 2026) [S28]; (b) not applicable; (c) 14% (household model, current rules, 2028-29) | (a) Within 1 point of Treasury's projection each year; (b) no larger than a 3-point fall, the binding limit in Table 9.6, reported by December 2031; (c) no more than 19%, the modelled level | ABS Labour Force; embedded trial; ATO and Services Australia records |
| 6 | Pay for it responsibly | (a) The Basic Income Levy, as a share of market income; (b) if legislated, the fund's capital; (c) cash balance, net debt and net financial worth against the Budget baseline | 2026-27 Budget baseline [S61] | (a) On central yields, nil before 2043-44 and no more than A$16.6bn (0.62% of market income) a year to 2045-46 (Table 6.6), with the joint stress test's range reported each Budget (Table 6.9); (b) only if a fund is later legislated: not spent on the dividend; (c) net financial worth not below baseline once the 2028-29 borrowing is repaid in 2031-32; the PBO certifies the five taxes against Stage 1's costs each year | PBO certificate |
| 7 | Share AI gains and support contribution | (a) If legislated, the dividend paid and published each year, with any fund's balance and the taxes that fund it; (b) rise in the dividend when the excess profits tax grows faster than the Budget baseline; (c) formal volunteering rate | (a) and (b) nil (2026); (c) 23% (2025) [S62] | (a) and (b) only if a dividend is later legislated: (a) every year from its first; (b) from 2033-34, once extra receipts have repaid the start-up advance, higher than on the Budget path in every year the excess profits tax has exceeded the Budget baseline (Table 10.17); (c) 26% by 2032 and 30% by 2040 | PBO; Treasury; ABS General Social Survey |

*Source: author's proposal; baselines as cited. Note: the poverty targets are the household model's first-round projections on the relative line with Stage 1 continuing (the official baseline plus the model's projected change, rounded up to the next half point; supplementary household_output_partf4.md, Table P2). The projected official rate with Stage 1 is 11.1% in 2030-31. The targets were set on Part 1's run; P\*'s modelled rates are lower (Table 5.7). Objective 1(b) is set on the central calibration; on the variant nearest DSS data child poverty is 7.7% in 2028-29 (Table 6.23). The targets loosen by 2034-35 because poverty with no reform rises in the model over those years (14.3% to 14.7% for all persons, 16.1% to 17.0% for children; household Table P2). In the paper's housing scenario rent and interest-rate effects raise Stage 1's rates by 0.3 to 0.7 points, and in the central case child poverty is 8.1%, above the 7.0% target (Table 6.32). Objective 3(b) measures the maximum rate: the floor keeps JobSeeker's income test and activity test, so part-rate recipients are paid less by design and a suspended payment is not paid at all. If a later Act legislates a universal payment, Parliament sets new targets then.*

### Why government must act

Markets will not provide this security on their own:

1. Missing insurance. No private market insures a worker against the risk that a technology makes their skills less valuable across a whole economy, because the risk falls on many people at once and cannot be pooled.
2. Concentrated gains. The IMF warns that "In most scenarios, AI will likely worsen overall inequality" [S23], because it lifts returns to capital. If gains concentrate, only government can share them at scale, through tax.
3. Unpaid contribution. Carers provide about 2.2 billion hours of care a year, worth A$77.9bn [S55], and markets do not pay for this work.
4. Failures of the current system. Means tests and mutual obligation create errors and harm; Robodebt raised about A$1.76bn in alleged debts against about 526,000 people [S56]. Stage 1 removes estimate debts from most family payments but keeps the activity test at the floor, so this failure is reduced, not removed.
5. Coordination and timing. Payment systems take years to build, so capacity has to exist before displacement shows up in headline unemployment.

### How the paper answers the questions a committee will ask

Each of the seven questions the Office of Impact Analysis sets for any policy proposal [S63] is answered in a named section (Table 2.3). The paper asks how Australia should prepare a basic income for the AI transition, through four questions of its own:

- (a) Is there a problem that government must address? (Section 2)
- (b) What would success look like, and what are the options? (Sections 3, 4 and 5)
- (c) Can a basic income be funded, and who would gain and lose? (Section 6)
- (d) How would it be implemented, consulted on, monitored and evaluated? (Sections 7, 8 and 9)

## 3. A picture of 2045

This chapter describes what a basic income could make possible by 2045. It is a picture of the pathway, not a forecast: it happens only if the pathway is triggered and every test is passed. AI must displace enough work to meet the need test, the Australian trial must find a fall in participation under 3.0 points, the PBO must certify the funding, and Parliament must then pass a new Act setting a rate and keep it. A full pension-rate basic income also needs AI-driven growth far above the Budget's baseline, and on the paper's figures it cannot be funded from income tax (Section 6). On the central path none of this happens, and Australia in 2045 has the recommended Stage 1: the floor at the poverty line, the child payment and the higher Rent Assistance. The pathway is insurance, and like most insurance it may never be used.

The seven people below are fictional and live on one path, chosen to show the pathway working. A severe AI shock from 2032-33 meets the need test early in 2035, and the floor holds its value until then. The trial has found a fall in participation under 3 points, the PBO certifies, and Parliament legislates a payment at the JobSeeker rate from July 2035. On the 22 October paper's run that needed a rise of every income-tax rate of 25.5 points with a dividend fund to lend, or 26.9 points without one, and there is no Stage 3 by 2045-46 (Tables 6.38 and 6.52). Duc and Tom are single, so the floor reaches them; a displaced worker whose partner earns above its cut-out would get nothing until the universal payment starts.

### Seven Australians in 2045

Four of the seven gain clearly from the universal payment, though one of them found the transition hard; two gain little, and one pays much more tax.

Leanne, 52, cares for her mother, who has dementia, for most of each day in Toowoomba. Her basic income arrives every fortnight whatever her hours, but Carer Payment is above the universal rate, so most of her income still comes as a Carer Payment top-up under the same rules. In 2022, 3.0 million Australians (11.9%) were carers and 1.2 million primary carers [S64]; two-thirds of primary carers were women and 30% cared for 40 or more hours a week [S65].

Duc, 39, lost his junior software-testing job in Parramatta in 2033 when AI tools took over routine checks, the kind of loss JSA's illustrative modelling shows for programming and clerical roles while nursing grows [S25]. The floor covered his rent and food while he retrained as a nurse and the basic income replaced it in 2035. In the OpenResearch trial, recipients under 30 spent about an extra hour a week on education [S66].

Mere, 38, is a ceramic artist in Hobart. Her basic income lets her spend her best hours in the studio rather than in casual shifts. Ireland's randomised Basic Income for the Arts pilot found recipients spent about 11 more hours a week on creative work and were 15 percentage points less likely to feel depressed [S67, S68].

Graham, 71, a retired fitter in Port Augusta, lost his wife in 2041 and spends three mornings a week at the Men's Shed. His Age Pension is above the universal rate, so the basic income changes little for him.

Anna, 50, is an engineer in Brisbane who pays much more tax than before. Before the new Act she paid a small levy, and from July 2035 every income-tax rate rose by about 25 to 27 points (Tables 6.38 and 6.52). In return her two adult children study without needing her support, and she has a floor if AI tools take over her design work.

Tom, 58, lost his payroll job in Wollongong in 2034 when his employer automated the work, an occupation JSA's modelling places among those losing most from AI [S25]. His payment, topped up to the poverty-line floor, kept him housed but did not cover his rent comfortably [S41], and the universal payment began only in July 2035. Retraining at 47 was slow, he pieced together casual work for three years, and the transition was hard.

Kirra, 19, is a first-year electrical apprentice in Dubbo. Her basic income tops up her apprentice wage, so she does not need a second job.

### The aggregate picture

Today's data show where the gains could come from. In 2024, people who did unpaid work averaged 4 hours 25 minutes a day on it [S69], and 15% of Australians aged 15 and over were lonely [S70]. Formal volunteering fell from 36% of adults in 2010 to 25% in 2020 [S71] and 23% in 2025 on a slightly different series, and trust in others from 61.4% in 2020 to 49.9% [S62]. Figure 3.1 shows the longer volunteering series.

**Figure 3.1 [CHART]. Formal volunteering rate, adults aged 18 and over, Australia, 2006 to 2020 (per cent)**

![Figure 3.1: formal volunteering rate](charts/fig3_1_volunteering.png)

*Source: Australian Institute of Health and Welfare, 2025, using ABS data [S71]. Data: charts/data/fig3_1_volunteering.csv [S5]. Note: the 2025 ABS survey (23%, people aged 15 and over) uses a different age base, so it is not added to this series [S62].*

Mental ill-health is common and costly: in 2020–22, 21.5% of Australians aged 16 to 85 had a mental disorder in the previous year, and 38.8% of those aged 16 to 24 [S72]. The PC put its cost, with suicide, at A$200–220bn a year in 2018-19 [S73]. Trials suggest an income floor moves these numbers the right way, though modestly and not in every trial; Table 3.1 sets aims for Parliament to adopt, not forecasts.

**Table 3.1. Baselines (2018-19 to 2025) and proposed 2045 aims for contribution and wellbeing, Australia (per cent unless stated)**

| Measure | Latest value (year) | Proposed 2045 aim | What the trials suggest |
|---|---|---|---|
| Formal volunteering, adults | 23% (2025) [S62] | 30% | German pilot: more donations; differences in volunteering small and not significant [S74] |
| Weekly face-to-face contact with family or friends | 53% (2025) [S62] | 68% (the 2019 level) | German pilot: more time with friends, family and partner [S74] |
| Lonely, aged 15 and over | 15% (2024) [S70] | 10% | Finland: less loneliness reported, but not proven to be caused by the payment [S75] |
| Mental disorder in the past 12 months, aged 16–85 | 21.5% (2020–22) [S72] | 18% | Germany: mental health up 0.30 standard deviations; OpenResearch: gains faded by year 3 [S74, S76] |
| Cost of mental ill-health and suicide | A$200–220bn a year (2018-19) [S73] | Falling as a share of gross domestic product (GDP) | Canada's Mincome: hospital admissions 8.5% lower [S77] |
| Took part in a cultural activity, adults | 32% (2021-22) [S78] | 40% | Ireland: about 11 more hours a week on creative work [S67] |
| In study or training, aged 15–74 | 34.3% (2024-25) [S79] | 40% | OpenResearch: under-30s spent about 1 more hour a week on education [S66] |
| Value of unpaid informal care | A$77.9bn a year (2020) [S55] | Every carer has an income floor | Reductions in paid work concentrate among carers, who shift time to care [S80] |
| Life satisfaction (0 to 10) | Australian baseline not found | Rising | Germany: 7.6 for recipients against 7.1 for the control group [S74] |

*Source: as cited in each row; aims are the author's proposals. Note: aims are not model forecasts. Baselines are official statistics; the strength of each trial is rated in Table 4.1.*

### What contributing to society looks like

In this picture contribution is broader than paid work and includes caring, volunteering, learning, making things and starting enterprises. The German pilot found recipients gave more to charity (€28 a month against €12) and more support to family and friends (€126 against €50) [S74]. In Kenya the long-term basic income raised the number of household enterprises by about 14% [S81].

A universal payment would stay unconditional. Two voluntary measures, which the paper proposes now, support objective 7 (Table 2.2). A National Contribution Register in myGov would let people record care, volunteering, learning and creative work as evidence of skills, never to test eligibility or raise debts. A Community Contribution Fund would match money raised by local groups, dollar for dollar, up to A$50,000 a project, in a pilot from 2029-30 to 2031-32 capped at A$100 million a year (Table 6.2).

## 4. What the evidence says

Basic income trials consistently show small effects on work, short-run gains in financial security and mixed effects on health, but no rich country has tested a permanent, universal, full-rate basic income. The evidence supports a modest first stage and careful testing; it does not show that a full-rate payment would work at national scale.

### How to read the evidence

Each study is rated by method, sample size and relevance to Australia. A randomised controlled trial (RCT), which compares people randomly given a payment with similar people who were not, gives the strongest evidence of cause and effect. Quasi-experimental studies use a natural comparison, and observational studies show patterns without proving cause. Table 6.8 rates the fiscal and financing evidence on the same scale.

### International trials

The high-income trials paid selected groups for set periods, and recipients knew the payments would end. Table 4.1 summarises 17 trials and programs.

**Table 4.1. Basic income and guaranteed income trials, 1974 to 2026: design, findings and strength of evidence (sample size in persons; payments in local currency per person unless stated)**

| Trial (place, years) | Design | n | Payment | Duration | Method | Key findings | Null or negative findings | Strength of evidence | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| OpenResearch (Texas and Illinois, 2020–23) | Adults aged 21–40 on low incomes | 3,000 (1,000 paid) | US$1,000 a month | 3 years | Preregistered RCT | Spending up at least US$300 a month; better financial health; more interest in starting a business; under-30s spent more time on education | Participation down 3.9 points; hours down 1.3 a week; non-transfer income down about US$1,900 a year; wellbeing gains faded; no physical health effect | **Strong** (large RCT; targeted and temporary) | [S66, S76, S82, S83] |
| Finland (2017–18) | Unemployed people on national benefit; compulsory | 2,000 paid | €560 a month, tax-free | 2 years | National RCT | Employment up 6 days in year 2; better life satisfaction; less mental strain and loneliness | No employment effect in year 1; 23% survey response; no baseline survey | **Moderate** (employment); **weak** (wellbeing) | [S75] |
| Stockton Economic Empowerment Demonstration (California, 2019–21) | Residents of lower-income areas | 131 paid, 200 control | US$500 a month | 2 years | Mixed-methods RCT | Lower income volatility (19% against 26%) and less distress in year 1 | Employment gain not statistically significant; gains faded in the pandemic | **Weak** (small; high attrition) | [S84] |
| Cambridge Recurring Income for Success and Empowerment (Massachusetts, from 2021) | Low-income single carers of children | 130 paid, 156 control | US$500 a month | 18 months | RCT | More full-time work; higher income; better food security | Some effects ambiguous; effect sizes not reported | **Weak** (small; sizes not reported) | [S85] |
| Denver Basic Income Project (Colorado, 2022–23) | People experiencing homelessness; three arms | 807 | US$1,000 a month, or US$6,500 then US$500; comparison US$50 | 12 months | Mixed-methods RCT | Living in own home rose from 6% to 44–48% | No significant difference from the US$50 group on housing; no change in stress | **Weak** (cash effect not isolated) | [S86] |
| Baltimore (Maryland, from 2022) | Parents aged 18–24 | 153 paid, 156 control | US$1,000 a month | 24 months (12-month results) | RCT | Household income US$23,608 against US$16,233; none unhoused against 5% | No significant effect on participation, distress or physical health | **Weak** (small; early) | [S87] |

| Mincome (Manitoba, Canada, 1974–79) | Guaranteed annual income; one town open to all residents | Not found | Not found; 50% taper | About 5 years | Quasi-experimental (health); RCT arms (work) | Hospital admissions 8.5% lower; more teenagers finished Year 12 | Annual hours fell about 1% for husbands, 3% for wives and 5% for unmarried women; not significant once time effects are included | **Moderate** (old; partial data) | [S77, S90] |
| Ontario (Canada, 2017–19) | Low-income residents aged 18–64 in four areas | About 4,000 enrolled; survey of 209 | Up to C$16,989 a year (single); 50% taper | Cancelled in 2018; payments stopped March 2019 | Observational survey | 76.1% of those employed kept working; over 80% reported better mental health | Self-selected; 32 moved into unemployment against 10 the other way | **Weak** (no causal claim possible) | [S91, S92, S93] |

| Iran (from 2010–11) | Near-universal cash replacing energy subsidies | Over 70 million | About 28% of median income per person | Ongoing | Quasi-experimental | No fall in hours or participation; more work by women and self-employed men | Inflation and sanctions later cut purchasing power (not attributed to the transfers) | **Moderate** | [S100] |
| Wales, care leavers (2022–25) | Young people leaving care at 18 | 644 | £1,600 a month before tax | 24 months | Theory-based evaluation | Many reported better mental health; some funded courses | Mixed employment effects; some debt; harmful income drop at the end | **Weak** so far (impact data due 2027) | [S101] |
| Ireland, Basic Income for the Arts (2022–26) | Artists, randomly selected from applicants | 2,000 paid, 1,000 control | €325 a week | About 3.5 years | RCT | About 11 more hours a week on creative work; 15 points less likely to feel depressed; deprivation down 18.5 points at 6 months | 3 fewer hours a week in other jobs (6 months) | **Moderate** (RCT; some figures from secondary reports) | [S67, S68, S102] |
| India, Madhya Pradesh (about 2011–13) | Whole villages paid, run with the Self-Employed Women's Association and the United Nations Children's Fund | Over 6,000 paid | 200 rupees per adult a month, rising to 300 | 12–17 months | Village-level RCT | School enrolment 12% higher; twice as likely to increase productive work | Findings described as preliminary | **Moderate to weak**; low relevance | [S103] |

*Source: as listed in each row. Note: RCT = randomised controlled trial. Strength combines method, sample size and relevance to Australia: **strong** = large RCT or robust quasi-experiment; **moderate** = smaller RCT or quasi-experiment; **weak** = small, observational or method unclear.*

Reviews and large studies reach mixed conclusions: work effects are mostly small, but targeted programs cut poverty more per dollar. A 2020 review of 27 studies in *Lancet Public Health* found mixed health effects, small employment effects for men and larger ones for women with young children [S104]. A Stanford umbrella review of 16 reviews found small participation effects and better mental health, and cautioned that a fully universal program could produce quite different results [S80]. A World Bank study of 10 countries found existing programs on average about 60% more effective in reducing poverty than a budget-neutral basic income [S105], and the OECD found that a budget-neutral basic income would raise poverty [S228]. Table 4.2 rates these studies and the Australian evidence on the Coronavirus Supplement.

**Table 4.2. Reviews, Coronavirus Supplement studies, and evidence on spending and prices, with type and strength of evidence (published 2009 to 2021)**

| Evidence (year) | Main finding | Type of evidence | Strength and main caveat |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gibson, Hearty and Craig, *Lancet Public Health* (2020) [S104] | Mixed health effects across 27 studies; small employment effects for men, larger for women with young children | Scoping review | **Moderate.** No study tested a full basic income |
| Hasdell, Stanford Basic Income Lab (2020) [S80] | Small participation effects and better mental health across 16 reviews; a universal program could differ | Umbrella review of reviews | **Moderate.** Reviews of partial and targeted schemes |
| Gentilini and others, World Bank (2020) [S105] | Existing programs about 60% more effective in poverty reduction than a budget-neutral basic income, across 10 countries | Microsimulation | **Moderate.** Mostly lower-income countries; budget-neutral design unlike the ABI |
| Egger and others, National Bureau of Economic Research (NBER) (2019) [S106] | Local multiplier of 2.4; minimal price inflation after large one-off transfers in Kenya | Cluster RCT with general-equilibrium measurement | **Strong** design; **low** relevance to Australia (rural, one-off, low-income economy) |
| Cunha, De Giorgi and Jayachandran, NBER (2011) [S107] | Cash transfers raised local prices relative to in-kind transfers in Mexico | Quasi-experimental | **Moderate.** Remote villages with thin markets |
| Grudnoff, The Australia Institute (2021) [S108] | The Coronavirus Supplement lifted about 470,000 people out of poverty; about 900,000 fell back as it was cut | Microsimulation by an advocacy think tank | **Moderate.** Not peer reviewed |
| Phillips, Gray and Biddle, ANU (2020) [S109] | Poverty and housing stress fell below pre-pandemic levels during the supplement | Microsimulation | **Moderate.** Scenario modelling, not measured outcomes |
| ACOSS survey of recipients (2020) [S110] | 80.8% said they were eating better or more regularly | Non-random survey | **Weak.** Self-selected respondents |
| Australian Journal of Social Issues study, summarised by Scimex (2021) [S111] | The supplement helped 92 recipients meet basic needs and look for work | Peer-reviewed qualitative study (secondary summary) | **Weak to moderate.** Small, not causal |
| Berger-Thomson, Chung and McKibbin, Reserve Bank of Australia (2009) [S112] | Low-income households spent about 0.9 of a one-off payment on non-durables, against 0.3 to 0.7 overall | Observational (household survey panel) | **Moderate.** Australian, but the difference by income was not statistically significant |

*Source: as cited in each row. Note: strength uses the scale in Table 4.1.*

### Contrary evidence and what it means for the design

The strongest evidence against a basic income concerns work effort, cost and poverty reduction per dollar. The design responds by targeting Stage 1, lifting the lowest payments first and testing before any universal step. Table 4.3 sets out each contrary finding.

OpenResearch's preregistered RCT followed 3,000 low-income United States adults aged 21 to 40 for three years, 1,000 of whom received US$1,000 a month. Recipients cut their labour-force participation by 3.9 percentage points in the trial's latest revision, and their income excluding transfers fell by about 16 cents for each dollar received [S82]; they worked 1.3 fewer hours a week [S83]. In Australia, the e61 Institute found that the Coronavirus Supplement cut recipients' chance of finding a job by 19% and raised job separations by 64% [S113]. The Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) found that a budget-neutral basic income in the United Kingdom would push about 7% of people into poverty and lift only 2% out, and "would not be a cost-effective way of directly reducing income poverty" [S114].

**Table 4.3. Contrary evidence (studies published 1985 to 2026) and how the design responds**

| Contrary finding | Method and strength | What it means | How the design responds |
|---|---|---|---|
| OpenResearch: participation down 3.9 points, hours down 1.3 a week, non-transfer income down about US$1,900 a year [S82, S83] | RCT; strong | Work falls, modestly but really | Costing assumes market income falls 16 cents per dollar of gain; Stage 1 is targeted and keeps the activity test; no universal payment without an Australian trial, and none if participation falls more than 3 points; at the trial's latest 3.9-point estimate that cap binds with probability 0.79 to 0.84, so a universal payment is not the expected outcome (Section 5) |
| OpenResearch: stress and food security gains faded by year 3; no physical health effect [S76] | RCT; strong | Wellbeing gains may not last | No wellbeing gains counted in the costing; evaluation tracks them |
| e61: Coronavirus Supplement cut job-finding by 19% and raised job separations by 64% [S113] | Quasi-experimental; moderate | Higher means-tested payments slowed return to work in Australia | The floor's caseload response, including people who leave work and return to payment, is costed; a universal payment needs the need test, the trial and a later Act |
| OECD: a budget-neutral basic income in the United Kingdom would push 7% of people into poverty and lift 2% out [S114] | Microsimulation; strong for its question | A cheap basic income that replaces targeted payments hurts the poor | Stage 1 is targeted and not budget-neutral; working-age payments lifted to the floor; no payment cut under today's rules [S228] |
| World Bank: existing programs about 60% more effective in poverty reduction than a budget-neutral basic income, measured by the squared poverty gap across 10 countries [S105] | Microsimulation, mostly lower-income countries; moderate relevance | Universality costs more per unit of poverty reduction | Accepted: this is why Stage 1 is targeted. At the same budget P\* leaves poverty at 10.5% and the targeted package at 9.0%, against 11.3% for Part 1, the design with a universal-leaning supplement (Table 5.7) |
| 1970s United States negative income tax experiments: on average husbands cut work by the equivalent of about two weeks of full-time work, and wives and single mothers by about three weeks [S115] | Randomised trials; old; peer-reviewed synthesis | Larger responses are possible at full rate | High-response case tested; Stage 3 described, not authorised |
| Los Angeles and Baltimore: no gains, or slightly higher distress [S87, S89] | Randomised trials; moderate to weak | Cash alone may not fix deep disadvantage | Rent Assistance, disability and carer supports retained |
| Denver: a US$50 group improved as much on housing [S86] | RCT; weak | Housing depends on services and supply | Rent Assistance +75% in Stage 1; rent capture tested (Table 6.31); the rent safeguard raises Rent Assistance further |
| Alaska: substance-related incidents rise after payment [S95] | Quasi-experimental; moderate | Large lump sums can cause short-term harm | No lump sums: Stage 1 is paid each fortnight through existing payments, and so would any later universal payment |
| Wales: debt and a harmful income drop when payments ended [S101] | Theory-based; weak | Temporary payments create shocks | Stage 1 is permanent; it is not a temporary payment |
| Mexico: cash raised local prices compared with in-kind support [S107] | Quasi-experimental; moderate | Inflation risk where markets are thin | Tax-funded; the Reserve Bank keeps control of interest rates; interest-rate and rent scenario (Section 6); rent safeguard (Table 9.6) |
| Foster (University of New South Wales, 2016): a A$20,000 basic income would cost about A$380bn a year [S116] | Costing; moderate | Cost is the binding constraint | No universal rate legislated; a PBO certificate before any; Stage 3 described, not authorised, because no rate passes its certificate |

*Source: as cited in each row; design responses are the author's.*

### Australian precedents

The Coronavirus Supplement and Robodebt both shape the design. The Coronavirus Supplement added A$550 a fortnight to JobSeeker and related payments from 27 April to 24 September 2020, then A$250 to 31 December 2020 [S117] and A$150 until it ended on 31 March 2021 [S118]. It lifted about 470,000 Australians, including 75,000 children, out of poverty, and about 900,000 more fell into poverty as it was cut back during 2020 [S108]. ANU researchers found poverty and housing stress fell below pre-pandemic levels [S109], and a qualitative study of 92 recipients found the supplement helped people meet basic needs and look for work [S111].

Robodebt used averaged tax data to raise debts against welfare recipients until November 2019 [S119]. The Royal Commission, reporting on 7 July 2023 [S120], found the scheme "a crude and cruel mechanism, neither fair nor legal", as the Prime Minister quoted that day [S121]. The child payment is set by last year's income, though on Part 1's run about 49,500 families a year still owe a debt after an income-fall review (Table 5.4); P\*'s figure is essentially the same. Because the floor and the child payment are still income-tested, the Act requires a human decision before any debt is raised from matched data.

### What the evidence does not tell us

The trial evidence is on partial, temporary or targeted payments, which leaves gaps on permanence, economy-wide effects and the taxes that pay for a full scheme. People may behave differently when they know a payment will last for life [S122], effects on rents, wages and prices are largely unmeasured outside Kenya and Alaska [S80, S106], and no trial has measured the effect of the taxes. No trial has tested what decides the question: paying everyone in a market, raising the taxes to fund it, and keeping it going for good. Where universal cash has been paid for years from volatile revenue it has been cut or eroded: Alaska halved its dividend in 2016, and its 2025 dividend was its smallest in real terms [S99, S229].

## 5. The proposal

Stage 1 is a targeted floor, recommended because the paper's own comparison favours it. The universal stages are a pathway whose tests are legislated now and whose rates are not. No dividend is recommended.

### Stage 1: a targeted floor (P\*)

P\* has three parts, all from 1 July 2028 (Table 5.1). It lifts working-age payments to a floor at the poverty line. It replaces Family Tax Benefit with a child payment that has no cliffs and no supplement for middle-income families. And it raises the maximum rates of Commonwealth Rent Assistance by 75%. It costs the same as Part 1 in 2028-29 on the fiscal model's basis, A$62.0bn, because the money Part 1 spent on the middle-income supplement pays for the Rent Assistance rise and a slightly smaller supplement. Its full fiscal path is in Section 6.

The variant the 22 October paper reported (A$6,973 a child, with automatic enrolment) was solved against the household model's own costing and cost A$6.2bn more than Part 1 in 2028-29 on the fiscal model's basis. This revision re-costs P\* at Part 1's budget, which sets the child supplement at A$6,750. Re-costed, P\* still has less poverty than Part 1 in every group, on three random seeds and three calibrations of the household model (Table 5.7). Its figures are provisional until the model's last robustness runs are checked.

**Table 5.1. P\*: the three parts of the recommended Stage 1, 2028-29 (A$; A$ billion; per cent)**

| Part | Design | Who it reaches | Cost, 2028-29 (A$ billion) |
|---|---|---|---:|
| Payment floor | JobSeeker, Youth Allowance, Austudy, the ABSTUDY living allowance, Parenting Payment and Special Benefit rise to A$569.81 a week for a single person (A$32,570 a year), couple rates in proportion; income tests, the partner income test and the activity test kept; indexed to wages | 0.94 million JobSeeker-type recipients, 0.18 million students and 0.39 million Parenting Payment recipients gain; about 150,000 more people claim | 19.9, with the caseload response |
| Child payment | A$6,750 a child plus the family's maximum FTB, withdrawn at 18 cents a dollar of parents' income above A$72,631; assessed on the latest assessed income; current FTB kept where higher | 82.3% of families with children gain | 37.3 |
| Rent Assistance +75% | Maximum rates of Commonwealth Rent Assistance raised 75% | With the other two parts, 77.4% of private-renter households gain | 4.7 |
| **All three, with implementation** | | **62.1% of households gain before the five taxes; 35.4% are better off after them** | **62.0** |

*Source: author's household model and fiscal model [S5]; Tables 5.7, 5.10, 6.1, 6.14 and 6.20. Note: recipient counts are Part 1's (Table 6.20); P\*'s floor is the same. The floor's cost includes its caseload response (Table 5.10). The child payment is net of the FTB it replaces. The child supplement is A$6,750 rather than Part 1's A$7,452 so that the designs cost the same. The three parts and implementation sum to the total within rounding; the floor and implementation are the remainder in Table 6.1. Provisional.*

### Who is eligible

The floor, the child payment and Rent Assistance follow the current payments' residence rules. The child payment keeps Family Tax Benefit's one-year waiting period for new residents [S125], about 56,000 children. Temporary visa holders, including New Zealand citizens on Special Category Visas, are not eligible. Any later universal payment would follow the newly arrived resident's waiting period that applies to JobSeeker Payment, four years for visas granted on or after 1 January 2019 [S123]; about 0.52 million adults are in that period [S124].

### Rates and indexation

Stage 1 starts on 1 July 2028 (Table 5.2). JobSeeker, Youth Allowance, Austudy, the ABSTUDY (Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander study assistance) living allowance, Parenting Payment and Special Benefit rise to a floor of A$569.81 a week for a single person, the Melbourne Institute poverty line for March 2026 [S4]. The floor keeps JobSeeker's income tests, including the partner income test, and its activity test. It adds about 150,000 recipients and A$4.9bn in 2028-29: e61's job-finding response plus a quarter of its separations response, for people who leave work and return to payment [S113]. Student payments rise 73% for the 179,000 students on living allowances and Parenting Payment 13%, which at e61's job-finding elasticity adds about 32,000 recipients and A$1.0bn (Table 6.55). Those students' allowance is A$3.0bn of the Budget's A$6.9bn student assistance line in 2028-29; the rest is loans and other spending (Table 6.55, C4a).

**Table 5.2. Rates by stage: what the Act sets now and what it leaves to a later Act, 2028-29 to 2037-38 (A$ a year, nominal unless stated)**

| Stage | Earliest start | What the Act sets now | Universal adult payment | Payment floor for working-age payments, first year | Child supplement per child, first year | Indexation |
|---|---|---|---|---:|---|---|
| 1 Targeted floor (P\*) | 1 July 2028 | The floor, the child payment and Rent Assistance +75% | None | 32,570 (A$569.81 a week at the March quarter 2026) | 6,750 | Floor by wages (pension rules); child supplement by prices; Rent Assistance by prices, as now |
| 2 Basic income at the JobSeeker rate | Not before 1 July 2032, and only under a later Act | The tests only: need test, trial and work-effort cap, PBO certificate | Benchmark only: JobSeeker single basic rate, A$824.90 a fortnight [S40], A$21,447 in 2026 dollars and A$26,592 in 2032-33 | 37,375 in 2032-33, if kept as a top-up floor | Set by the later Act | Set by the later Act |
| 3 Full basic income at the Age Pension rate | Not authorised | Nothing; described only | Benchmark only: Age Pension single maximum basic rate, A$1,135.40 a fortnight [S126], A$29,520 in 2026 dollars and A$43,471 in 2037-38 | 44,390 in 2037-38, if kept as a top-up floor | – | – |

*Source: author's design on Department of Social Services rates and the Melbourne Institute poverty line [S3, S4, S5]. Note: the floor applies to JobSeeker, Youth Allowance (other and student), Austudy, the ABSTUDY living allowance, Parenting Payment (single and partnered) and Special Benefit; couple and other rates rise in proportion. Stage 2 and 3 figures are the benchmarks the 22 October paper costed (Section 6); no rate is legislated.*

The floor is indexed like the Age Pension, modelled as wage growth of 3.5% a year [S127], and the child supplement by prices.

### The child payment

The child payment supplies most of Stage 1's fall in child poverty, at the price of income-testing families' payments and weaker work incentives for some parents. Each child aged 0 to 17 attracts a supplement paid to their main carer, A$6,750 in 2028-29, indexed like the child supplement. It replaces FTB Parts A and B. Each family receives the supplement plus its maximum FTB, withdrawn at 18 cents a dollar of parents' income above A$72,631, until it is fully withdrawn. Families whose current FTB is higher keep it, and FTB's maintenance, shared-care and immunisation rules carry over.

P\* differs from Part 1 in one respect. Part 1 also paid the supplement alone to families above that range, withdrawn at 18 cents above A$144,584, a threshold near median parental income: the middle-income supplement. That second schedule bound mainly for one-child families. P\* drops it and spends the saving on the 75% Rent Assistance rise, with a slightly smaller supplement, which leaves less poverty in every group the model reports (Table 5.7).

The schedule is continuous, so A$50 more of parents' income never lowers family payments by more than A$9. Payments are assessed on the latest assessed taxable income, on average 1.5 years earlier [S129], so families whose circumstances do not change cannot owe a debt from a wrong estimate. After a separation the resident parent is re-assessed at once on their own income; after a birth or job loss a family may ask for a review on an estimate, reconciled only up to what it added. On Part 1's run about 49,500 families a year still owe a debt, averaging about A$1,000, and the review costs A$0.8bn in 2028-29, rising to A$1.2bn by 2045-46 (supplementary household_output_partf4.md, Table P11). P\*'s are essentially the same: about 49,500 families and A$0.75bn in 2028-29 (provisional).

Tables 5.3 to 5.5 show the child payment as run for Part 1, whose first schedule P\* keeps unchanged. They show why a universal supplement was rejected: it sends almost none of its cash to households in poverty and leaves poverty at 14.3% on the relative line (Table 5.3).

### What is changed and what is retained

At Stage 1 only Family Tax Benefit is replaced. Pensions, supplements and disability supports are unchanged, and Rent Assistance rises (Table 5.6).

| Tax-free threshold (A$18,200), Low Income Tax Offset (LITO, up to A$700) and the tax scale | Unchanged |
| Pension and energy supplements; Carer Supplement and Carer Allowance | Retained in full |
| Commonwealth Rent Assistance | Maximum rates raised 75%; for families, Rent Assistance and the Health Care Card are re-linked from FTB Part A to the child payment's FTB-equivalent amount, so families who first claim after 2028 keep access (Table 7.1) |
| Remote Area Allowance | Retained in full |
| National Disability Insurance Scheme (NDIS) | Retained in full |
| Paid Parental Leave and Child Care Subsidy | Retained in full |
| Veterans' payments | Unchanged |

### Who gains: three designs at the same budget

At the same budget, P\* leaves 10.5% of people in poverty after housing costs on the relative line, against 11.3% for Part 1 and 9.0% for the targeted package charged the same income effect (Table 5.7). It has less poverty than Part 1 in every group shown: children 6.6% against 7.2%, sole-parent households 14.8% against 16.4%, people aged 67 and over 11.6% against 12.1%, and households relying on the Disability Support Pension 52.6% against 68.2%. That holds on all three random seeds and all three calibrations of the household model. Part 1 leaves 0.20 million more people in poverty.

The targeted package does better still on poverty, with 0.40 million fewer people in poverty than P\* and less poverty in every group in every robustness run. It does so by adding an earned-income credit and a larger FTB Part A, which stack withdrawal rates on low- and middle-income working families, and by automatic enrolment.

Preferring P\* to the package is a value judgement, and the paper states its price on every method it uses. The package leaves about 0.40 million fewer people in poverty (1.5 points). On the paper's method it is A$2.0bn to A$4.3bn a year ahead of P\*; on the complete accounting it is between A$1.9bn a year behind and A$2.3bn ahead; with the Green Book's weights it is about A$10.7bn a year ahead. It breaks even at lower distributional weights: 1.3 to 2.4 on the paper's method and 2.0 to 3.8 on the complete accounting, against P\*'s 1.5 to 3.3 and 2.3 to 4.9 (Table 6.12, provisional). On average the package also treats working families better: second earners' mean participation tax rate is 28.9% against 33.0% under P\*, 22.4% of second earners face 40% or more against 31.6%, and 21.8% of higher earners in families with children face an effective marginal tax rate of 50% or more against 41.6% (Table 5.7). P\*'s work-incentive advantage is only at the extreme: 1.3% of higher earners face 60% or more under P\* against 14.2% under the package, and no second earner faces 60% or more against 6.8%. The paper's second reason is the fiscal path. The package's costs grow faster, so it needs A$108.1bn of levies to 2045-46 against A$36.5bn for P\*, and a levy before 2044-45 is more likely, 87% against 74% in the joint stress test (Tables 6.6 and 6.9; model results Table B5; provisional). The paper judges that avoiding those very high rates and the larger levy is worth the price. Parliament may reasonably judge otherwise, and should choose the package if it weights poverty above them.

A variant of P\* with automatic enrolment, re-costed to Part 1's budget, must cut the child supplement to A$5,734. It has lower overall poverty than P\* (10.3%) but higher child poverty (7.0% against 6.6%), and its levies to 2045-46 are larger (A$65.4bn against A$36.5bn). It is not recommended.

### Effective tax rates and second earners

P\* keeps Part 1's 18-cent taper, so its effective tax rates are close to Part 1's. Of higher earners in families with children, 41.6% face an effective marginal tax rate of 50% or more, most of them at exactly 50% (the taper on the 30% rate, with the Medicare levy); 14.7% face more than 50% and 1.3% face 60% or more (Table 5.7). The targeted package stacks a 20-cent taper on its FTB increment and the credit's phase-out on FTB, so fewer families sit at exactly 50% but more face very high rates: 21.8% above 50% and 14.2% at 60% or more.

Second earners keep less under P\*'s taper than today. Their mean participation tax rate, the share of their earnings lost to tax and withdrawn payments, is 33.0%, against 28.9% under the package, 32.9% under Part 1 and 27.3% under current rules. None faces 60% or more under P\*, against 6.8% under the package. Against Part 1, P\*'s rates are much the same: slightly fewer higher earners at high rates, but slightly more second earners at 40% or more (31.6% against 29.4%). So the value judgement that favours P\* concerns the package, not Part 1, which P\* beats on poverty. On Part 1's run, whose taper P\* keeps, a second earner taking a A$50,000 job keeps 62 cents of each dollar whether their partner earns A$100,000, A$130,000 or A$160,000; today they keep 61, 72 and 88 cents (Table 5.4). The taper is set by regulation, so couples' labour-supply modelling, due by 30 June 2027, can change it; that modelling will cost second earners who stop work (Recommendation 13).

Take-up is full for both. Table 5.4's 19% counts every parent with earnings, and this table each family's higher earner, so the two shares differ. Part 1's 18-cent taper on the 30% rate, with the 2% Medicare levy, gives exactly 50%, which the 50%-or-more row counts in full; an earlier count dropped families whose rate computes a fraction below 50%, which understated Part 1's share by 1.1 points.*

The PBO costed the raise-the-rate policy from 2025-26; re-timing its first-year cost to 2028-29 by the nominal GDP factor used for the revenue measures gives A$24.1bn, while the PBO's own figure for 2028-29 is A$27.2bn. P\*'s and Part 1's cells are revised for this paper; the other columns are the 22 October paper's.*

### The price of choosing Part 1

The 22 October paper recommended Part 1, though its own figures favoured P\* and the targeted package. This revision follows the figures. Part 1's reason was that its child payment is the one a universal Stage 2 would pay every child, so a later stage would change only the adult payment. That saves at most A$0.09bn to A$0.37bn once, in expectation, because even after a qualifying displacement Stage 2 passes the trial's work-effort cap with probability 0.16 to 0.21 at the OpenResearch trial's latest 3.9-point estimate. That saving is measured against the targeted package; against P\*, which keeps the same schedule for most families, it is close to nil. The paper's method leaves out one other value of Part 1: paying the supplement to middle-income families as a universal entitlement, with its reach and payment channel. That cannot be priced; its break-even weights are in Table 6.12. Nothing the model review found lifts Part 1's advantage above about A$0.4bn once, against P\*'s A$0.8bn to A$3.1bn a year.

Against that saving, at the same budget Part 1 leaves:

- 0.20 million more people in poverty than P\* (0.8 points; children 0.6 points) and A$1.5bn to A$1.8bn a year less net benefit on the complete accounting, with more poverty in every group on every robustness run (Table 5.7);
- 0.56 million to 0.61 million more people in poverty than the targeted package, 2.2 to 2.4 points (Tables 5.12 and 5.13).

A targeted route reaches Part 1's poverty rate for much less. The floor, automatic enrolment, Rent Assistance +75% and the earned-income credit give 11.2% for A$40.4bn, A$22.4bn a year less than Part 1's budget (Table 5.11). No value the paper can name is worth that price, so Part 1 is rejected. Parliament could still choose it, but it would need to state the value it places on paying the middle-income supplement now and accept 0.20 million more people in poverty to get it.

Tables 5.10 to 5.14 give the equal-budget comparisons the 22 October paper reported, which this revision does not change.
