### The cash guarantee, and who is worse off after housing costs

The 22 October paper said no current recipient would lose income. That holds only for payments. The guarantee this paper proposes is narrower and is stated as such: no one who receives, or would qualify for, a payment Stage 1 changes will be paid less than under today's rules. Under P\* the only payment replaced is Family Tax Benefit, so the guarantee works through family payment protection: every family keeps at least its current-rules FTB, indexed as current rules would index it. The Act defines the protected group by the payments' income and assets tests.

The guarantee does not cover income after housing costs. Pensioners' payments do not change at Stage 1, but in the model's rent and interest-rate scenario rents and mortgage rates rise as the reform lifts other incomes. On the paper's central scenario (45 basis points on variable mortgages and the middle of the rent pass-through range), 0.70 million recipient households (1.65 million people, 16% of recipient households) are worse off after housing costs under P\*, by A$770 a year on average (Table 6.21, provisional). Under Part 1 the figure was 1.11 million households (2.30 million people, 25% of recipient households), losing A$933 a year on average, including 60% of Disability Support Pension households and 84% of privately renting Age Pension households. No JobSeeker, Youth Allowance or Parenting Payment household is worse off under either design.

P\*'s Rent Assistance rise removes 37% of Part 1's losers, not most of them. Privately renting Age Pension households fall from 84% to 25% worse off and Disability Support Pension households from 60% to 34%, but pensioners who own their home or have a mortgage do not move: about 15% are worse off under both designs. The rent safeguard raises Rent Assistance further if rents outpace wages (Table 9.6), but it does not reach owners.

If a later Act legislated a universal payment, the guarantee would need more parts: a Recipient Protection Offset, so that protected recipients pay no more tax on other income than under current rules, capped at the tax current rules levy on the payment; and a phase-out of protection at 50 cents a dollar above a phase-out point, so there are no cliffs. Those parts would create a protected class worth A$21,000 to A$50,000 a year to the worked households at Stage 3 (Table 6.28), which people may claim a payment to acquire [S134]. A net-income guarantee avoids that and costs less, but protected recipients would gain little at later stages (Table 6.30). The choice belongs to the Act that would set a rate.

### The universal pathway: tests, not rates

The universal stages are a pathway Parliament can take if AI displaces work at scale. The Act would legislate three tests now and no rates (Table 5.15).

1. **An AI need test**, over the same 8 consecutive quarters. Youth unemployment must be at least 3 percentage points above its September 2026 level of 11.1%. Employment of 22 to 29 year olds in the most AI-exposed fifth of occupations must fall at least 5% relative to the least exposed, on JSA's series. Measured as a change since November 2022, the AI leg cannot stay met indefinitely, so a later general recession does not meet it. Productivity growth cannot start a stage. On the Budget's forecasts the test is not met; meeting it needs the employment-to-population ratio to fall at least 2 points.
2. **A randomised trial with a work-effort cap.** About 5,000 working-age adults not on a payment receive a payment at the JobSeeker rate from July 2029 to June 2031, against a control group. If participation falls more than 3 points, with the lower end of its 90% confidence interval above 1 point, no universal payment may be proposed under the Act.
3. **A PBO certificate.** The PBO costs any proposed rate afresh and certifies that funding at least equals net cost in each of the next four years; for a payment at the Age Pension rate, also under a doubled taxpayer response and the combined downside of Section 6. Under the AI receipts rule half of tax receipts above the 2026-27 Budget's projections, fixed when the Act passes and indexed to prices, count toward that funding; non-AI windfalls count too.

Only when all three are met may a Minister introduce a Bill setting a rate, and Parliament then votes on it. In a displacement declared after the need test's youth unemployment leg has been met for eight quarters, the floor and child payment keep their indexation and a levy meets any shortfall (Table 6.51).

What the pathway would cost comes from runs of the 22 October design (Part 1 with the wealth-tax fund's dividend), which have not been repeated on P\*; they show the order of magnitude, not P\*'s exact figures. A payment to every adult at the JobSeeker rate (Stage 2) would need every income-tax rate about 15.6 points higher on the smallest displacement that meets the need test, and 18.6 to 25.5 points on slower paths timed to the trigger (Table 6.38). The trial's own evidence makes it unlikely to start. At the trial's latest estimate of a 3.9-point fall in participation, the work-effort cap would bind with probability 0.79 to 0.84. A payment at the Age Pension rate (Stage 3) would need about 30.9 points, and no rate passes its certificate on any modelled path: a levy on market income alone cannot fund it, and under a doubled taxpayer response or the combined downside no rate rise does (Table 6.48). The paper therefore describes Stage 3 and does not authorise it.

Why legislate tests that will probably never be met? Because the tests, the data series and the trial take years to build, and the time to agree them is before a crisis, not during one. If AI cuts demand for labour across the economy, most people would qualify for means-tested payments, so targeting stops saving money and steep tapers fall on nearly everyone; a universal payment would then be the simplest way to share the gains. The tests are cheap: the trial costs about A$120m a year for two years, and the indicators and certificates about A$10m a year (Table 6.2). The pathway may never be used, and the paper says so (Section 8).

Legislating no rate also removes a risk the 22 October paper conceded: a contingent rise of 16 to 31 points written into law could change where high earners settle and when they realise income, before any trigger.

**Table 5.15. What the Act legislates now, and what it leaves to a later Act, from 2027**

| Element | In the Act now | Left to a later Act |
|---|---|---|
| Stage 1 (P\*) | Rates, indexation, the child payment rule, the Rent Assistance rise and the cash guarantee | – |
| Five durable taxes and the Basic Income Levy | Yes; the levy is nil until surpluses kept from earlier years are used up | – |
| AI need test | Yes, with the JSA and ABS series it needs | – |
| Randomised trial and work-effort cap | Yes: trial from July 2029 to June 2031; the cap binds | – |
| PBO certificate | Yes, with a stress test for any payment at the Age Pension rate | – |
| AI receipts rule | Yes | – |
| A universal payment at the JobSeeker rate (Stage 2) | No rate; described | The rate, start date, protections and the tax rise, after all three tests are met |
| A universal payment at the Age Pension rate (Stage 3) | Not authorised | Everything |
| A dividend to every adult | No | One of the four options in Section 10, after a PBO costing and the tests set out there |

*Source: author's proposal.*

### A dividend: left for later

The 22 October paper set out four ways to pay a yearly dividend to every adult and left the choice to Parliament. None reduces poverty: Stage 1 gives the same poverty rate with or without a dividend, because a payment to everyone lifts the median and the line with it. This revision recommends none of them now and moves them to their own chapter, Section 10, with the tests any dividend should pass before Parliament legislates it.

## 6. Making the dollars work

The recommended Stage 1 is paid for by five taxes on large superannuation balances, excess profits, mining, gas and business fuel, with borrowing in its first year. It costs A$62.0bn in 2028-29, the same as Part 1, and A$1,667.6bn over 2028-29 to 2045-46, between 1.71% and 1.86% of GDP in every year (Table 6.1). In 2028-29 the five taxes fall A$20.7bn short, and the Budget borrows the gap, repaid by 30 June 2032. From 2037-38 the taxes fall short each year; surpluses kept from the 2030s fill the gap until a levy on market income from 2043-44 on central yields, or 2044-45 if Rent Assistance grows as the fiscal model's own path assumes (Table 6.6). That date is uncertain: a joint stress test gives a 48% to 74% chance of a levy before 2044-45 and 37% to 59% before 2034-35 (Table 6.9, provisional). Every Stage 1 design has a negative monetised net benefit on the complete accounting, which counts the cost of the taxes and Stage 1's own behavioural costs, and a range spanning zero on the paper's method, which counts the taxes' cost alone; P\* ranks above Part 1 on every reading (Table 6.12). A universal payment at the Age Pension rate cannot be funded from income tax on central assumptions (Tables 6.34 to 6.53). The P\* figures in this chapter are provisional: they rest on the re-costing at Part 1's budget (Section 5) and on how Rent Assistance grows.

### Price basis and status of these figures

All figures are the author's modelling, not a PBO costing, in nominal dollars on an underlying cash basis. They use the 2026-27 Budget's parameters: wages grow 3.5% a year, prices 2.5% and labour productivity 1.2% [S61, S127]. Nominal GDP grows 4.97% a year after 2029-30, matching the PBO's medium-term projections [S139].

The central path is now P\* with no dividend, and its tables are marked "P\*". Many other tables were run on the 22 October design, Part 1, often with the wealth-tax fund's dividend. Each says which design it was run on, and they are kept where the comparison or the sensitivity still applies. The dividend options' own tables are in Section 10.

As in PBO practice, the costing states its behavioural responses [S140]. Earners reduce market income by 16 cents for each dollar of net gain from the child payment, as in the OpenResearch trial [S82]. That figure is the trial's fall in income other than transfers per dollar received, not its fall in participation, so the correction of participation from 4.2 to 3.9 points does not by itself change it; it could not be re-checked against the trial's latest revision, and if it had moved in proportion it would be about 15 cents. The floor adds recipients, including people who leave work and return to payment [S113]. Taxpayers and parents respond to higher rates and tapers with a taxable-income elasticity of 0.2 (Section 11), at all incomes, not only above A$180,000 as the PBO assumes. Effects on wages, prices, employment and goods and services tax (GST) receipts are left out, and interest rates and rents enter only through a scenario.

### The recommended Stage 1: gross and net cost

Including implementation and the income-fall review, P\* costs A$62.0bn in 2028-29 and A$130.8bn in 2045-46 (Table 6.1). It tracks Part 1 closely: A$0.05bn more in 2028-29 by construction and at most A$1.0bn more in any later year, because its Rent Assistance rise follows its maximum rates while rents grow with incomes. If Rent Assistance grows instead on the fiscal model's own path (prices and population), P\* costs up to A$1.5bn a year less than Part 1 by 2045-46.

### The funding stack

Five durable revenue measures pay for Stage 1, each with a PBO election commitment costing (ECR) prepared for the Australian Greens at the 2025 election [S145, S146, S147, S148, S149]. Each starts in 2028-29, scaled by nominal GDP, except that the gas measure keeps falling (Tables 6.4 and 6.5). After the PBO's last year the fuel-credit and coal-and-minerals measures are held flat in real terms. The fuel-credit measure's base is machinery and large vehicles, which "will still receive fuel tax credits" as light vehicles electrify [S151]. For the minerals measure flat real growth is a conservative assumption: resource export earnings are forecast to ease on lower prices, with thermal coal volumes falling as importers "shift away from thermal coal power generation" [S152]. Growing both with GDP, as earlier drafts did, is the high case: on Part 1's run the levy then starts only in 2045-46 (Table 6.10). All five are first-round: the costings "do not include broader second round effect of this policy" [S146]. A sixth measure, a net wealth tax, appears only under one of the dividend options in Section 10, with the revenue the PBO says it would cost in personal income tax [S133, S150]; Table 6.4 shows it as a memo.

### The recommended Stage 1: the five taxes, the borrowing and the levy

The five taxes do not cover P\*'s first year. In 2028-29 they raise A$41.3bn against a net cost of A$62.0bn, so the Budget borrows A$20.7bn (provisional). The paper calls this what it is: borrowing, repaid with interest at the debt rate (4.13%) from the taxes' later surpluses by 30 June 2032. Net debt is A$20.7bn, A$18.8bn and A$7.7bn above baseline at 30 June 2029, 2030 and 2031. For Part 1 the borrowing was A$20.6bn.

Surpluses are then kept as debt reduction, reaching A$44.9bn at 30 June 2038. From 2037-38 the taxes fall short of net cost each year, the kept surpluses are drawn down, and on central yields a levy on market income meets the gap from 2043-44, at 0.62% of market income in 2044-45 and 2045-46 (Table 6.6; Figure 6.1). Levies total A$36.5bn to 2045-46, against A$25.8bn for Part 1, because P\*'s Rent Assistance rise grows faster than Part 1's supplement. If Rent Assistance instead grows on the fiscal model's own path, the first levy is in 2044-45 and levies total A$19.8bn. The five taxes must yield 100.2% of their costings for no levy before 2044-45, against 99.7% for Part 1, and the joint stress test gives a 37% to 59% chance of a levy before 2034-35 (Table 6.9, provisional). The cash balance is worse than baseline in 7 of the 18 years.

The 2028-29 shortfall is borrowed by the Budget and repaid with interest at the debt rate (4.13%). Surpluses are then kept as debt reduction and drawn down when the taxes first fall short of net cost; the levy meets the gap once they are used up. Cash balance against baseline = taxes + levy − net cost + interest; net debt against baseline is minus the balance. PROVISIONAL.*

### A phased start to borrow less

Starting the child payment a year after the floor would avoid the borrowing altogether. Table 6.7 costs that phasing: the floor and Rent Assistance +75% from 1 July 2028, and the child payment from 1 July 2029, with families keeping current FTB in 2028-29. Stage 1 then costs A$24.8bn in 2028-29, less than the five taxes raise, so there is no borrowing; the surplus kept carries forward, and on central yields no levy is needed to 2045-46. The price is a year without the child payment, which would have paid families A$26.4bn above the Family Tax Benefit they keep (the budget saves A$37.3bn, which also includes A$7.2bn of timing, behavioural responses and the income-fall review that are not cash to families): most of Stage 1's fall in child poverty is delayed by a year. The paper offers the phased start to Parliament as an option with that price; it does not recommend it.

|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|

| P*: floor only in 2028-29 | 20.1 | 0.0 | 21.2 | 20.7 | none to 2045-46 | 2043-44 | 0.0 | 36.5 | 49.4 |

*Source: author's fiscal model, option B runs (model results Table B13) [S5]. Note: in 2028-29 families keep current FTB (no child payment cost and no FTB replaced); from 2029-30 the design in full. The five taxes as costed: their 2028-29 yield, A$41.3bn, exceeds the phased cost, so the borrowing becomes a surplus kept. PROVISIONAL.*

### How strong is the evidence behind the numbers?

Every revenue measure has an official costing, but the PBO warns that several are highly uncertain. The evidence on incidence, financing costs, the fund's return, the child payment's flows and household effects is weaker still (Table 6.8).

**Table 6.8. Strength of the fiscal, behavioural, incidence, financing-cost and household evidence behind the costing and the distribution tables (costings and estimates published 2006 to 2026)**

| Evidence (year) | What it supplies | Type of evidence | Strength and main caveat |
|---|---|---|---|
| PBO ECR-2025-3128, superannuation above the transfer balance cap (2025) [S145] | A$9.0bn in 2028-29 | Official costing | **Strong to moderate.** The PBO's plausible band is about plus or minus A$5bn by 2035-36, because the affected group is small and changes each year; first-round, like every PBO costing. The PBO costed it as superseding Divisions 293 and 296 [S145], so it is net of Division 296 as designed in 2023; the enacted Division 296 (Act No. 8 of 2026, assented 13 March 2026), which from 1 July 2026 taxes realised earnings on balances above A$3 million [S153], raises less, so the measure is already net of it. Table 6.10 halves the measure as a test of the costing |
| PBO ECR-2025-3194, 40% excess profits tax (2025) [S146] | A$14.8bn in 2028-29, rising | Official costing | **Moderate.** The PBO reports a high degree of uncertainty and that its costings "do not include broader second round effect of this policy"; very sensitive to economic conditions; behaviour could be greater or smaller than estimated |
| PBO ECR-2025-3518, coal and minerals super-profits tax (2025) [S147] | A$7.2bn in 2028-29 | Official costing | **Moderate.** The PBO says it is "extremely sensitive to forecasts of mineral prices"; a highly variable tax; first-round |

| PBO ECR-2025-3463, net wealth tax (2025) [S150] | A$26.5bn in 2028-29, falling to nil | Official costing | **Weak to moderate.** The capital-flight rate is indicative only; affected people would likely respond with legal action; valuation is uncertain; second-round effects are not included. The PBO adds that losses in personal and company tax mean "it is possible that the proposal could reduce revenue overall". ### The risk to the funding: a joint stress test

The central levy date rests on every assumption holding at once, and they will not. A joint stress test draws them together, 4,000 times (Table 6.9). Each is drawn from a triangular distribution over the paper's own tested range: the five taxes' yield (80% to 120% of their costings), the floor's caseload response (elasticity 0.21 to 0.56), the response to the child payment's taper (0.1 to 0.4) and the labour response (0.05 to 0.29). Cost overruns of nil to A$2bn a year, the floor costed on the model's or the PBO's basis, and the household model's three calibrations are drawn too. AI displacement follows the paper's moderate or severe scenario in 10% and 5% of draws, an assumption, because the paper has no probabilities for them.

On P\* the chance of a levy before 2044-45 is 48% to 74%, and before 2034-35 37% to 59%. The low ends draw only the symmetric risks (yield, behaviour and calibration); the high ends add cost overruns, the PBO's floor costing and AI displacement. The first levy is most often in 2032-33, because borrowing not repaid by 30 June 2032 is levied at once; in 22% of draws there is no levy to 2045-46. P\* carries the same funding risk as Part 1 (47% to 73% before 2044-45); the targeted package carries more (70% to 87%). The 59% and 45% in the revision plan came from a first run that could not be reproduced; these figures replace them.

So the levy is better read as a range than as a date. The central path is the case in which every assumption lands on its central value at once; the range in Table 6.9 is the better guide.

|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|

### Sensitivity: one assumption at a time

Stage 1 has no fund to absorb a shortfall, so its levy moves with every assumption. On Part 1's run the five taxes had to yield 99.7% of their costings for no levy before 2044-45. At 90% the levy started in 2032-33 and peaked at 1.82% of market income; at 80% it totalled A$370.8bn to 2045-46 and peaked at 4.41%, or started in 2029-30 and peaked at 1.49% if the borrowing is repaid over five years. P\*'s results, in the last three columns of Table 6.10, are close to Part 1's in every case: at 80% its levy starts in 2032-33 and peaks at 4.47% of market income, and the five taxes must yield 100.2% of their costings for no levy before 2044-45. Calibration moves the date either way.

Note: under the paper's rule borrowing still outstanding after the year it is due to be repaid is levied at once, which is why Part 1's largest levy falls in 2032-33 in the shortfall cases; the five-year rule repays it over five years. Each case changes one assumption from the central case. The labour rows scale the child payment's income effect with the response; the calibration rows re-solve the household model and recompute the child payment's rule flows. The literal reading of the safeguard levies each year's shortfall and leaves the kept surpluses as lower debt; the Act states the drawdown rule instead (Tables 7.1 and 9.6). The superannuation case tests the costing: the PBO costed the measure as superseding Division 296's 2023 design, so the enacted Division 296 does not reduce it.*

### Net benefit

On the complete accounting, which counts the economic cost of the taxes that finance it and the design's own behavioural costs, every Stage 1 design and every later stage has a negative monetised net benefit; on the paper's method, which counts the taxes' cost alone, Stage 1's ranges span zero (Tables 6.12 and 6.13). Payments move income between people rather than using it up, so the real costs are implementation, market income forgone and each tax's excess burden. Treasury puts that burden at 50 cents a dollar for company income tax and 21 cents for a flat tax on labour income [S159] (Table 6.11). No published estimate for a wealth tax was found [S179]; its range of 25 cents to A$1 is the author's, built from the PBO's assumed shrinking of the base by 10% of its initial value a year [S150].

**Table 6.11. Marginal excess burden assigned to each funding measure, 2028-29 (A$ billion; cost per dollar of revenue)**

| Measure | Revenue 2028-29 (A$ billion) | Excess burden per dollar, low | Excess burden per dollar, high | Basis |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Super above the transfer balance cap (ECR-2025-3128) | 9.0 | 0.20 | 0.34 | Personal income tax benchmark: Treasury 21c for a flat labour income tax [S159]; Murphy 21% [S160]; Tran and Wende 34c [S162] |
| 40% excess profits tax (ECR-2025-3194) | 14.8 | 0.00 | 0.50 | Low: a tax on economic rent has a negative or nil burden (Murphy: rent tax −10%, floored at zero) [S161]; high: company income tax, Treasury 50c [S159]; Murphy 84% [S160] and Tran and Wende 83c [S162] would raise it |
| Coal and minerals super-profits tax (ECR-2025-3518) | 7.2 | 0.00 | 0.10 | Low: resource rent tax (Murphy: PRRT −10%, floored at zero) [S161]; high: author's allowance for imperfect measurement of rents |
| PRRT reform and 10% offshore royalty (ECR-2025-3123) | 2.9 | 0.00 | 0.15 | Low: PRRT −10%, floored at zero [S161]; high: royalty burden of 47% [S161] on about one-third of the revenue (author) |
| End fossil-fuel subsidies incl. fuel tax credits (ECR-2025-3001) | 7.4 | 0.20 | 0.50 | Benchmarks, as no published estimate for fuel excise was found: low, personal income tax and GST (Murphy 21% each [S160]); high, company tax (Treasury 50c [S159]), because the credits tax a business input |
| Net wealth tax (ECR-2025-3463) | 26.5 | 0.25 | 1.00 | No published estimate found (the OECD's review gives none [S179]); author's range from the PBO's assumption that the base shrinks by 10% of its initial value each year [S150]; see the note |
| Less: personal income tax interaction (ECR-2025-3901) | 0.1 | 0.20 | 0.34 | Lost personal income tax: personal income tax benchmark |
| Stage 2 and 3 income-tax rate rises | – | 0.20 | 0.34 or more | Personal income tax benchmark: low 0.20 [S159, S160], against Treasury's 27c and 32c at average rates of 21.5% and 25%; high the larger of 0.34 [S162] and the model's own response-implied rate |

*Source: author's assignment from the sources cited [S5]. Note: Treasury's figures are from its Working Paper 2015-01 (Tables 2 to 4), a static model with one representative household; Murphy's figures are from conference presentations marked preliminary. A burden below zero for a rent tax is floored at zero. Wealth tax: under a linear response, the PBO's assumption implies a burden of 0.12 in the first year, 0.33 in the second, 0.75 in the third and 2.00 in the fourth, so the range is not an upper bound after year 3. Fuel tax credits: no published estimate of the excess burden of fuel excise was found, so the range runs from the personal income tax benchmark to company tax, because the credits tax a business input.*

The central benefit is the reduction in the household poverty gap after housing costs, valued with a distributional weight of 1.5 to 2.0, the author's value judgement. Table 6.12 applies that method to the three Stage 1 designs. On the complete accounting all three are negative: P\* −A$23.3bn to −A$2.3bn, the targeted package −A$25.2bn to nil and Part 1 −A$24.8bn to −A$4.1bn in 2028-29. Against Part 1, P\* gains A$1.5bn to A$1.8bn a year and the package −A$0.4bn to A$4.1bn, so Part 1 ranks below P\* on every reading and below the package on all but its least favourable one.

The method is narrow. It counts the poverty gap closed and the cost of the taxes, and nothing else: not health, care or the value of insurance. A broader method, the UK Green Book's, puts a weight on every household's gains and taxes according to its income (an inequality-aversion elasticity of 1.3). On that method all three designs turn positive: Part 1 at A$37.0bn to A$45.8bn, P\* at A$40.2bn to A$49.0bn and the package at A$50.9bn to A$59.7bn, so the same weights widen the package's lead (provisional). Table 6.12 also gives each design's break-even weight: the weight at which its net benefit reaches zero.

So the paper does not claim a positive monetised benefit for Stage 1. It claims that Stage 1 cuts poverty substantially at a cost the five taxes largely meet, and that among the designs of equal cost P\* is the paper's choice, weighing poverty against work incentives and fiscal sustainability. On every method the package is ahead of P\* or close to it: by A$2.0bn to A$4.3bn a year on the paper's method, by −A$1.9bn to A$2.3bn on the complete accounting and by about A$10.7bn with the Green Book's weights (Section 5 sets out the value judgement and its price).

Note: the paper's method values the reduction in the household poverty gap after housing costs at (weight − 1) and charges the excess burden of each tax (Table 6.11) and, in the complete accounting, the fiscal cost of the design's own behavioural responses at 1 in the low case and ½ in the high. Part 1's complete-accounting figure is −A$24.8bn to −A$4.1bn here and −A$24.9bn to −A$4.1bn in the 22 October paper; the difference is rounding. The Green Book method weights each household cash flow by (median ÷ quintile mean equivalised disposable income) to the power 1.3, with the design's whole 2028-29 cost borne as the five taxes are. P\* and package figures are PROVISIONAL (the option B re-costing), and so are all the Green Book figures (the quintile weighting and the cost allocation are choices made for this revision).*

Note: monetised benefit = (weight − 1) × the reduction in the household poverty gap, because a transfer itself has no net social value. The two memo rows are Part 1 alone (no dividend), from model Table F4-67.*

The monetised case for any dividend is not made either: on the complete accounting every dividend option scores below Stage 1 alone (Section 10). The table cannot value coverage, payment without an income test, insurance against displacement or the health and care effects in Section 3; Parliament has to weigh those against each option's measured price.

### Who gains and who loses, after the taxes that pay for it

After the five taxes are allocated, 34.2% of adults live in households better off under P\* in 2028-29, or 32.4% by A$5 a week or more, against 32.5% under Part 1 and 43.0% under the targeted package; before the taxes, 65.3% do (Table 6.14, provisional). Of households, 35.4% are better off after the taxes, and 32.6% to 41.4% across the incidence variants; before the taxes, 62.1% gain. Deciles 1 to 7 gain on average after the taxes and deciles 8 to 10 lose (Table 6.15). The taxes fall mostly on the top income decile, which bears 52.9% of them, and the top three deciles bear 88.4% (Table 6.15). The same taxes fund every Stage 1 design, so the designs differ in who gains, not in who pays.

These results come first because a household's gain from Stage 1 means little without its share of the taxes that pay for it. The before-funding figures sit beside them. Table 6.15 gives each income decile's share of the five taxes, the allocation the online calculator now uses. Tables 6.16 to 6.20 give the 22 October paper's results by decile, incidence assumption and payment, run on Part 1 and its six taxes; they are on the fiscal model's individual-income basis, on which 29.0% of adults were better off under Part 1, against 32.5% on the household basis used here.

That assumption is contestable: Treasury's modelling finds that about two-thirds of the long-run benefit of a company tax cut goes to households through wages [S158]. If half the excess profits tax, or two-thirds of all company-type taxes, falls on wages, 28% of adults are better off at Stage 1, and the bottom eight deciles still gain (Table 6.17). Allocating the fuel measure by petrol spending leaves 72% of adults worse off at Stage 1, not 71% (Table 6.18).

### The cash guarantee and households worse off after housing costs

No recipient is paid less under P\* than under today's rules: payments are unchanged or higher, and families keep at least their current-rules FTB. The guarantee is that no one's payment falls under today's rules, not that no one loses. It does not extend to income after housing costs. In the paper's central rent and interest-rate scenario (Tables 6.31 and 6.32), mortgage rates rise 45 basis points and private renters' rents rise by the middle of the pass-through range, while pensioners' payments do not change. On that scenario 0.70 million recipient households, 16% of them, are worse off after housing costs under P\*, by A$770 a year on average (Table 6.21).

Under Part 1 the figure was 1.11 million households, a quarter of recipient households, losing A$933 a year on average. P\*'s Rent Assistance rise removes 37% of them. Privately renting Age Pension households fall from 84% to 25% worse off, Disability Support Pension households from 60% to 34% and Carer Payment households from 54% to 34%. Pensioners who own their home or have a mortgage do not move: 15% are worse off under both designs, because Rent Assistance does not reach them. No JobSeeker, student or Parenting Payment household is worse off under any design. The targeted package leaves 0.56 million households worse off. Most of those P\* leaves worse off are Disability Support Pension households (0.28 million) and Age Pension households that own or are buying their home (0.26 million). Counting the scenario's effects, poverty on the relative line is 11.1% with P\*, 11.7% with Part 1 and 9.8% with the package (Table 5.7).

Each design's own first-round gain is used, so Rent Assistance rises are themselves partly passed into rents. Worse off means the first-round gain less higher repayments and rent is below −A$1. ### Household poverty

The tables in this section were run on Part 1; P\*'s results at the same budget are in Table 5.7. On the relative line that ACOSS and UNSW publish, Part 1 cuts poverty after housing costs from 14.2% to 11.3% in the model, with or without the wealth-tax fund's dividend (Table 6.22), and P\* to 10.5%. It raises the median, so the line rises from A$592 to A$631 a week: 0.93 million people are lifted and 0.16 million fall below the new line. On a line held at the pre-reform median, poverty falls to 9.6%. In the model, child poverty falls from 15.6% to 7.2% and poverty in sole-parent households from 31.9% to 16.4%. On the relative line, which rises with the median, poverty rises for groups whose incomes barely change. It goes from 11.1% to 12.1% at 67 and over, 23.3% to 24.9% in Age Pension-reliant and 63.6% to 68.2% in DSP-reliant households. For older renters it goes from 30.8% to 33.1%, for older owners from 7.8% to 8.6% and for couples without children from 11.5% to 12.2% (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-117).

That result depends on full-rate pensioners' private incomes, which the model assumes because DSS data could not be obtained [S135]. With incomes a quarter lower, the variant nearest DSS's 2015-16 data, Stage 1 gives 11.7% (11.8% without the dividend) and people aged 67 and over 13.5%; with half, 12.8% and 19.7% (Table 6.23). If triggered, Stages 2 and 3 cut poverty to 7.9% and 4.9%.

The model pays the child payment on current income, while the design pays on income 1.5 years old. A crude test draws 6% or 9% of families with children at random for a 20% or 40% fall in income, as the Reserve Bank found for about 6% of indebted households a year. It gives child poverty of 7.2% to 7.3% on the relative line and 5.5% anchored (Table 6.24). It ignores who has volatile incomes and any rises in income, so it bounds the effect rather than estimating it.

### The individual-income measure

The individual-income measure of earlier drafts overstates the effect, because 54% to 67% of its gap reduction goes to adults in households that are not poor (Table 6.26).

### Five households at the boundaries

The phase-out removes the cliffs but leaves bands of 82% to 94% effective marginal tax rates (EMTRs). Single workers above about A$80,000 at Stage 2, or above A$60,000 to A$70,000 at Stage 3, have less net income than now. These worked examples were run on the 22 October design. At Stage 1 a single JobSeeker recipient with no other income gains A$9,655 a year, a single parent with a four-year-old A$11,177 and a couple earning A$70,000 and A$30,000 with two school-age children A$17,562. An age-pensioner couple with A$30,000 of investment income gains nothing (Tables 6.27 to 6.30).

The JobSeeker recipient's EMTR stays at 66% to 70% while the payment tapers, then rises to 82% to 94% in the phase-out band, against 32% to 34% now. The rate rise lifts the single worker's EMTR from 32% to 47% at Stage 2 and 58% at Stage 3.

### Housing tenure and interest rates

Extra demand from Stage 1 could raise interest rates and rents. The scenario was run on Part 1. The scenario assumes extra demand of 0.6% to 1.2% of GDP, more than Stage 1's household gains imply (Section 8). It also assumes a cash-rate rise of 25 to 75 basis points per 1% of GDP, anchored on the Reserve Bank's MARTIN model [S167]. That gives 15 to 90 basis points on variable mortgages [S175, S176]. Private rents capture 6.6% to 32.4% of renters' gains, the range the Australian Housing and Urban Research Institute (AHURI) found for Rent Assistance [S164], against 60% to 78% for tied allowances abroad [S165, S166].

About 17% of Stage 1's first-round household gains are then lost in the central case. Poverty after housing costs is 11.6% to 12.0% on the relative line, against 11.3% first-round, and 9.8% to 10.6% on the anchored line (Tables 6.31 and 6.32). A renting pensioner couple with no cash gain is A$582 to A$2,856 a year worse off, which is why P\* raises Rent Assistance 75% and the rent safeguard raises it further (Table 9.6). The scenario leaves out depositors' interest, landlords' extra rent and adult children leaving home.

### Funding if the universal pathway were triggered

Everything from here to the assumptions register, except Figure 6.3, was run on the 22 October design: Part 1 with the wealth-tax fund's dividend, and Stages 2 and 3 at the rates that paper costed. No universal rate is now legislated; these tables show what a later Act would face. The lower reference path has Stage 1 in full and Stages 2 and 3 at their earliest dates with no displacement, which cannot meet the need test. On it net cost is 1.9% of GDP at Stage 1, 8.2% at Stage 2 and 12.2% at Stage 3, and 11.9% by 2045-46 (Tables 6.34 and 6.35).

The adjustment column takes out the wealth tax, which goes to the fund net of the interaction line, and adds the dividend the fund pays and the 2028-29 start-up advance. No official costing of a uniform rise in every rate exists: the rate-rise revenue is the author's estimate, scaled from PBO costing ECR-2025-2525 (Table 6.39). Reprioritised funding in 2027-28 comes from Services Australia's Program 1.3.*

### Funding if Stages 2 and 3 are triggered

The smallest displacement that meets the need test, the employment-to-population ratio 2 points lower by 2030-31 with recipients 10% and profits 3.3% higher, gives the headline rises of 15.6 and 30.9 points (Table 6.38). With no displacement, which cannot meet the test, the rises would be 14.9 and 25.8 points. On slower trigger-timed paths Stage 2 needs 18.6 or 25.5, and with wages 2% lower or a larger early shock Stage 3 is past the revenue peak.

**Table 6.38. Funding paths: Stage 1 continuing, and Stages 2 and 3 if triggered, 2028-29 to 2045-46 (percentage points; A$ billion, nominal)**

| Path | Rises, aggregate form (Stage 2 / Stage 3, points) | Rises, per-person form | **Rise that covers the stage: the larger of the two, or the best attainable rise where neither form restores funding** | Smallest margin in Stages 2 and 3 at that rise (A$ billion; year) | Stage 1 years: smallest margin (A$ billion; year) | Net cost 2032-33 (A$ billion) | Net cost 2037-38 (A$ billion) | Cumulative margin at the fund's return, 2045-46 (A$ billion) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| A. Central path: Stage 1 continues with the fund; no stage triggered (Tables 10.10 and 10.11) | none | none | **none** | durable taxes short from 2039-40 (levy) | −10.8 (2045-46) | – | – | – |
| **B. Smallest shock that meets the need test in time (headline): employment-to-population ratio 2 points lower by 2030-31, wages unchanged, recipients +10%, profits +3.3%; Stage 2 from 2032-33, Stage 3 from 2037-38** | 15.5 / 30.7 | 15.6 / 30.9 | **15.6 / 30.9** | −1.5 (2045-46): short under the per-person form at the best rise; past the revenue peak | −8.9 (2030-31) | 335.6 | 685.5 | 207.0 |
| B2. Same, with wages 2% lower | 15.8 / beyond peak | 15.9 / beyond peak | not feasible | – | – | – | – | – |
| C. Moderate shock (ratio 3 points lower, wages 3% lower) in place by 2030-31 | 16.4 / beyond peak | 16.5 / beyond peak | not feasible | – | – | – | – | – |
| D. Lower reference: earliest dates, no displacement (cannot meet the need test; Tables 6.34 and 6.37) | 14.8 / 25.7 | 14.9 / 25.8 | **14.9 / 25.8** | 0.4 (2045-46) | 0.0 (2028-29) | 333.7 | 633.9 | 216.2 |
| E. Moderate shock from 2032-33; Stage 2 from 2037-38, when the test is met (no Stage 3 by 2045-46) | 18.5 / 0.0 | 18.6 / 0.0 | **18.6 / 0.0** | 0.8 (2045-46) | −12.8 (2036-37) | 79.2 | 441.4 | 151.5 |
| F. Severe shock from 2032-33; Stage 2 from 2035-36, when the test is met (no Stage 3 by 2045-46) | 25.3 / 0.0 | 25.5 / 0.0 | **25.5 / 0.0** | 0.6 (2045-46) | −24.0 (2034-35) | 86.4 | 483.2 | 359.1 |

*Source: author's modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-35) [S5]. Note: each rise follows the stage rule (the largest need in any year of the stage, rounded up to 0.1 of a point) in each form, and the larger covers the stage; no Stage 3 rise passes its certificate on any path (Table 6.48), so the Act sets none. Funding for Stages 2 and 3 is the durable taxes plus the dividend the fund pays on the central path, an approximation, since a stage's own surpluses and shortfalls would change the fund. Surpluses in years when a rise over-collects go to the fund. Displacement paths are measured against a baseline that has absorbed the shock. On the lower reference path the Stage 3 rise is 25.6 points in the aggregate form used by the costing tables; this table's solver gives 25.7 in that form and 25.8 in the per-person form, which the Act would legislate as the larger.*

### Is the rate-rise revenue reliable?

No official costing of a uniform rise in every income-tax rate exists. The revenue is therefore scaled from PBO costing ECR-2025-2525, which raised the second tax rate from 14% to 16% on incomes from A$18,201 to A$45,000 and put the gain at A$7.4bn in 2028-29 [S154]. The model reproduces that figure (Table 6.39).

**Table 6.39. Anchoring the rate-rise revenue to a PBO costing, 2028-29, 2032-33 and 2037-38 (A$ billion, nominal; percentage points)**

| Item | 2028-29 | 2032-33 (Stage 2) | 2037-38 (Stage 3) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| PBO costing ECR-2025-2525: 16% instead of 14% on taxable income from A$18,201 to A$45,000 (A$ billion) | 7.4 | – | – |
| Author's model, same change (A$ billion) | 7.4 | – | – |
| Taxable income in the A$18,201 to A$45,000 band (model, A$ billion) | 368.4 | – | – |
| Revenue per percentage point on that band: PBO and model (A$ billion) | 3.7 | – | – |
| Taxable income on which the stage rise is levied, including the universal payment, protected recipients excluded (A$ billion) | – | 2,083.6 | 2,819.9 |
| Revenue per percentage point on all of that income, before any response (A$ billion) | – | 20.8 | 28.2 |
| Rise on the earliest-dates path, aggregate form (percentage points) | – | 14.8 | 25.6 |
| Revenue before any response, from the unrounded revenue per point (A$ billion) | – | 308.4 | 721.9 |
| Less: taxpayer response, elasticity 0.2 in its standard (constant-elasticity) form, at average marginal rates of 40.5% and 42.9% (A$ billion) | – | −51.5 | −162.8 |
| **Revenue from the legislated rise, aggregate form (Table 6.37)** | – | **256.9** | **559.1** |

*Source: PBO costing ECR-2025-2525, Table 1, first page [S154]; author's modelling [S5]. Note: the model reproduces the PBO costing; the stage rises apply the same per-point method to all taxable income, and the author deducts the taxpayer response at the PBO's elasticity of 0.2 [S140]. Underlying cash basis. The anchor tests only one band, while incomes above A$180,000 in 2023-24 dollars hold 19% of the Stage 3 base. The model's mean taxable income is within 0.6% of the ATO's [S171], but it puts 32% of tax on incomes above A$180,000 (2023-24 dollars), against 37% in ATO data for 2022-23 [S172], slightly understating the response.*

### The tax change needed at Stages 2 and 3

On the lower reference path the rise needed grows from 14.3 points in 2032-33 to 14.8 in 2036-37, and from 24.1 in 2037-38 to 25.6 in 2045-46 (Table 6.40). At the headline 30.9-point rise a taxpayer on today's 30% rate would face 60.9% at Stage 3 if a rate were ever legislated.

Note: the first gap is the net cost of payments (Table 6.34) less the six revenue measures (Table 6.4); moving the wealth tax to the fund, and adding the dividend it pays and the start-up advance, gives the gap after the funding available, against which the rises are set. 'With taxpayer response' uses an elasticity of taxable income of 0.2 in its standard form [S140]. The levy applies to market income only; 'beyond peak' means no levy on market income alone raises enough. The rises in Table 6.37 also cover implementation and the largest need in any year of the stage.*

### AI receipts

AI-driven growth would raise the five taxes' yield and, under the AI receipts rule, lower any rate rise a later Act needs (Tables 6.41 and 6.42).

**Table 6.41. AI receipts: excess profits and minerals taxes plus half of new AI receipts, per eligible adult, under three productivity paths, 2032-33, 2037-38 and 2045-46 (A$ a year, nominal)**

| Year | Eligible adults (million) | Productivity Commission path: all AI receipts | 2.0% a year: all AI receipts | 3.0% a year: all AI receipts | Productivity Commission path: new AI receipts only | 2.0% a year: new AI receipts only | 3.0% a year: new AI receipts only |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2032-33 | 21.24 | 2,732 | 3,396 | 5,261 | 752 | 1,416 | 3,281 |
| 2037-38 | 22.78 | 3,829 | 5,549 | 9,734 | 1,318 | 3,038 | 7,223 |
| 2045-46 | 25.00 | 5,085 | 10,339 | 20,726 | 1,771 | 7,025 | 17,412 |

*Source: author's modelling [S5, S30, S32, S127]. Note: "all AI receipts" includes the excess profits and minerals taxes, which also appear in the revenue measures; "new AI receipts only" avoids that double count. Eligible adults exclude people in the waiting period. The receipts do not depend on the stage design.*

Under the AI receipts rule (Section 5), half of tax receipts above the 2026-27 Budget's projections, fixed when the Act passes and indexed to prices, count toward a Stage 2 or 3 rise. The rule cannot tell AI windfalls from others, so a commodity boom would also count. Even at 3.0% productivity growth a year, the Stage 3 rise needed in 2037-38 is then 15.8 points (Figure 6.2; Table 6.42).

### Break-even conditions

AI-driven growth pays for the step from Stage 2 to Stage 3 only if labour productivity grows 3.40% a year from 2026-27, against the Budget's 1.2%, counting only receipts not already in the six measures (Table 6.43).

**Table 6.43. Break-even conditions: what AI-driven growth must deliver, 2032-33 to 2045-46 (per cent)**

| Test | Labour productivity growth needed, sustained from 2026-27 (% a year) | GDP above the Budget baseline in the test year (%) |
|---|---:|---:|
| New AI receipts alone cover the Stage 2 to Stage 3 step in every year, 2037-38 to 2045-46 | 3.40 | 29.4 (2037-38) |
| All extra receipts replace the Stage 2 rate rise, 2032-33 | 4.29 | 23.5 (2032-33) |
| All extra receipts replace the Stage 3 rate rise, 2037-38 | 3.96 | 38.1 (2037-38) |
| All extra receipts replace the Stage 3 rate rise, 2045-46 | 2.84 | 38.0 (2045-46) |
| *Comparator:* Budget baseline | 1.2 | 0 |
| *Comparator:* Productivity Commission estimate, 4.3% over a decade | About 1.6 | 4.3 (2035-36) |

*Source: author's modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-17) [S5, S30, S32, S127]. Note: "all extra receipts" assumes every dollar of tax from higher GDP funds the ABI; the AI receipts rule shares only half.*

### Why Stage 3 is not authorised

No rate passes Stage 3's stress-tested certificate on any modelled path, so the paper describes Stage 3 and does not authorise it (Table 6.48).

1. A levy on market income alone cannot fund it. On the headline path the most such a levy can raise in 2037-38 is A$455.0bn, at a rate of 40.4%, against a gap of A$583.3bn; above that rate, revenue falls.
2. If taxpayers respond twice as strongly as the PBO assumes (an elasticity of 0.4), no Stage 3 rise restores funding, and at the headline rise Stage 3 is A$290.0bn short in 2045-46.
3. In the combined downside no Stage 3 rise restores funding either: Stage 3 is A$218.4bn short in 2045-46.
4. With wages 2% lower, no Stage 3 rise in either form restores funding, because the rate is past the revenue peak.

The same holds on the lower reference path, so Stage 3 depends on AI-driven growth far above the Budget baseline (Table 6.42) or on broader tax bases.

### Sensitivity of the pathway's costs

For Stages 2 and 3 the taxpayer response matters most: halving it lowers the Stage 3 rise needed in 2037-38 from 24.1 to 21.0 points, and doubling it leaves no rise that works (Table 6.44).

### Testing the fiscal rules

On P\*'s central path the cash balance is worse than baseline in 7 of 18 years and net financial worth is below baseline until the 2028-29 borrowing is repaid in 2031-32 (Figure 6.3). Table 6.45 holds the rises fixed under other assumptions. A doubled taxpayer response leaves Stage 3 A$225.9bn short in 2045-46. Under the high scenario the cash balance is up to 4.57% of GDP worse than baseline by 2045-46, and net debt about 33 points of GDP higher (Table 6.46).

### A combined downside

If the five taxes yield 70% of their costings and the labour response is 0.29, Part 1's levy peaked at 7.65% of market income (A$566.4bn to 2045-46; Table 6.10), and P\*'s at 7.64% (A$570.1bn to 2045-46). With the taxpayer response also at 0.3 for the Stage 2 and 3 rises, no Stage 3 rise restores funding, and on the lower reference path Stage 2 needs 19.7 points (Tables 6.47 and 6.48). The PBO assumed that behaviour "would not materially change if the proposals were to be implemented as a package" [S133], which this scenario tests.

**Table 6.47. Combined behavioural downside: revenue measures at 70% of the PBO costings, taxpayer response 0.3 and labour response 0.29, lower reference path, 2028-29 to 2045-46 (A$ billion; percentage points)**

| Stage | Smallest funding less net cost at the legislated rises (A$ billion; year) | Legislated rise (points) | Restoring rise (points) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Stage 1 | −23.8 (2030-31) | 0.0 | 2.0 |
| Stage 2 | −73.2 (2036-37) | 14.8 | 19.7 |
| Stage 3 | −185.4 (2045-46) | 25.6 | beyond peak |

*Source: author's modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-22) [S5]. Note: the restoring rise is the uniform rise that would close the largest gap in the stage under the downside, by the same rounding rule as the legislated rises; 'beyond peak' means no rise does. Stage 1's shortfall is levied, not met by a rise.*

| Finding | Result | Status |
|---|---:|---:|

| Lower reference (no displacement): Stage 3 by a levy on market income alone, 2037-38 | most it raises A$469.7bn (at 40.4%) against a gap of A$533.1bn | Infeasible |

*Source: author's modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Tables F4-35 and F4-36) [S5]. Stage 3's certificate needs a rate that covers it both under the doubled taxpayer response and in the combined downside; on every path no rate does.*

### An AI displacement shock

In a moderate or severe AI shock the counter-cyclical rule holds the floor's and child payment's indexation once a displacement is declared, after the need test's youth unemployment leg has been met over eight consecutive quarters: 2036-37 (moderate) or 2034-35 (severe; Table 6.53). With no fund, the levy meets the gap. On Part 1's run, with no universal payment voted, it levies from 2035-36 or 2032-33 and reaches 2.37% or 6.17% of market income by 2045-46, as market income falls 7.5% or 19.4% (Tables 6.50 and 6.51). Under the wealth-tax fund option the fund would lend for about a year and the levy would reach 2.17% or 5.87%. These runs have not been repeated on P\*.

Those scenarios hold GDP and the GDP-scaled taxes at baseline, so Table 6.52 adds two variants [S134]: (a) GDP falls with the lost wages, and (b) the lost wages reappear as profits taxed by company tax and the excess profits tax. With no universal payment, Part 1's levy then reaches 3.21% to 6.30% of market income by 2045-46 in the severe case and 1.43% to 2.41% in the moderate case (Table 6.52). A shortfall remains in every variant, because displacement adds more to Stage 1's cost than the extra excess profits tax returns.

Table 6.49 starts Stage 2 in 2032-33, before its trigger could be met, as a stress test.

*Source: author's modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Tables F4-65, F4-78 and F4-116) [S5]. Note: the scenarios of Table 6.50, phased in from 2032-33, with GDP held at baseline. Stage 1 continues with no Stage 2 vote; rows after the year Stage 2 could start show the levy if no vote is held. A displacement is declared from 2036-37 (moderate) or 2034-35 (severe), once the need test's youth unemployment leg has been met over eight quarters; before then shortfalls are levied (Table 6.53). The fund then lends after the reserve, at the 10-year bond rate (4.13%), up to a quarter of its balance; the cap does not bind. The levy pays the loan's interest until the first year Stage 2 could start, then repays it over 10 years whether or not a vote is held. The memo column is the levy under the reserve-then-levy rule. The Part 1 alone rows have no fund, loan or dividend: kept surpluses (the reserve column) meet the gap before any levy, and their memo column is the levy with the wealth-tax fund's declaration timing (supplementary model_output.md, Tables F4-115 and F4-116). Without a fund to lend, Part 1 alone needs a larger levy, not an earlier one: in the severe case both levy from 2032-33, but Part 1 alone's reaches 1.96% of market income in 2034-35, when the fund lends, and 6.17% by 2045-46 against 5.87%; in the moderate case the fund levies first, and Part 1 alone's levy is 1.03% in 2036-37, when the fund lends, and 2.37% by 2045-46 against 2.17%. The levy includes never-cut top-ups before the guarantee lapses. Any dividend is not cut in cash terms before the lapse, and the floor and child payment keep their indexation.*

Note: the first eight rows are the wealth-tax fund's, on the loan rule the Act adopts; the last four are Part 1 alone's, with kept surpluses and then the levy and no fund to lend, and their ranges also span the midpoint variant. Part 1 alone needs the larger Stage 2 rise, 19.1 and 26.9 points against 18.6 and 25.5, because no fund dividend helps fund it (corrected in round 14: an earlier run funded it with the fund's dividend and advance) (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-115); with the reserve-then-levy rule as a memo, and a nil share of the excess profits tax. In (a) nominal GDP falls by the labour income lost (74.3% of the market income lost, ATO 2023-24) less the profit rise the paper's scenario assumes, and the five measures other than the excess profits tax fall with GDP; in (b) output is held and the lost labour income reappears as company profits, taxed at the effective company tax rate (26.2%) and, in entities in scope, by the excess profits tax. GST, franking and second-round demand effects are not modelled. The Stage 2 rise funds Stage 2 from the year it could start and repays the loan.*

Note: the need test's youth unemployment leg, 3 points above its pre-shock level, is assessed over eight consecutive quarters; it is met in October to December 2036 (moderate) and January to March 2035 (severe). The Act declares a displacement from the financial year containing that quarter. Before then shortfalls are levied, and the loan is repaid from the first year Stage 2 could start. On a one-quarter reading a non-AI spike, such as youth unemployment of 15.0% to 15.5% in 2020, would declare one.*

### The status quo, costed on the same basis

On the same basis, social security and welfare spending under current policy rises from A$315.2bn in 2027-28 to A$649.5bn in 2045-46, falling slowly as a share of GDP [S136, S139] (Table 6.54; Figure 6.4).

### Assumptions

Every assumption has a stated value and source. The two that most affect the results are the revenue measures' yield, which moves the levy, and the taxpayer response, which decides whether Stages 2 and 3 can be funded (Table 6.55).

**Table 6.55. Assumptions register, 2026-27 to 2045-46 (values as used in the model)**

| ID | Assumption | Value used | Source or basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| P1 | Recommended Stage 1 (P\*) | The payment floor (C1); the child payment rule E14 without its supplement-only schedule, with a supplement of A$6,750 a child in 2028-29, re-costed at Part 1's fiscal cost; Rent Assistance maximum rates +75% (C19); no dividend, wealth tax or fund; the five durable taxes, with kept surpluses drawn before any levy | Round 13 household model (model Table F4-117); P\* full fiscal path (option B model results, Tables B1 to B16) |
| P2 | Joint stress test | Five taxes' yield, labour, caseload and taxpayer responses, AI path and cost overruns drawn together from triangular distributions over the paper's tested ranges, with AI displacement in 15% of draws (an assumption); 4,000 paths | Model review |
| B1 | Stage designs and dates (22 October design) | Stage 1: the payment floor (C1) and child payment from 2028-29 (Part 1), continuing unless a later stage is triggered (D5); any Part 2 dividend from 2029-30, under the option Parliament chooses (F5, F6, F26 and F27). Stage 2: JobSeeker single basic rate A$824.90 a fortnight × 26 = A$21,447 (September 2026), from 2032-33 at the earliest. Stage 3: Age Pension single maximum basic rate A$1,135.40 × 26 = A$29,520, from 2037-38 at the earliest, legislated as an authority with no rate. All protected | Design brief; rates [S40, S126] |
| B18 | Stage 1 rate rule | Replaced by the fund rule (F5 and F6) | Table 9.3 |
| A1 | Start date of the original full-rate model | 1 July 2027 (original model only) | Earliest full year after legislation |
| A2 | Population growth to 2035-36 | 1.2% a year; reproduces 31.5 million in 2035-36 | Centre for Population projection [S180, S181] |
| B12 | Population growth after 2035-36 | 1.1% a year to 2039-40, then 1.0%; age drift halves after 2037-38 | Author's assumption at the upper end of official projections. Net overseas migration of 225,000 a year, the ABS medium assumption [S182], plus natural increase at its level in the year to March 2026 (100,600) [S178] would add about 1.0% a year to 31.5 million people. The Intergenerational Report 2026 projects 0.9% a year over 40 years, which, with 1.2% a year to 2035-36, implies about 0.8% a year after that [S30]. The higher path is cautious: the revenue measures grow with GDP, not population, so more people raise the tax rise needed. |
| A3 | Ageing of the population | Share aged 67+ rises 0.30 points a year; share aged 0–17 falls 0.15 points a year | ABS and AIHW [S143, S183] |
| A4 | Coverage | Citizens and permanent residents aged 18+; temporary visa holders (8.9% of population, 90% adults) excluded | Department of Home Affairs stock at 30 June 2026, as reported by a secondary source [S155]. The official dataset, Temporary visa holders in Australia [S184], was not reachable on 15 October 2026, so the figure could not be checked against it. The all-residents variant in Tables 6.44 and 6.45 shows the effect of including temporary residents |
| A5 | Take-up | 100% for lodgers; 80% for non-lodgers, who enrol with Services Australia, an assumption with no Australian precedent (95% and 98% tested overall) | Refundable offset with the tax return; Services Australia for non-lodgers after enrolment (F6) |
| A6 | Indexation (Stage 3 and child supplement) | Adult rate by wages (3.5% a year, proxy for the pension benchmark); child supplement by prices (2.5%) | Budget forecasts [S127] |
| B2 | Indexation (Stages 1 and 2) | Wages, 3.5% a year | Budget forecasts [S127] |
| A7 | Growth in taxable income | 3.5% a year from 2023-24; tax brackets not indexed | Budget forecasts [S127] |
| A8 | Base-rate share of Age Pension and DSP spending | 91.7% (A$1,135.40 ÷ A$1,237.70) | Department of Social Services rates [S3] |
| A9 | Base-rate share of carer income support | 49.4% (derived) | AIHW counts and Budget spending [S136, S144] |
| A10 | Growth in student payments after 2029-30 | 4% a year | Budget forward estimates trend [S136] |
| A11 | Administration savings | 20% of Services Australia departmental expenses from 2028-29, applied only to recipients fully replaced (B6); +2.5% a year after 2029-30 | Author's judgement. Social security made up 62% of the A$263.0bn Services Australia paid in 2024-25 [S185]. The ABI ends means testing only for people it fully replaces, and the agency keeps Medicare, child support, retained payments, top-ups and the ABI itself, so the author assumes about one-third of that share. Expense base from the Portfolio Budget Statements [S142]. |
| B6 | Administration savings with top-ups | A11 × share of recipients fully replaced | Top-ups still need administration |
| A12 | Status quo welfare growth after 2029-30 | 4.22% a year (growth of PBO program projections) | Author's derivation from PBO medium-term program lines [S139] |
| B3 | No-worse-off rule | Replace the lower of current payment and ABI; excess kept as a top-up | Design brief |
| B19 | Growth in protected recipient numbers | 5.47 million in 2028-29, 5.94 million in 2032-33, 6.57 million in 2037-38: a constant share of each age group, so numbers grow with the population and ageing | AIHW recipient counts [S143, S144] and A2, A3 |
| B4 | Spread of payments within each program | Share at full rate: Age Pension 0.67; JobSeeker 0.85 and Parenting Payment 0.55, calibrated; DSP and Carer Payment 0.90; students 0.80. The rest are spread evenly below the full rate | Age Pension share from AIHW [S143]. JobSeeker and Parenting Payment shares are calibrated so that Budget spending divided by AIHW recipient counts gives the average payment [S3, S144]. The same calibration gives 1.0 for DSP and students and 0.95 for Carer Payment, but the AIHW counts cover only people aged 16–64 and the student line includes ABSTUDY, so those are upper bounds. The author uses 0.90 and 0.80 to allow for part-rate recipients under the income, assets and parental income tests. |
| B5 | Recipient Protection Offset | Income tax on recipients' universal payment and retained payment offset; extended to tax on other income so that none pays more than under current rules, capped at the tax current rules levy on the payment (F14); exemption from the stage rate rises; all withdrawn at 50 cents a dollar above the phase-out point (C14) | Design brief; extension and phase-out added after the adversarial review |
| A15 | Other taxable income of protected recipients | Replaced by C8: inferred from where each recipient's payment sits on the income tests | The earlier assumption of nil other income hid a break in the no-worse-off rule (Table 9.3) |
| A16 | Income distribution | Lognormal curve fitted to ATO 2023-24 data; reproduces mean taxable income of A$77,673 against the ATO's A$78,127 | ATO taxation statistics [S171] |
| A17 | Low Income Tax Offset phase-out | A$700, reducing by 5 cents a dollar above A$37,500 and nil above A$66,667 (straight line from A$37,500 to A$66,667 in the model) | ATO [S186]; SuperGuide [S130] |
| – | Tax scale | 2026-27: 15% to A$45,000, 30%, 37% and 45% [S187]; 14% from 1 July 2027 (legislated, not yet on the ATO page); Medicare levy 2% | [S130, S131, S187] |
| MPE | Labour-supply response (marginal propensity to earn) | Market income falls 16 cents per dollar of net gain (low 0.05, high 0.29). The central value is the trial's individual-income estimate; the high value is close to its household-income estimate of about 28 cents; the low value reflects Alaska's universal, permanent dividend, which had no effect on employment, although part-time work rose 1.8 points | OpenResearch trial of low-income United States adults aged 21–40 [S82, S83]; Alaska study [S94]. Applied to all Australian market earners, which is a strong assumption |
| ETI | Taxpayer response to higher rates (elasticity of taxable income) | 0.2 in the standard constant-elasticity form, ln(M1/M0) = 0.2 × ln((1 − m − t)/(1 − m)), applied to all taxpayers (0.1 and 0.4 tested); also applied to the child payment's tapers (E12); the legislated rise is the larger of the aggregate and person-by-person forms (E16) | The PBO applies 0.2 only above A$180,000 [S140]; applying it to all incomes is a conservative departure |
| B8 | Tax change instruments | Uniform rise on all rates, or flat levy on market income | Modelling convention |
| B9 | Distribution method | Static first-round cash effect per adult, before price, rent, wage and interest-rate effects; child payment included, shared across adults in families that gain by the household model's shares (E14); household tables in Tables 6.22 to 6.32 | Standard incidence method |
| B20 | Incidence of revenue measures | Superannuation, wealth tax and interaction measures on decile 10; company, minerals, gas and fuel measures by decile in proportion to the PBO's estimate of capital gains tax discount savings (82% to decile 10). Table 6.17 tests half of the excess profits tax, and two-thirds of all company-type measures, borne through wages | Author's assumption, using PBO and Treasury evidence [S157, S158] |
| A14 | Timing of PBO costings (original model) | Year 1 = 2027-28, scaled for growth | PBO costings |
| B7 | Timing of PBO costings (staged design) | Year 1 = 2028-29, scaled by GDP growth (× 1.124); grown with GDP after the last PBO year, except the fuel-credit and coal-and-minerals measures, held flat in real terms (CPI growth): the fuel-credit measure because machinery and large vehicles keep fuel credits as light vehicles electrify, and the minerals measure as a conservative assumption, with resource export earnings forecast to ease on lower prices and falling thermal coal volumes (corrected in round 11; growth with GDP is the high case, Table 6.10); wealth tax nil after year 11 | PBO costings [S145, S146, S147, S148, S149, S150]; [S151, S152]; model Table F4-110 |
| A13 | Gas measure in the stack | PBO ECR-2025-3123: Petroleum Resource Rent Tax reform and a 10% royalty on offshore gas, PBO annual figures re-timed under B7 | [S148] |
| B17 | Gas measure after the last PBO year | Falls 11.2% a year, the rate of decline in the PBO's figures from 2030-31 to 2035-36, because the PBO assumes no new projects | Author's assumption, conservative [S148] |
| A19 | Overlap of the excess profits tax with other stack measures | Nil | The excess profits tax excludes coal, oil and gas and the main mining industries [S146]; the PBO's interaction analysis lists no interaction with other stack measures [S133] |
| A20 | Interaction deduction | The PBO's full personal income tax interaction line (−A$920m to 2028-29; −A$16,870m to 2035-36), re-timed year by year on the PBO's profile and extended past 2035-36 with nominal GDP | Conservative: the line also covers a measure not in the stack [S133] |
| B21 | Implementation and departmental costs | Build A$300m (2027-28 and 2028-29); running A$50m a year from 2028-29, +2.5% a year; oversight A$10m a year; trial, evaluation and Community Contribution Fund as in Table 6.2. The 2027-28 cost of A$210m is reprioritised within Services Australia's departmental appropriation, from Program 1.3, Technology and Transformation (A$1,318.2m in 2027-28; Portfolio Budget Statements, Table 2.1.1) | Author's estimates [S141, S142] |
| A18 | Productivity Commission AI path | 4.3% added to GDP by 2035-36, phased in evenly | [S32, S188, S189] |
| B10 | AI receipts rule (Section 5; Tables 6.41 to 6.43) | Half of tax receipts above the Budget baseline count toward any Stage 2 or 3 rise; AI receipts are the excess profits and minerals taxes plus that half; paths of 2.0% and 3.0% a year against 1.2%, from 2026-27, and a late path of 2.0% from 2036-37 only | Design brief; Budget and Intergenerational Report, whose 1.2% assumption AI is expected to support [S30, S127] |
| B22 | Receipts-to-GDP ratio applied to extra GDP | Budget ratios to 2029-30 (25.4% in 2028-29), then a straight line to 27.0% from 2036-37 | Budget forward estimates and medium-term projection [S61] |
| B11 | Nominal GDP | Derived from Budget ratios to 2029-30; 4.97% a year from 2030-31 | Author's derivation [S61, S139] |
| B23 | Accounting basis | Underlying cash balance, consistent with Budget Paper No. 1; each PBO measure taken from its underlying cash line; net cost excludes public debt interest, shown as a memo item | [S61]; PBO practice |
| B24 | Rate-rise revenue | 1% of taxable income (including the ABI, protected recipients excluded) per point, less the taxpayer response; the per-point method reproduces PBO ECR-2025-2525 (A$7.4bn in 2028-29). No official costing of a uniform rise in every rate exists; Recommendations 12d and 12e ask the PBO to cost the legislated rises before each stage's enabling vote | [S154]; Table 6.39 |
| B13 | Underlying cash balance baseline | Published Budget years and medium-term projections used; straight line from 2030-31 to 2033-34; held at the final medium-term projection (2036-37) after that | [S61] |
| B14 | Net debt baseline | Rolled forward from the Budget's net debt at 30 June 2030 and calibrated to the Budget's medium-term net debt projection for 30 June 2037 | [S61] |
| B15 | Interest on extra debt | 4.13% (PBO interest ÷ gross debt, 2026-27) | [S61, S139] |
| B16 | Stage tax rule | One rise per stage: the largest need in any year of the stage, including implementation costs, rounded up to 0.1 of a point (14.8 and 25.6 on the earliest-dates path in the aggregate form used by the costing tables, and 25.8 in the per-person form the Act would legislate; levy alternative 20.2% at Stage 2, none possible at Stage 3). Revenue in every table is calculated at the rounded-up rate | Fiscal rule test; Table 6.37 |
| – | Child supplement | FTB Part A maximum under-13 rate × 26 + annual supplement; A$7,452 in 2028-29 | [S128] |
| – | Temporary residents (coverage variant) | All residents included | Sensitivity only |
| C1 | Payment floor | A$569.81 a week (Melbourne Institute poverty line, single adult not in the workforce, March quarter 2026), indexed to wages: A$32,570 a year in 2028-29 | [S4] |
| C2 | Earlier-edition variant of the floor | A$536.40 a week at June quarter 2025, wage-indexed (A$550.42 at March quarter 2026) | Melbourne Institute, June quarter 2025 edition (model Table C0) [S5] |
| C3 | Minimum universal payment | If the funded payment were below A$3,000, the floor would be set to what the measures fund at A$3,000; the rule did not bind | Author's rule |
