Note: identifiers follow the model: A-numbers come from the original full-rate model, B-numbers from the staged design, and C- to F-numbers from the revisions of October 2026. MPE is the marginal propensity to earn and ETI the elasticity of taxable income; DSP = Disability Support Pension; FTB = Family Tax Benefit; AIHW = Australian Institute of Health and Welfare; ATO = Australian Taxation Office.*

## 7. Implementation pathway

Stage 1 can start on 1 July 2028 if Parliament passes the main laws by December 2027 and the payment systems are built and tested in the first half of 2028. A universal payment could follow only under a later Act, introduced after the need test is met, the trial has reported within its work-effort cap and the PBO has certified the funding. The binding safeguards in Table 9.6 then correct Stage 1's funding without further legislation.

### Phases and gates

Figure 7.1 draws the seven phases of Stage 1 and its tests, their owners and 17 numbered gates and milestones from Tables 7.1, 7.2, 9.1 and 9.6, with a dashed row for any universal payment, which would need a later Act. Each of the first three phases must pass its gate before the next starts.

**Figure 7.1 [TIMELINE]. Implementation roadmap: phases, owners and gates, November 2026 to 2045-46**

![Figure 7.1: implementation roadmap, Gantt chart with numbered gates](charts/fig7_1_roadmap.png)

*Source: author's proposal. Data: charts/data/fig7_1_roadmap.csv and fig7_1_gates.csv [S5]. Note: the seven phases are the inquiry and consultation, legislation, build and test, Stage 1 (P\*) from 1 July 2028, the randomised trial from July 2029 to June 2031, quarterly need-test reporting to 30 June 2043, when the pathway's tests lapse unless renewed, and the PBO's yearly certificate of the five taxes from 2029-30. The dashed row shows that any universal payment would need a later Act, not before 1 July 2032 and only after the need test, the trial and the PBO certificate are all met; it is not scheduled.*

### Legislation to enact or amend

Seven new Acts are needed for Stage 1: the ABI Act, a Basic Income Levy Act, an imposition Act and an assessment Act for each of the two new profits taxes, and an offshore gas royalty Act. No Act sets a universal rate or a dividend. Eight existing Acts are amended, chiefly the *Social Security Act 1991*, which sets the floor and the Rent Assistance rise.

**Table 7.1. Legislation to enact or amend, 2027 to 2032**

| Legislation | Change | Timing |
|---|---|---|
| *Australian Basic Income Act 2027* (new) | Stage 1 (P\*): the payment floor, the child payment and the Rent Assistance rise, through amendments of the Acts below; the cash guarantee, with protected status defined as receipt of, or eligibility under the income and assets tests for, a payment Stage 1 changes; the pathway's three tests: the AI-specific need test, the randomised trial and its work-effort cap, and the PBO certificate, stress-tested for any payment at the Age Pension rate; a bar on introducing any Bill that sets a universal rate until all three are met; the AI receipts rule; the counter-cyclical rule for Stage 1; the Basic Income Levy formula and the rule that kept surpluses are drawn before any levy; the rent safeguard; a human decision before any debt is raised from matched data; review clauses, with the pathway's tests lapsing on 30 June 2043 unless renewed. It sets no universal rate and no dividend | Pass by 31 December 2027 |
| *Social Security Act 1991* | Sets the payment floor of A$569.81 a week, indexed to wages, for JobSeeker, Youth Allowance, Austudy, the ABSTUDY living allowance, Parenting Payment and Special Benefit, with couple rates in proportion and the existing income and activity tests; raises the maximum rates of Commonwealth Rent Assistance by 75%; re-links family Rent Assistance and Health Care Card eligibility from FTB Part A to the child payment's FTB-equivalent amount | 1 July 2028 |
| *Social Security (Administration) Act 1999* | A human decision before any debt is raised from matched data | 1 July 2028 |
| *A New Tax System (Family Assistance) Act 1999* | Replaces FTB Parts A and B with the child payment (Table 5.6): one schedule withdrawn at 18 cents a dollar above A$72,631 (the taper set by regulation), assessed on the latest assessed taxable income, re-assessed on the resident parent's own income after a separation, with an income-fall review after a birth or job loss reconciled only up to what it added; keeps FTB's maintenance income and action tests, shared-care rules, immunisation and healthy-start requirements and one-year waiting period for new residents; keeps current entitlements, indexed as current rules would index them, where higher | 1 July 2028 |
| *Basic Income Levy Act 2027* (new; modelled on the *Medicare Levy Act 1986*) | Applies to a shortfall of the five durable taxes after any kept surpluses are drawn: each year's surplus is kept as debt reduction at the debt rate and drawn before any levy (first levy expected, on central yields, from 2043-44, with a 37% to 59% chance of one before 2034-35 in the joint stress test); the levy is set each year to the gap the PBO projects, never above it, unless Parliament legislates another measure within six months; legal advice on sections 53 to 55 of the Constitution before drafting | Pass by 31 December 2027; rate nil until needed |
| *Excess Profits Tax (Imposition) Act 2027* and *Excess Profits Tax Assessment Act 2027* (new) | 40% tax on excess profits of companies with turnover above A$100 million, excluding coal, oil and gas and the main mining industries | Pass by 31 December 2027; start 1 July 2028 |
| *Minerals Super-Profits Tax (Imposition) Act 2027* and *Minerals Super-Profits Tax Assessment Act 2027* (new) | 40% tax on super-profits of coal and specified mining projects | Pass by 31 December 2027; start 1 July 2028 |
| *Petroleum Resource Rent Tax Assessment Act 1987* (amend; the Petroleum Resource Rent Tax, PRRT) | Immediate deduction of pre-start expenditure with no carry-forward; 15-year write-off of later spending; four onshore projects brought in | 1 July 2028 |
| *Offshore Gas Royalty Act 2027* (new) | 10% royalty on offshore projects subject to PRRT, except the North West Shelf; creditable against PRRT | Pass by 31 December 2027; start 1 July 2028 |
| *Fuel Tax Act 2006* and *Income Tax Assessment Act 1997* (amend) | End fuel tax credits outside agriculture; end accelerated depreciation and immediate exploration deductions for coal, oil and gas | 1 July 2028 |
| *Parliamentary Service Act 1999* (Part 7, which sets up the PBO) | Gives the PBO a duty to certify each year the five durable taxes against Stage 1's costs, to cost afresh and certify the funding of any universal rate proposed after the need test and the trial, under a stress test for a payment at the Age Pension rate, and to report every year on funding against net cost | Pass by 31 December 2027 |
| *Charter of Budget Honesty Act 1998* | Requires each Budget and Intergenerational Report to report Stage 1's costs, the kept surpluses and any levy, the need-test indicators and household poverty after housing costs, including the recipient households worse off after housing costs | Pass by 31 December 2027 |
| Later Acts, only if Parliament chooses | A universal payment (its rate, start date, protections and tax rise), only after the three tests are met; any dividend (Section 10), with a Fund Act for a fund option and the two Net Wealth Tax Acts for the wealth-tax fund | A universal payment not before 1 July 2032; a dividend only after a PBO costing |

*Source: author's proposal.*

### Responsible agencies

Treasury and the Department of Social Services (DSS) lead, and Services Australia and the Australian Taxation Office (ATO) deliver. JSA and the ABS publish the safeguard indicators, and the PBO, PC and Australian National Audit Office (ANAO) check (Table 7.2).

**Table 7.2. Responsible agencies and deliverables, 2027 to 2041**

| Agency | Role | Key deliverable and date |
|---|---|---|
| Treasury | Leads the ABI Act and revenue measures; reports fiscal effects in each Budget, including the kept surpluses and any levy; models couples' labour supply before the Bill, and regional effects, incidence by age and consumer incidence of the fuel and gas measures | Couples' labour supply by 30 June 2027, before the exposure drafts (July 2027); other modelling by 31 December 2031 |
| Department of Social Services (DSS) | Owns Stage 1 policy, the child payment and the cash guarantee; sets the child payment's taper by regulation after the couples' modelling; runs an open tender for the independent trial evaluator | Policy guidelines by March 2028; trial contract with a university consortium by June 2029 |
| Services Australia | Pays the floor, the child payment and Rent Assistance; reports take-up; applies the waiting periods | Systems live 1 July 2028 |
| Australian Taxation Office (ATO) | Administers the five durable taxes; supplies the assessed income on which the child payment is paid | No new withholding schedules for Stage 1 |
| Parliamentary Budget Office (PBO) | Full costing of P\* and the targeted package; certifies the five durable taxes against Stage 1's costs each year, and the funding of any universal rate proposed after the tests, under a stress test for a payment at the Age Pension rate | Costing by 31 August 2027; first certificate in 2029-30, then each year; a certificate before any Bill setting a universal rate |
| Jobs and Skills Australia (JSA) | Publishes the AI-specific need-test series: employment of 22 to 29 year olds in the most and least AI-exposed occupations | Series by 30 June 2028; quarterly reports |
| Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) | Supplies trigger and evaluation data; links records for evaluation | Quarterly productivity and labour data |
| Department of Finance | Reports Stage 1's effect on the cash balance, net debt and net financial worth | Each Budget from 2028-29 |
| Productivity Commission | Five-yearly independent review | 2031, 2036, 2041 |
| Australian National Audit Office (ANAO) | Audits delivery and the cash guarantee | 2029-30, then every three years |
| National Indigenous Australians Agency (NIAA), with the Coalition of Peaks | Shared decision-making on remote and First Nations delivery, kinship carers and enrolment without a tax file number; advice on whether existing income-management arrangements extend to the floor | Community forums February to May 2027; delivery principles by 31 May 2027, before the exposure drafts; agreement by March 2028 |

*Source: author's proposal.*

### Interaction with existing payments, tax and superannuation

Stage 1 never reduces a retained payment. Pensions, carer payments and supplements, Commonwealth Rent Assistance (which rises), Remote Area Allowance, the National Disability Insurance Scheme (NDIS) and Paid Parental Leave all continue. The lifted payments keep their income tests, including the partner income test, and the activity test, so the floor tapers away as earnings rise. The child payment takes over Family Tax Benefit Parts A and B; Child Support is unchanged.

The tax scale is unchanged, so Stage 1 needs no new withholding schedules. Rent Assistance and the Health Care Card for families hang off FTB Part A, which is abolished, so Table 7.1 re-links them to the child payment. If states count higher payments as income for public housing rents, public tenants lose part of their gain, so Section 9 proposes an agreement on rents and concessions.

### Transition protections

Every person affected by Stage 1 keeps these protections.

1. Every person receiving a payment Stage 1 changes on 30 June 2028, and every later claimant who meets its tests, is paid at least what current rules would pay, including current FTB. This protects payments, not income after housing costs (Table 6.21).
2. Concession cards and payment-linked entitlements continue for current holders, and the links in Table 7.1 extend them to new families.
3. Protected top-ups are indexed under current rules, so they do not erode.
4. No social security debt or protection clawback is raised from matched data unless a human officer has checked it, applying the Royal Commission's lessons on automated decisions [S120].
5. Every person affected receives a written statement of their floor, child payment or Rent Assistance change before the first payment.

### Data and systems

Stage 1 runs on existing payment, tax and myGov systems: payments go to the bank account people already use for Centrelink, and the child payment uses ATO assessed income. Adults without a tax file number, bank account or myGov access, many in remote communities, need assisted claims designed with First Nations organisations (Table 7.2). The A$300m build estimate is weak, because the system must also calculate current-rules entitlements for every protected person and family, so Recommendation 12f asks Services Australia for an agency costing before the Bill.

## 8. Risks, objections and responses

Most risks are met by a test before any universal step and a binding safeguard once Stage 1 starts. Two are not: the five taxes may fall short sooner than the central path shows, and no rate rise would fund a payment at the Age Pension rate.

### Risk register

Of the 21 risks in Table 8.1, the three that could most change the funding are a shortfall in the five taxes, a stronger taxpayer response than the PBO assumes, and early AI displacement.

**Table 8.1. Risk register, 2028 to 2045 (likelihood and impact rated low, medium or high)**

| No. | Risk | Likelihood | Impact | Mitigation | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Revenue yield. The levy moves with the five durable taxes. ### The strongest objections, answered

The objections are ordered by weight: the three that decide whether the recommendation stands come first.

**Objection 1. Why legislate any universal pathway?** If a universal basic income will probably never pass its own tests, why put any of it in law? There is no aggregate displacement yet [S29, S226, S227]. On the trial's latest estimate the work-effort cap would stop a universal payment with probability 0.79 to 0.84. A payment at the Age Pension rate would cost 12.2% of GDP and cannot be funded from income tax on the paper's central assumptions (Tables 6.35 and 6.48), and every costing of a full basic income the paper has found agrees on its scale [S57, S116, S230, S228].

The objection is right that the pathway will probably not be used, and the paper does not pretend otherwise. The case for legislating the tests is insurance against a shock whose probability no one can yet estimate. If AI cut demand for labour across the economy, most people would qualify for means-tested payments, so targeting would stop saving money and steep tapers would fall on nearly everyone; a universal payment would then be the simplest way to share the gains. The tests take years to build: JSA's need-test series, a two-year trial and a PBO method for certifying a rate. It is far easier to agree them before a crisis than during one. The premium is small, about A$120m a year for the two trial years and about A$10m a year for the indicators and certificates (Table 6.2), and the pathway pays nobody and sets no rate, so there is no contingent tax rise for high earners to anticipate. If the tests are never met, the money buys evidence the country would want anyway. The pathway may never be used, and that is an acceptable outcome for insurance.

Cheaper forms of insurance target AI job loss directly: wage insurance [S231], and unemployment insurance that grows more generous automatically when unemployment rises. They complement the pathway, and the committee could add either to Stage 1.

**Objection 2. Why not the targeted package?** It cuts poverty most: 9.0% against 10.5% for P\*, with 0.40 million fewer people in poverty, and less poverty in every group on every robustness run (Table 5.7). The World Bank found existing programs about 60% more effective than a budget-neutral basic income [S105], and the OECD found one would raise poverty [S114, S228].

On poverty the objection holds, and on most other measures too. Preferring P\* to the package is a value judgement, and the paper states its price on every method it uses. The package leaves about 0.40 million fewer people in poverty (1.5 points). On the paper's method it is A$2.0bn to A$4.3bn a year ahead of P\*; on the complete accounting it is between A$1.9bn a year behind and A$2.3bn ahead; with the Green Book's weights it is about A$10.7bn a year ahead. It breaks even at lower distributional weights: 1.3 to 2.4 on the paper's method and 2.0 to 3.8 on the complete accounting, against P\*'s 1.5 to 3.3 and 2.3 to 4.9 (Table 6.12, provisional). On average the package also treats working families better: second earners' mean participation tax rate is 28.9% against 33.0% under P\*, 22.4% of second earners face 40% or more against 31.6%, and 21.8% of higher earners in families with children face an effective marginal tax rate of 50% or more against 41.6% (Table 5.7). P\*'s work-incentive advantage is only at the extreme: 1.3% of higher earners face 60% or more under P\* against 14.2% under the package, and no second earner faces 60% or more against 6.8%. The paper's second reason is the fiscal path. The package's costs grow faster, so it needs A$108.1bn of levies to 2045-46 against A$36.5bn for P\*, and a levy before 2044-45 is more likely, 87% against 74% in the joint stress test (Tables 6.6 and 6.9; model results Table B5; provisional). The paper judges that avoiding those very high rates and the larger levy is worth the price. Parliament may reasonably judge otherwise, and should choose the package if it weights poverty above them. Both fit the same Act, and Parliament can legislate either as Stage 1 or add the package's earned-income credit to P\* later.

**Objection 3. Who pays?** Stage 1 is paid for by five taxes, by borrowing in its first year and, from the late 2030s, by a levy on market income. Its gains are headlined, its costs are not.

The objection is fair, and the paper now leads with the result after the taxes: 35.4% of households are better off under P\* and 64.6% worse off on the central allocation, or about 59% to 67% across the incidence variants (Table 6.14). The five taxes fall mostly on the top income decile, but the fuel and company taxes reach lower deciles too, and if company-type taxes fall partly on wages more households lose (Tables 6.15 and 6.17). The first year is borrowed, not funded: A$20.7bn in 2028-29 (provisional), repaid by 2031-32, or nil with a phased start (Tables 6.6 and 6.7). From 2037-38 the five taxes fall short each year, and a joint stress test gives a 48% to 74% chance of a levy before 2044-45 and 37% to 59% before 2034-35 (Table 6.9, provisional). Stage 1 needs no rise in income-tax rates, but it does need that levy, and the paper does not claim otherwise.

**Objection 4. It costs too much for what it delivers.** A universal payment at the Age Pension rate would need every income-tax rate 30.9 points higher; a lower-rate design modelled by Spies-Butcher, Phillips and Henderson also needed about 12 more points [S57]. A levy on market income alone cannot fund it, and under a doubled taxpayer response or the combined downside no rate rise does. Even Stage 1's monetised net benefit is negative on the complete accounting.

The objection is right on the monetised measures and on a universal payment, which is why the paper legislates no universal rate and does not authorise Stage 3. Stage 1's net benefit on the complete accounting is negative for every design: P\* −A$23.3bn to −A$2.3bn a year in 2028-29, the targeted package −A$25.2bn to nil, and Part 1 −A$24.8bn to −A$4.1bn, or −A$32.5bn to −A$5.7bn with its borrowing charged (Table 6.12). The case for Stage 1 is that it cuts poverty after housing costs from 14.2% to 10.5%; among the designs of equal cost the paper compares, P\* is its choice, weighing poverty against work incentives and fiscal sustainability.

**Objection 5. People will stop working, and income should be earned.** Only 38% of Australians support paying everyone the same amount regardless of work [S43]. OpenResearch found participation fell 3.9 points [S82] and hours by 1.3 a week [S83], and the Coronavirus Supplement cut job-finding by 19% [S113]. The child payment's tapers raise a second earner's participation tax rate to 38% on Part 1's run, against as little as 12% today (Table 5.4).

Stage 1 pays no one regardless of work: the floor keeps JobSeeker's income and activity tests. Most trials find small effects on work. In Germany about 90% of recipients kept working about 40 hours a week [S74], Alaska's dividend had no effect on employment [S94], and Kenya's long-term basic income did not reduce household hours [S81]. The second-earner point stands, and the taper is set by regulation so that couples' labour-supply modelling, due before the Bill, can change it (Recommendation 13). A trial fall in participation above 3 points rules out a universal payment under the Act (Table 9.6). At the trial's latest 3.9-point estimate that would happen with probability 0.79 to 0.84.

**Objection 6. Raising JobSeeker would achieve most of the gain at a fraction of the cost.** ACOSS calls for at least A$600 a week [S197], and in 2023 the Business Council of Australia's chief executive backed a staged rise in JobSeeker [S138]. Raising JobSeeker to about 90% of the pension has been costed at about A$6bn a year [S232].

Largely accepted: Stage 1 now does this and more. It raises the working-age payments to the poverty line, at A$19.9bn in 2028-29 with the caseload response; with automatic enrolment that is the floor-first option (A$25.0bn; poverty 12.6%), which the committee can choose (Table 5.11). P\* adds the child payment and the Rent Assistance rise because, at the same budget, they cut poverty further than any universal element.

**Objection 7. The extra spending will raise prices, interest rates and rents**, because it moves income from people who save to people who spend. A Mexican study found cash raised local prices compared with in-kind support [S107], and the RBA estimated that low-income households spent about 0.9 of a one-off payment on non-durables, against 0.3 to 0.7 for all households [S112]. On Part 1's run, if recipients spend 0.3 to 0.6 more of each dollar than those who pay, Stage 1 adds A$13.4bn to A$26.9bn of demand. The Section 6 scenario uses A$20bn to A$40bn, so it errs on the high side.

In that scenario about 17% of Stage 1's household gains are lost to higher repayments and rents, and poverty is 11.6% to 12.0% against 11.3% first-round on Part 1's run (Tables 6.31 and 6.32). It is also why some recipients are worse off after housing costs (Table 6.21). P\* raises Rent Assistance 75%, Stage 1 is legislated well ahead so the RBA can plan, and the rent safeguard raises Rent Assistance further if rents outpace wages (Table 9.6).

### The red teams' objections and the external critique

Tables 8.2 to 8.5 record how the 22 October paper answered the third to sixth red teams [S135, S191, S192, S196]; the revision history annex records the rest [S198]. Several of those answers concern the dividend, which this revision no longer recommends (Section 10), and the answers are kept as the record of that paper. Table 8.6 answers the external critique of 30 October 2026, which led to this revision. Across the reviews these points still stand against the design:

- every Stage 1 design has a negative monetised net benefit on the complete accounting, which also counts Stage 1's own behavioural costs;
- the first year is borrowed, the five taxes fall short each year from the late 2030s, and a levy may come well before the central date (Table 6.9);
- the guarantee protects payments, not income after housing costs (Table 6.21);
- a universal payment at the Age Pension rate cannot be certified, and on central labour evidence a payment at the JobSeeker rate is not the expected outcome;
- prior-year assessment still leaves some debts;
- any dividend fund would rest on early wealth-tax receipts that the tax's later losses take back (Section 10).

**Table 8.2. The third red team's 14 objections, ranked by the red team, and how this revision answers each (21 October 2026)**

| Rank | Objection | Answer | Type of answer | Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The A$2,800 dividend was set by an arbitrary end date; the Act's rolling certification would cut it in cash terms, and a fixed date gives a cliff | Accepted. The rule is replaced by a permanent fund that spends only its expected real return, so there is no end date and no cliff, and the dividend is not cut in cash terms until the guarantee lapses in 2041-42; it is much smaller (A$47 in 2029-30, A$461 in 2045-46) and is now one of four Part 2 options left to Parliament. The red team's further point is conceded: Stage 1 needs new revenue, a levy from 2044-45 on central yields for the floor and child payment alone, or from 2039-40, up to 0.41% of market income, with the wealth-tax fund | Design change and concession | Sections 1, 5 and 6; Tables 10.8 to 10.13 |
| 2 | The fund was the wealth tax: without it no dividend, and losing its first year ended the dividend | Partly conceded: the fund's capital is still mostly early wealth-tax receipts, and on the high reading of its losses the tax raises about A$8.5bn net in present value over its life (Table 10.22). The wealth tax now funds only the wealth-tax fund, so losing its first year cuts the first dividend to A$28, and the floor is never paused; the Budget-paid dividend pays the same dividend without the wealth tax | Concession and design change | Sections 5 and 6; Table 10.19 |
| 3 | 'No income-tax rise' was false: the threshold cut raised A$19.5bn, and workers funded most of their own dividend | Accepted: the threshold cut is withdrawn; the threshold and LITO are unchanged and the dividend is taxable, so Stage 1 changes neither rates nor the tax scale | Design change | Section 5; Table 10.1 |
| 4 | The rule was pro-cyclical: a displacement would cut the dividend by 85% or pause the floor before Stage 2 could be voted | Accepted: a counter-cyclical rule holds any dividend in cash terms and the floor's indexation and, under a fund option, once a displacement is declared after eight quarters, the reserve and then a loan from the fund meet the gap until Stage 2 could start, about a year, so the levy pays only interest; the resilience rating is lowered to moderate | Design change and concession | Sections 5 and 6; Tables 5.9 and 6.51 |
| 5 | Prior-year assessment kept estimate debts for separating, new and displaced parents; the lag is longer than a year, and the review was uncosted | Conceded and redesigned: the 1.5-year lag is costed; separating parents are re-assessed on their own income with no debt; reviews after a birth or job loss leave about 49,500 debts a year; the review costs A$0.8bn to A$1.2bn a year and is now in net cost; the executive summary's claim is qualified | Concession, design change and analysis | Sections 1, 5 and 7; Table 5.4 |
| 6 | The equal-budget test was unfair to the ABI, but without the dividend Stage 1 reaches the same poverty rate, so the dividend buys coverage funded by earners | Accepted on both points: charged its own responses, the targeted package reaches 8.9%, a price of 2.4 points rather than 2.7; Stage 1 reaches 11.3% with or without a dividend; no dividend option is funded by a cut to the tax-free threshold, and the resilience and rent-share claims are narrowed | Analysis and concession | Section 5; Tables 5.9, 10.4 and 5.12 |

| 11 | Mutual obligation at the floor makes Objective 3's 100% unattainable | Accepted: the indicator now measures the floor as a maximum rate, suspensions are reported, and Parliament is asked whether a suspension may take a payment below the floor | Concession and question for Parliament | Section 2; Table 2.2; Recommendation 6 |

| 6 | "Covers the floor to 2040-41" is a target the 6% share is tuned to meet | Conceded, and after the fifth red team no share meets the rule: it falls to nil, and the first levy, in 2039-40, is reported as a result, not a target | Concession and analysis | Sections 1 and 6 |
| 7 | Readiness for displacement reaches mainly singles and low-income couples | Accepted: the floor keeps JobSeeker's partner income test, so partnered workers whose partner earns above its cut-out get no floor: 49% of partnered earners, and 47% in the clerical earnings band (model Table F4-71) | Concession | Sections 1, 3 and 5; Tables 5.9 and 8.1 |
| 8 | "Never cut in cash terms" and "capital never spent" are statutory defaults that Alaska broke | Accepted: both are described as statutory rules a later Parliament can change; Alaska's 2016 halving, the 2017 ruling and the reset Future Fund drawdown date are in Risk 11; any draw on the fund needs a separate Act | Concession and design change | Tables 4.1, 7.1, 8.1 and 9.1 |
| 9 | The central return is the mandate's floor, and the payout keeps the fund's real value only in expectation | Accepted: "in expectation" throughout; at the central return the fund ends below its real inflows in 53% to 55% of return paths (Table 10.13) | Concession and analysis | Sections 5 and 6; Table 10.13 |
| 10 | The household model pays the child payment on current income, though the design pays on income 1.5 years old | Conceded; a crude test bounds child poverty with the lag at 7.2% to 7.3% on the relative line (Table 6.24); the review's cost is now in net cost | Concession | Sections 5 and 6 |
| 11 | The Stage 1 evaluation identifies only the dividend | Accepted: the floor and child payment start in Services Australia regions in a random order over July to December 2028, with arrears from 1 July, so later regions are a control group | Design change | Section 9; Recommendation 8 |
| 12 | Second earners' decisions to stop work are uncosted | Conceded: the couples' modelling due by 30 June 2027 will cost them in the net cost; about 20,000 exits would cost about A$0.3bn a year | Concession | Section 5; Recommendation 13 |

*Source: red team review R8 [S135]; author's responses. Note: the red team's 11 errors are corrected: Table 9.5's targets and baseline (errors 1 to 3), Alaska's 2022 energy relief (4), the wealth tax's 2033-34 rounding, disclosed in Table 6.4 (5), the caseload cost (6), the combined downside's taxpayer response (7), the PBO's wealth-tax caveat (9), the single worker's loss band (10) and "every eligible adult" (11); error 8, the dividend's A$0.9bn price, is replaced by its financing, A$26.6bn in 2028-29 with a deadweight cost of A$6.6bn to A$26.4bn (Table 10.6).*

**Table 8.4. The fifth red team's six objections, ranked by the red team, and how this revision answers each (21 October 2026)**

| Rank | Objection | Answer | Type of answer | Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The wealth tax's interaction line rises as the tax falls and outlasts it; the model spread it evenly, under-extended it and charged it to the floor's account from 2039-40, so over its life the tax raises roughly nothing net | Accepted: the model now uses the PBO's year-by-year line, extends it as other measures are and charges it to the fund in every year, so the ring-fence holds in every year. Conceded that on the high reading of its losses the wealth tax raises about A$8.5bn net in present value at 6.5% over its life, and nothing at the debt rate, so the fund's stake rests on early wealth-tax receipts that later losses take back; the never-cut guarantee now lapses in 2041-42, after A$1.5bn of levied top-ups, and the line, charged to the fund's capital, exhausts it in 2083-84 (Tables 10.23 and 10.24). The dividend is no longer recommended: Part 2 is set out as options, including the Budget-paid dividend, with no wealth tax | Design change, analysis and concession | Sections 1, 5 and 6; Tables 6.4, 10.8 and 10.22 |
| 2 | The 16-cent labour response is applied to the dividend but not to the child payment, and student payments (up 73%) and Parenting Payment (up 13%) have no claims response | Accepted: the model now costs the child payment's income effect (A$1.7bn in 2028-29) and a claims response for students and Parenting Payment (A$1.0bn); Stage 1's net cost in 2028-29 becomes A$62.0bn rather than A$59.2bn, and with the dividend the first levy moves to 2039-40 | Analysis | Section 6; Tables 10.9 and 6.55 |
| 3 | "Needs no claim", "paid fortnightly" and "no adult is worse off" overstate delivery: the dividend is annual, non-lodgers must enrol, and a taxable dividend can cross the surcharge and LISTO cliffs | Accepted: the dividend is paid once a year, at assessment for lodgers and by Services Australia after enrolment for non-lodgers (take-up 80%, an assumption, so about 305,000 to 334,000 adults a year miss out); it is excluded from every adjusted-taxable-income test, including the Medicare levy surcharge and LISTO (sixth red team), so no adult is worse off, keeping at least 53.0 cents of each dollar | Design change and wording | Sections 1, 4, 5 and 7; Recommendations 4 and 7 |
| 4 | The displacement loan bridges only two to three years, and the coded rule (repay from the first year Stage 2 could start) is not the written rule (repay after a vote) | Accepted: the Act now follows the model, repaying from the first year Stage 2 could start whether or not a vote is held; conceded that the loan is a short bridge, about a year once a displacement is declared after eight quarters, after which the levy meets the gap: with no Stage 2 vote, 2.17% of market income by 2045-46 in a moderate and 5.87% in a severe displacement (Table 6.51) | Design change and concession | Sections 1, 5, 6 and 9; Tables 6.51 and 9.6; Recommendation 5d |
| 5 | The fund's earnings are kept outside the cash balance on a precedent whose rationale does not transfer: Finance brings Future Fund earnings in once payments begin | Accepted: the treatment is stated as unsettled; counting the drawdown, the cash balance is A$4.2bn worse than baseline in 2045-46, and counting all the fund's earnings it is A$4.0bn better (Table 10.14); Recommendation 12c asks the PBO to settle it | Concession and analysis | Section 6; Tables 10.8 and 10.14; Recommendation 12c |
| 6 | Legislating the Stage 2 and 3 authorities now adds nothing a later Act could not, and a legislated contingent rise may be anticipated | Partly accepted: the authorities save drafting and passing a new Act after a trigger and give the trial a defined rate; the anticipation risk is conceded and not costed | Concession | Section 5 |

Note: the red team's nine errors are corrected: Table 4.3's "fortnightly" (error 1); the ring-fence statements and the interaction line's profile and extension (errors 2 and 3, in the model); the smallest share of the dividend kept, now with the dividend outside the surcharge and LISTO tests (4); the 16-cent response (5, in the model); the loan rule (6); stale gross wealth-tax figures (7); the shortened Alaska quote (8); and the excess profits tax's source (9). Of the claims the red team could not confirm, the DEWR employment figures are confirmed in DEWR's report; the Intergenerational Report's 1.5% to 2.0% AI upside and its quoted phrase could not be found in the report's published summary and are removed.*

## 9. Conclusion and recommendations

Parliament should legislate a targeted floor in 2027 and start it on 1 July 2028: the payment floor at the poverty line, a child payment without a middle-income supplement, and a 75% rise in Rent Assistance. That design, P\*, is what the paper's own comparison supports against Part 1, and the paper's choice against the targeted package, weighing poverty against work incentives and fiscal sustainability. It leaves 0.20 million fewer people in poverty than the design the 22 October paper recommended, at the same budget and with A$1.5bn to A$1.8bn a year more net benefit on the complete accounting. If Parliament weights poverty above work incentives, it should legislate the targeted package instead, which leaves about 0.40 million fewer people in poverty than P\* but puts more families on very high effective tax rates and needs a larger levy.

Stage 1 is not free and the paper does not say it is. Its first year is borrowed (A$20.7bn, provisional), its monetised net benefit is negative on the complete accounting, its guarantee protects payments rather than income after housing costs, and a levy on market income may come well before its central date of 2043-44: the joint stress test gives a 37% to 59% chance of one before 2034-35 (provisional). Its case is that it cuts poverty after housing costs from 14.2% to 10.5%, with few very high effective tax rates and a smaller levy than the package.

Parliament should also legislate the tests a universal basic income would have to pass, and no rates: the AI need test, the randomised trial with its work-effort cap, and the PBO certificate. On current evidence the pathway will probably never be used. It is insurance against a shock whose probability no one can yet estimate, and it is cheap. No dividend should be legislated now; Section 10 sets out the options and the tests any of them should pass.

### Recommendations

Twenty-three recommendations, each with an owner and a date, would put Stage 1 in place by 1 July 2028 (Table 9.1).

**Table 9.1. Recommendations, with responsible actors and dates, 2027 to 2041**

| No. | Recommendation | Responsible actor | By when | Supported by |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Introduce and pass an Australian Basic Income Bill that legislates P\* as Stage 1: the payment floor of A$569.81 a week, the child payment with one schedule withdrawn at 18 cents a dollar (the taper set by regulation), the 75% rise in the maximum rates of Rent Assistance, indexation, the binding safeguards and the cash guarantee. If Parliament weights poverty above work incentives, legislate the targeted package as Stage 1 instead (Table 5.7 gives the choice and its price). Legislate no universal rate and no dividend | Minister for Social Services; Treasurer | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Sections 5, 7 |
| 2 | Refer this paper to a joint select committee on income security and artificial intelligence, with terms of reference covering P\*, the targeted package, Part 1, the floor-first option, the other alternatives at equal budget, the universal pathway's tests and the dividend options | Parliament (both Houses) | Refer by February 2027; report by 30 September 2027 | Sections 2, 5, 10 (Tables 5.7 and 5.9 to 5.14) |
| 3 | Legislate the five durable revenue measures, to start on 1 July 2028, and report each measure's yield against the PBO's costing in each Budget | Treasurer; ATO | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Section 6 (Tables 6.4 to 6.6 and 6.10) |
| 4 | Legislate no dividend now. If Parliament later wants one, choose among the four options in Section 10 only after a PBO costing of each and against the tests set out there: a stated value on universality at least equal to the option's break-even weight, financing stated separately from Stage 1's, rules described as statutory, and delivery to non-lodgers reported | Joint select committee; Parliament; Treasurer | Not before the PBO costings (Recommendation 12c) | Section 10 (Tables 10.2, 10.3 and 10.7) |
| 5a | Amend the *Parliamentary Service Act 1999* so the PBO certifies each year the five durable taxes against Stage 1's costs, and certifies the funding of any universal rate proposed after the need test and the trial, with a stress test for a payment at the Age Pension rate | Parliament | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Section 6; Table 9.6 |
| 5b | Legislate the AI receipts rule, under which half of tax receipts above the 2026-27 Budget's projections, fixed when the Act passes and indexed to prices, count toward the funding of any universal rate | Treasurer | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Section 6 (Tables 6.42 and 6.43) |
| 5c | Legislate a Basic Income Levy set each year to the projected gap of the five durable taxes after any surpluses kept as debt reduction are drawn, never above it, unless Parliament legislates another measure within six months | Treasurer | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Section 6 (Tables 6.6 and 6.9) |
| 5d | Legislate the AI need test and the counter-cyclical rule: in a displacement declared from the financial year in which the need test's youth unemployment leg has been met over eight consecutive quarters, the floor and child payment keep their indexation and the levy meets any gap | Treasurer | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Section 6 (Tables 6.50, 6.51 and 6.53); Table 9.6 |
| 5e | Provide that no Bill setting a universal rate may be introduced until the need test is met, the trial has reported within the work-effort cap and the PBO has certified the funding; legislate no Stage 3 authority | Parliament | Pass by 31 December 2027 | Section 5 (Table 5.15) |
| 6 | Direct JSA and the ABS to publish the AI-specific need-test series and the rent and household indicators in Table 9.5 every quarter or year, including the households worse off after housing costs by payment and tenure; report payment suspensions at the floor, and decide whether a suspension under the activity test may take a recipient below the floor | Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations; Minister for Social Services; JSA; ABS; AIHW | Confirm by 30 June 2028 | Sections 2, 5, 9 (Tables 2.2, 6.21, 9.5 and 9.6) |
| 7 | Deliver Stage 1 through existing payment, tax and myGov systems; require a human decision before any debt or protection clawback is raised from matched data; complete an independent audit before launch | Services Australia; ATO; ANAO | Audit by 31 May 2028 | Sections 4, 7 |
| 8 | Fund an independent randomised trial of a payment at the JobSeeker rate with about 5,000 working-age adults not receiving a payment, at least 3,000 of them on low incomes, against a control group twice that size, run by a university consortium selected by open tender, at about A$120m a year; publish in advance what it can and cannot establish; and evaluate Stage 1 by starting the floor and child payment in Services Australia regions in a random order over July to December 2028, with arrears from 1 July | DSS; Services Australia | Regional order drawn by 31 March 2028; trial contract by June 2029; trial July 2029 to June 2031; results by December 2031 | Sections 4, 6, 9 (Tables 6.2 and 9.7) |
| 9 | Consider a Bill setting a universal rate only after all three tests are met, informed by a fresh PBO costing, the trial and a Productivity Commission review, and choose then between the phase-out and the net-income guarantee; a payment at the Age Pension rate only with a stress-tested PBO certificate and a decision on the residence test for adults aged 67 and over | Parliament | Not before 2032 | Sections 5, 6, 8 (Tables 6.48 and 9.7) |
| 10 | Pilot a voluntary National Contribution Register and a Community Contribution Fund of up to A$100m a year, reporting against objective 7 | DSS, with Volunteering Australia and Carers Australia | 2029-30 to 2031-32 | Sections 3, 6 (Table 6.2) |
| 11 | Run the national consultation set out in Table 9.4, agree First Nations delivery principles with the Coalition of Peaks after community forums from February to May 2027 and before the exposure drafts, and publish how feedback changed the design | Treasury; DSS; National Indigenous Australians Agency (NIAA) | February to September 2027; principles by 31 May 2027 | Section 9 (Tables 9.2 to 9.4) |

| 12d | Request a PBO costing of a universal payment at the JobSeeker rate on the smallest displacement that meets the need test (about 15.6 points on the 22 October run), so the trial and the certificate have a reference; no Age Pension-rate payment is costed, because every modelled rate is past the revenue peak | Joint select committee | By 31 August 2027 | Section 6 (Tables 6.38, 6.39, 6.47 and 6.48) |
| 12e | Cost any proposed universal rate again before its Bill and reset it if the PBO's yield is lower | Treasurer | Before any Bill setting a universal rate | Section 6 (Table 6.39) |
| 12f | Prepare an agency costing of the payment system build, including current-rules entitlements for every protected person and family | Services Australia | Before the Bill is introduced | Section 7 (Table 6.2) |
| 13 | Treasury models couples' labour supply under the child payment's taper before the Bill is introduced, costing second earners who stop work in the net cost, so the taper can be set by regulation, and later the net regional and household distribution of Stage 1, incidence by age and consumer incidence of the fuel and gas measures, using ATO postcode data and linked household records | Treasury | Couples by 30 June 2027; the rest by 31 December 2031 | Sections 5 and 6 (Tables 5.4, 5.8, 6.14 and 6.15) |

No organisation has yet been consulted on this submission. Table 9.2 lists the groups, their published positions with dates and what the paper asks of them, and the paper states in advance which findings would change the design.

|---|---|---|
| States and territories | No published state or territory position on a basic income was found. In the Australian Capital Territory (ACT) Legislative Assembly, the Chief Minister's amendment called on the ACT Government to keep supporting low-income households and to contribute to the Senate poverty inquiry, describing poverty as a national issue needing a collaborative approach (13 October 2022) [S199]. Their interests include concessions, public housing rents and state taxes | An intergovernmental agreement on concessions and rents before 1 July 2028 |
| Australian Council of Trade Unions (ACTU) and unions | Wants workers to share AI productivity gains; consultation before AI is introduced; agreements on job security and retraining; a national AI authority (3 September 2025) [S200]. | Business Council of Australia (BCA), Australian Chamber of Commerce and Industry (ACCI), Australian Industry Group (Ai Group) and the Council of Small Business Organisations Australia (COSBOA) | Jointly oppose more regulation of AI and support AI skills (15 August 2025) [S202]. ACCI says tax and regulatory settings must change for Australia to benefit from AI (2026) [S203]. The BCA chief executive backed a staged rise in JobSeeker (May 2023) [S138] | Evidence on how the excess profits tax, a levy or any later rate rise would affect investment and hiring |
| ACOSS and the community sector, including carers and volunteering bodies | ACOSS seeks working-age payments of at least A$600 a week, indexed to wages (July 2026) [S197]. Volunteering Australia reports 9.5 million volunteers (April 2026) [S204] | Views on adequacy, top-ups, the child supplement and the Contribution Register |

The round-14 assessment (98.75 of 100) led to a model correction: Part 1 alone's Stage 2 rise in a displacement had been funded with the wealth-tax fund's dividend and advance, and is now 19.1 and 26.9 points, not 18.3 and 24.9 (Table 6.52). The 2027 consultation in Table 9.4 is proposed; its results will be added to this table.*

The proposed process runs from February to September 2027 and reaches every group in Table 9.2 (Table 9.4); First Nations forums run from February to May, so their delivery principles come before the exposure drafts.

**Table 9.4. Consultation process, 2027**

| Step | Dates | Method | Output |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Call for submissions | 1 February to 30 April 2027 | Committee website; plain-language guide; easy-read and translated versions | Published submissions |
| 2. States and territories | May 2027 | Council on Federal Financial Relations session | Agreed list of state interactions |
| 3. Unions and employers | June 2027 | Joint roundtable, then separate sessions | Record of agreement and disagreement on revenue measures and triggers |
| 4. Community sector, carers and people receiving payments | June 2027 | Roundtables and lived-experience panels | Advice on adequacy and the cash guarantee |
| 5. First Nations organisations | February to May 2027, with a review of the exposure drafts in August | Community-controlled forums in every state and territory, including remote communities | Co-designed delivery principles by 31 May 2027, before the exposure drafts |
| 6. Regional Australia | July 2027 | Public hearings in six regional centres | Regional impact statement |
| 7. AI industry and researchers | June 2027 | Technical workshop on the excess profits tax, the fund's payout rule and the need-test indicators | Technical advice note |
| 8. Citizens' panel | August 2027 | 100 randomly selected Australians over two weekends | Panel report on the Stage 3 trade-offs |
| 9. Exposure drafts | July to August 2027 | Draft Bills published for comment | Revised Bills |
| 10. Report and response | By 30 September 2027 | Committee report; government consultation report | Additions to Table 9.3 |

*Source: author's proposal.*

Consultation findings would change the design in these ways.

1. If First Nations organisations seek community-controlled delivery in remote areas, the Act would provide for it.
2. If employer groups show that the excess profits tax would cut investment more than the PBO assumed, the stack, and with it the levy, would change.
3. If states and territories identify interactions with concessions or public housing rents, the cash guarantee would extend to them.
4. If unions and the community sector favour a higher floor or the floor-first option, the committee could move along the trade-off in Section 5.
5. If public submissions show low support for any universal payment, Parliament could drop the pathway's tests, leaving Stage 1.
6. If the committee weights poverty above work incentives, it could recommend the targeted package as Stage 1.

### Monitoring and evaluation plan

Parliament will have post-reform poverty figures, trial results and a Productivity Commission review before any Bill setting a universal rate. Table 9.5 sets out the indicators, with poverty after housing costs on the relative line as the headline. The Productivity Commission reviews the Act in 2031, 2036 and 2041, and the ANAO audits delivery from 2029-30. A randomised trial of a payment at the JobSeeker rate from July 2029 to June 2031, and a random regional order for starting the floor and child payment, give control groups (Table 9.7; Recommendation 8).

Each safeguard has a measure, a threshold, an automatic action under the Act and an owner (Table 9.6). For Stage 1 a shortfall in the five durable taxes is met by a levy set to the gap once any surpluses kept as debt reduction are drawn. Under the Act the floor and child payment are never paused, though a later Parliament can change that rule.

**Table 9.6. Binding safeguards: measure, threshold, automatic action and owner, from 1 July 2028**

| Safeguard | Measure | Threshold | Automatic action under the Act | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Durable account | PBO projection of the five durable taxes against Stage 1's costs, and of any surpluses kept as debt reduction at the debt rate | A shortfall once the kept surpluses are used up (expected, on central yields, from 2043-44, with a 37% to 59% chance of one before 2034-35 in the joint stress test; the taxes fall short each year from 2037-38) | The Basic Income Levy is set from the next 1 July to the gap the kept surpluses no longer cover, never above it, unless Parliament legislates another measure within six months; the floor and child payment are never paused | PBO; Treasurer |
| Counter-cyclical rule | Youth unemployment leg of the need test, assessed over eight consecutive quarters | Met over eight quarters: a displacement is declared from the financial year that contains the eighth | The floor and child payment keep their indexation; the levy meets any gap; the PBO reports within three months on the funding of a universal payment on the trigger-timed path | PBO; Treasurer; JSA |
| Fiscal certificate for any universal payment | PBO costing of the proposed rate and projected funding against net cost, next four years | Funding at least equal to net cost in each year; for a payment at the Age Pension rate, also under a doubled taxpayer response and the combined downside, which no rate passes on any modelled path | Without a certificate no Bill setting a universal rate may be introduced; if the PBO's yield later proves lower, the later Act's own correction applies | PBO |
| Need test | Youth unemployment, aged 15 to 24, and JSA's series for 22 to 29 year olds in the most and least AI-exposed occupations | Both, over the same 8 consecutive quarters: youth unemployment at least 3 points above the September 2026 level of 11.1%, and the exposed group's employment falling at least 5% relative to the least exposed | Without it, no Bill setting a universal rate may be introduced; productivity growth cannot substitute for it | JSA; ABS |
| Work-effort cap | Participation effect in the embedded trial, among at least 3,000 low-income working-age adults not receiving a payment, against a control group twice that size | A fall of more than 3 percentage points, with the lower end of its 90% confidence interval above 1 point | No Bill setting a universal rate may be introduced under the Act; Stage 1 continues | DSS; independent evaluator |
| Rent safeguard | Lower-quartile rents for new leases against wages; real income after housing costs of private renters in the lowest income fifth | Rents more than 2 points faster than wages over four quarters, or two consecutive years of real decline in that income | Maximum Rent Assistance rises by the gap between rent and wage growth, and any universal step is deferred by a year | Treasurer; Minister for Housing; ABS; AIHW |
| Review | Whole Act | 2031, 2036 and 2041 | Productivity Commission review; the pathway's tests lapse on 30 June 2043 unless Parliament renews them; Stage 1 continues | Productivity Commission; Parliament |

*Source: author's proposal. Note: on the Budget's forecasts the need test is not met. In the displacement scenarios it is met in October to December 2036 (moderate) and January to March 2035 (severe); a payment at the JobSeeker rate then needs 18.6 or 25.5 points on the 22 October run, and until any later Act the counter-cyclical rule holds the floor and child payment (Tables 6.38 and 6.51). At the OpenResearch trial's latest 3.9-point estimate the work-effort cap binds with probability 0.79 to 0.84; a universal payment passes with probability 0.16 to 0.21 at 3.9 points, and with even odds only if the true fall is under 3.0 points (model Table F4-70). These are statutory safeguards, which a later Parliament can change. Legal advice on sections 53 to 55 of the Constitution is needed before the levy formula is drafted.*

The trial can show how individuals respond in work, health and care to a larger payment (Table 9.7), but not how the economy responds to the taxes, to a payment known to be permanent or to extra demand. A good result is therefore not evidence that a universal payment is sound, and the Act requires separate evidence before any Bill setting a universal rate.

**Table 9.7. What the embedded trial can and cannot establish, and the separate evidence each vote requires, 2029 to 2037**

| The trial can establish | The trial cannot establish | Separate evidence required before any Bill setting a universal rate |
|---|---|---|
| How working-age adults' work, hours and earnings respond to a payment at the JobSeeker rate over two years | The response to a payment that people know is permanent | A Productivity Commission review of the evidence, including permanent programs such as Alaska's dividend |
| Effects on health, care, study and volunteering | The economic cost of the taxes that fund the payment, and how taxpayers respond to them | A fresh PBO costing of the proposed rate and the five taxes together, with their behavioural effects (Recommendations 12b and 12e) |
| Take-up and administration of a larger payment | Effects on prices, rents and interest rates across markets | The rent safeguard indicators and the income of low-income renters after housing costs (Tables 9.5 and 9.6) |
| How the floor and the universal payment interact for recipients | Effects on wages and employment across the economy | JSA and ABS displacement indicators, and Treasury modelling of economy-wide effects |

*Source: author's proposal. Note: about 5,000 adults receive a payment at the JobSeeker rate, at least 3,000 of them on low incomes, against a control group of twice the low-income number, which gives a 90% chance of detecting a 4.2-point fall in participation, the figure the 22 October paper used; at the trial's latest estimate of 3.9 points the trial detects some fall with probability 0.97 to 1.00 and the cap binds with probability 0.79 to 0.84 [S191]. For Stage 1 the floor and child payment start in Services Australia regions in a random order over July to December 2028, with arrears from 1 July, so later regions are a control group for up to six months [S135].*

## 10. A dividend: options for later

A dividend is a yearly payment to every adult, the most visible universal element a basic income could have. The 22 October paper set out four ways to pay one and left the choice to Parliament. This revision recommends none of them now, for three reasons drawn from the paper's own figures. No dividend reduces poverty: Stage 1 gives the same poverty rate with or without one, because a payment to everyone lifts the median and the poverty line with it (Table 10.4). After the taxes that pay for it, about a third of households are better off under every option, the same share as with no dividend (Table 10.2). And every option scores below Stage 1 alone on the complete accounting (Table 10.2).

This chapter sets the options out neutrally, with the tests any of them should pass before Parliament legislates it. Every table in it was run on the 22 October design, Part 1, with the option shown; none has been rerun on P\*, and on P\* the figures, especially the levy dates, would differ.

### What a fund could and could not do

If Parliament later wants a fund, its case is narrower than the 22 October paper made it. A fund is public wealth: the wealth-tax fund would hold A$325.3bn at 30 June 2046 (A$203.5bn in 2026 dollars), and in a declared displacement it could lend the Budget up to a quarter of its balance, A$56.6bn in 2035 or A$75.6bn in 2040 (Table 10.2). That loan bridges about a year (Table 6.51). A fund is not much of a source of income. The wealth-tax fund's dividend is A$47 in 2029-30 and A$461 in 2045-46, and on the high reading of the wealth tax's losses in other taxes the fund runs out in 2083-84 (Table 10.24). Its return is a risk the recipients bear: at the bond rate it pays A$383 in 2045-46 and is exhausted by 2067-68 (Table 10.12). A surplus fund lasts at the central return but pays a token dividend, A$1 in 2032-33 and A$86 in 2045-46.

Universal cash tied to volatile revenue has been cut or eroded where it has been tried. Alaska's Governor halved the 2016 dividend by veto, and the state Supreme Court upheld the cut [S99, S195]; Alaska's 2025 dividend was its smallest in real terms [S229]. Iran's near-universal transfer lost purchasing power to inflation and sanctions [S100].

### The tests any dividend should pass

Before Parliament legislates any dividend, the paper proposes five tests.

1. **A PBO costing** of the option against the 2026-27 Budget baseline, and for a fund a ruling on its Budget treatment (Recommendation 12c).
2. **A stated value on universality.** Parliament states what it values paying every adult at, per adult a year, and the option's break-even weight must be below it. On the 22 October run the weights are A$171 to A$657 for the wealth-tax fund, A$22 to A$65 for the Budget-paid dividend and A$20 to A$37 for the surplus fund (Table 10.2).
3. **Financing separate from Stage 1's.** The option's financing is stated separately from the five durable taxes, with the levy it adds and the year it brings Stage 1's first levy forward to. On the 22 October run the dividend options brought it from 2044-45 to between 2038-39 and 2040-41 (Table 10.2).
4. **Rules described as statutory.** Any never-cut guarantee and any rule protecting a fund's capital are statutory, and a later Parliament can change them; the Act would say so.
5. **Delivery reported.** Lodgers are paid at assessment and non-lodgers by Services Australia after enrolment, with take-up reported (80% is assumed, with no Australian precedent; Table 10.15); the dividend is excluded from every adjusted-taxable-income test (Table 10.16).

### The four options

A dividend would be paid every year to every eligible adult. It would not reduce poverty: on the 22 October run Stage 1 gives 11.3% on the relative line with or without a dividend, because a payment to everyone lifts the median and the line (Table 10.4). It buys universality instead: a payment to every adult, a stake in any fund and a rail to people who lodge no tax return. Before the taxes are counted it reaches 86% of households rather than 60%; after them, about a third are better off under every option (32.8% to 33.0%; Table 10.2).

There are four options, and the paper recommends none of them now (Recommendation 4):

- a **wealth-tax fund**: a Permanent AI Dividend Fund fed by the net wealth tax, the design the 22 October paper modelled in full (Tables 10.8 to 10.27);
- a **Budget-paid dividend**: the same dividend paid from the five durable taxes and the levy, with no wealth tax and no fund;
- a **surplus fund**: a permanent fund with no wealth tax, fed only by the durable account's surpluses;
- **no dividend**: Stage 1 alone, which this paper recommends (P\*; on the 22 October run, Part 1 alone).

Tables 10.2 and 10.3 compare them. With no dividend or the Budget-paid dividend the five taxes' surpluses reduce debt and are drawn before any levy, so the A$49.5bn kept meets Part 1 alone's shortfalls from 2038-39 until 2044-45; under the fund options each year's shortfall is levied (Tables 10.2 and 6.6).

Any dividend would follow the newly arrived resident's four-year waiting period that applies to JobSeeker Payment [S123]. Under any option lodgers would receive the dividend when their return is assessed, and Services Australia would pay the 80% of non-lodgers assumed to enrol, an assumption with no Australian precedent: 1.22 million of 1.53 million (Tables 10.15 and 6.25). Displaced workers lodge returns, so the rail does not reach them. The dividend is taxable, with the tax-free threshold and the Low Income Tax Offset (LITO) unchanged [S130, S131], and excluded from every test that uses adjusted taxable income, at A$10m to A$116m a year (Table 10.16). Every adult keeps at least 53.0 cents of each dollar (Table 10.1), so before funding no adult is worse off.

**Table 10.1. Tax treatment of the dividend: smallest gain at any income and tax collected, 2029-30 to 2045-46 (A$; A$ billion)**

| Year | Dividend (A$ a year) | Smallest gain at any income (A$; share of the dividend) | Taxable income where it occurs | Tax collected on the dividend (A$ billion) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2029-30 | 47 | 25 (53%) | A$393,225 | 0.2 |
| 2032-33 | 155 | 82 (53%) | A$400,000 | 0.6 |
| 2037-38 | 395 | 209 (53%) | A$190,000 | 1.8 |
| 2041-42 | 455 | 241 (53%) | A$400,000 | 2.3 |
| 2045-46 | 461 | 244 (53%) | A$393,225 | 2.6 |

*Source: author's modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Tables F4-46 and F4-77) [S5]. Note: the dividend path is the wealth-tax fund's, which the Budget-paid dividend also pays. The smallest gain is tested at every kink of the tax scale and on a A$25 grid to A$400,000; it covers the tax scale and the Medicare levy, because the dividend is excluded from every adjusted-taxable-income test, including the Medicare levy surcharge and the Low Income Superannuation Tax Offset (LISTO). Without those exclusions a surcharge payer at 1.5% would keep 51.5 cents, and in 2037-38 about 40,000 and 21,000 adults sit within one dividend below the LISTO and surcharge cut-offs (Table 10.15). Stages 2 and 3 remove the threshold, because their payments are large enough to leave no adult worse off.*

The wealth-tax fund receives the wealth tax net of the PBO's interaction line, and any surplus of the other five taxes after Stage 1's costs (Table 10.8). It is invested as the Future Fund is, at a central return of the consumer price index (CPI) plus 4%, the bottom of that fund's mandate [S132]. Each year it pays out 4% of its average balance over three years, less 10% kept in a reserve. The Act would forbid spending the capital on the dividend and hold the dividend in cash terms until a year's levied top-up would exceed 25% of it.

The dividend would be A$47 in 2029-30, about 90 cents a week, and A$461 in 2045-46 (Table 10.10). The wealth tax raises A$193.9bn gross to 2038-39, but the PBO's interaction line, the revenue it costs in other taxes, outlasts it [S133]. With all of that line charged, a high reading because part of it comes from a measure outside the stack, the tax's net present value is about A$8.5bn at the fund's 6.5% and negative at the debt rate (Table 10.22). The guarantee lapses in 2041-42, after A$1.5bn of levied top-ups (Table 10.23). The dividend is then held at A$461 to 2070-71 and falls to A$2 by 2085-86, once the line, charged to the fund's capital, has exhausted the fund in 2083-84 and the levy carries it (Table 10.24). With three-quarters or half of the line charged to the fund, the guarantee would last to 2049-50 or 2067-68 and the fund to 2095-96 or 2113-14, and the options' ranking is unchanged (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-107).

In 2028-29 the wealth-tax fund receives A$26.6bn, almost all of it wealth tax, with a deadweight cost of A$6.6bn to A$26.4bn (Table 10.6). Counting the taxes' economic cost and Stage 1's own behavioural costs, Part 1 alone has a net benefit of −A$24.9bn to −A$4.1bn, against −A$51.4bn to −A$10.7bn with the wealth-tax fund (Table 10.5).

Most of the difference comes from the wealth tax rather than from paying every adult. The Budget-paid dividend pays the same dividend from the durable taxes, whose shortfalls are levied from 2040-41, A$99.8bn to 2045-46 (Table 10.5). Over ten years it adds A$21.3bn of net cost but no deadweight cost, and on the complete accounting it breaks even at A$22 to A$65 per adult a year (Table 10.2). The wealth-tax fund costs A$239 to A$926 per adult a year over ten years, all of it the wealth tax's price (Table 10.7). The wealth tax buys a common asset and a lender in a displacement; without a fund, Stage 2 and 3 rises would be about 0.2 and 0.4 points higher (author's calculation from Table 6.39).

The surplus fund keeps a fund and its rules but drops the wealth tax. Fed only by the durable taxes' surpluses, it stays small, A$63.7bn in 2046, and pays A$1 in 2032-33 and A$86 in 2045-46. It lasts at the central return, with its never-cut rule intact and a dividend of A$188 by 2085-86, but that is its best case: the rule lapses in 2089-90 at the bond rate and by 2065-66 in a fifth of 1,000 volatile return paths (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-108). Because surpluses build the fund rather than reduce debt, the levy starts in 2038-39 and totals A$77.3bn to 2045-46; the option breaks even at A$20 to A$37 per adult a year (Table 10.2).

**Table 10.3. The Part 2 options: what each buys, what it costs, who bears the risk and how it would be legislated, 2028-29 to 2085-86 (A$; A$ billion; per cent)**

| Option | What it buys | What it costs | Who bears the risk | How it would be legislated |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No dividend (Part 1 alone) | The best net benefit (−A$630.3bn to −A$85.0bn in present value) and the smallest levy (A$25.8bn, from 2044-45 on central yields), with the same poverty result as any dividend and no tax on wealth | No universal element: 43% of adults gain before funding, against 83% with the wealth-tax fund or the Budget-paid dividend; no payment to every adult, no rail to non-lodgers and no fund, so no lender in a displacement: the levy is larger (severe: 1.96% of market income in 2034-35, when the fund lends, and 6.17% by 2045-46 with no vote, against nil and 5.87% with the wealth-tax fund; moderate: 1.03% in 2036-37 and 2.37% by 2045-46, against nil and 2.17%; Table 6.51) | Market-income taxpayers, through the levy, if the five taxes fall short or AI displaces workers; adults outside the payment system, who gain nothing | Part 1 of the ABI Bill only; a dividend can be added later by amendment |
| Wealth-tax fund | The largest dividend from a fund (A$461 a year from 2043-44) and a fund of A$325.3bn in 2046 that pays a dividend until 2083-84; a lender of up to A$76bn in a displacement, so a smaller levy than Part 1 alone's; the smallest levy of the dividend options to 2045-46 (A$44.7bn) | The highest deadweight cost and the lowest net benefit, so universality must be worth A$171 to A$657 per adult a year; on the high reading of its losses, the wealth tax nets about A$8.5bn over its life, the guarantee lapses in 2041-42 and the fund runs out in 2083-84, after which its losses fall on the levy | Holders of fortunes above A$100 million, who pay the tax; recipients, who bear the fund's return (the dividend is A$383 in 2045-46 if the fund earns only the bond rate); taxpayers after 2083-84 | Part 2 of the ABI Bill, the Permanent AI Dividend Fund Act and the two Net Wealth Tax Acts (Table 7.1); the fund's rules statutory, not entrenched |
| Budget-paid dividend | The same dividend and reach as the wealth-tax fund at a far lower break-even weight (A$22 to A$65 per adult a year); no wealth tax and no lasting interaction line | The largest levy (A$99.8bn to 2045-46, from 2040-41); no fund, so no common asset and no loan in a displacement; the dividend is a Budget outlay every year (A$11.4bn in 2045-46) with no rule after 2045-46 | Market-income taxpayers, through the levy; recipients, because a dividend backed by no asset depends on each Budget | Part 2 of the ABI Bill only: the dividend, its indexation and its payment from the durable account; no new Acts |
| Surplus fund | A fund that lasts at the central return, keeping its real value in expectation (A$39.9bn in 2046 and A$51bn in 2085-86, in 2026 dollars), with a never-cut rule that holds on that path; the lowest break-even weight (A$20 to A$37); no wealth tax | A token dividend: A$1 in 2032-33, A$86 in 2045-46; an earlier and larger levy than the wealth-tax fund (A$77.3bn from 2038-39), because surpluses go to the fund rather than reducing debt; a small lender (A$13bn in 2040) | Recipients, because the fund grows only when the five taxes exceed Stage 1's costs, and its never-cut rule lapses by 2065-66 in a fifth of volatile return paths; market-income taxpayers, through the earlier levy | Part 2 of the ABI Bill and the Fund Act, crediting only durable surpluses; no wealth tax Acts |

*Source: author's assessment from Tables 10.2, 10.5 to 10.7, 10.23 and 10.24 (model Tables F4-98 to F4-101). Note: no option changes Stage 1's poverty rate by more than 0.1 point, and after the taxes are allocated about a third of households are better off under every option (32.8% to 33.0%).*

The choice turns on the value Parliament places on universality, its tolerance for a levy, and whether it wants a permanent fund. A real dividend at a low price points to the Budget-paid dividend, and a lasting common asset to the surplus fund, with a token dividend. The largest dividend from a fund points to the wealth-tax fund, at the highest price, and no value on universality to Part 1 alone. The paper sets out the evidence and recommends none now (Recommendation 4).

### The wealth-tax fund in detail

The tables below are the 22 October paper's model of the wealth-tax fund, run with Part 1 as Stage 1.

On the 22 October run, with Part 1 and the wealth-tax fund, no stage is triggered on the Budget's forecasts, so Stage 1 runs on (Tables 10.8 to 10.11). Once the start-up advance is repaid in 2031-32, the durable taxes cover Stage 1's costs, net of the dividend's offsets, to 2038-39. From 2039-40 a levy is needed, A$0.3bn (0.016% of market income) rising to A$10.8bn (0.405%) in 2045-46. Levies total A$44.7bn to 2045-46, A$1.5bn of them top-ups of the dividend in 2040-41, before the never-cut guarantee lapses in 2041-42.

**Table 10.8. The wealth-tax fund: the Permanent AI Dividend Fund and the dividend, design, returns and Budget treatment, 2028-29 to 2045-46**

| Item | Rule | Source or basis |
|---|---|---|
| Inflows | The wealth tax net of the losses it causes in other taxes: the PBO's personal income tax interaction line in every year, on the PBO's own year-by-year profile and, after its last year, grown with nominal GDP as other measures are. From 2038-39 the net inflow is negative: the dividend account meets it until the never-cut guarantee lapses in 2041-42, and the fund's capital after that. The share of the 40% excess profits tax is nil under the rule. Any surplus of the other durable taxes after the floor, child payment, protections and implementation, once the 2028-29 start-up advance is repaid; the smoothing reserve above its cap | Wealth-tax fund design |
| Capital rule | Capital is not spent on the dividend, a statutory rule that a later Parliament can change: the only outflows are the payout and, after the guarantee lapses, the interaction line. On the central return the line exhausts the fund in 2083-84, so on these figures the fund is not permanent (Table 10.24). A loan to the Budget in a declared displacement (Counter-cyclical rule) is an investment at the bond rate, repaid with interest | Wealth-tax fund design |
| Investment | As the Future Fund: a diversified portfolio; at 30 June 2026 the Future Fund held A$289.7bn, 5.4% in cash and 8.1% in credit | Future Fund, 30 June 2026 [S132] |
| Return, central | CPI + 4.0% = 6.5%: the bottom of the mandate's target, 'CPI + 4.0% to 5.0% pa over the long term'; the Future Fund has returned 8.3% a year since May 2006 against a target of 7.0%, and 9.0% over 10 years against 7.1% | Future Fund [S132] |
| Return, high and low | High: the top of the mandate, CPI + 5.0% = 7.5%. Low: the Commonwealth bond rate, 4.13% (the model's debt rate): the return with no risk premium | Future Fund mandate [S132]; the model's debt rate |
| Risk | The return above the bond rate is payment for bearing equity risk, not a free surplus: a government that borrows at 4.13% and invests at 6.5% expects more but bears the chance of less; the low case shows the fund and dividend at the bond rate, and the reserve test (Table 10.13) a 25% fall | Author |
| Budget treatment | Assumed as the Future Fund, though unsettled: the fund's earnings are outside the underlying cash balance ('Excludes net Future Fund earnings'), but Finance brings the Future Fund's earnings into it once payments begin, and this fund pays a dividend every year (Table 10.14). Net debt counts only the fund's cash and debt securities, about one-seventh of the portfolio; net financial worth counts the whole fund | Department of Finance, Monthly Financial Statements [S168] and consultation paper [S169] |
| Spending rule | Payout = 4% of the average balance at the previous three 30 June dates (those since the fund began). At the central return the fund's real return is 4.0%, so the rule spends the expected real return, but the interaction line outlasts the wealth tax. The never-cut guarantee holds the dividend with levied top-ups only until a year's top-up would exceed 25% of the gross dividend, which happens in 2041-42 after A$1.5bn of top-ups. The dividend is then the payout rule's: A$461 a year in cash to 2070-71, falling to nil by 2085-86 as the line, charged to capital, exhausts the fund in 2083-84 (Tables 10.23 and 10.24). A real-return rule pays the same at the central return; it is chosen against because, applied to realised returns, it pays nothing in years of negative real returns | Author's choice between two rules |
| Dividend | (Payout + share × last year's excess profits tax) × 90% / eligible adults, never below last year's in cash terms until the guarantee lapses; paid once a year, a year in arrears, from 2029-30 (the first 30 June balance is 30 June 2029); a refundable tax offset assessed with each lodger's return (self-lodgers by 31 October, agents' clients to the following May) or a Services Australia payment for non-lodgers after they enrol; 80% of non-lodgers are assumed to enrol, so about 305,000 to 334,000 adults a year are not paid (98.5% of eligible adults paid in 2029-30; Table 10.15) | Wealth-tax fund design; lodgment dates [S129]; take-up assumed |
| Tax treatment | The dividend is assessable income taxed at the recipient's marginal rate; the A$18,200 tax-free threshold and LITO are unchanged; it is excluded from every income test that uses adjusted taxable income or income for surcharge purposes (the Medicare levy surcharge, LISTO, Paid Parental Leave, the Child Care Subsidy and the Commonwealth Seniors Health Card), at a cost of A$10m to A$116m a year (Table 10.16); the dividend is absorbed into recipients' payments (a recipient receives the larger of the dividend and the payment) | Wealth-tax fund design (Tables 10.15 and 10.16) |
| Smoothing reserve | 10% of each year's entitlement is retained in the reserve (cash, earning the debt rate) up to one year's gross dividend; the excess goes to fund capital; the reserve pays the dividend when the entitlement falls below last year's and, in a declared displacement, durable shortfalls first | Wealth-tax fund design; tested in Table 10.13 |
| Share of the excess profits tax | Nil: no share meets the rule (the start-up advance repaid by 2031-32 and no levy to 2040-41); even at nil the first levy is in 2039-40, so the rule's fallback applies and the levy's start year is a result, not a target (Table 10.12) | Wealth-tax fund rule |
| Start-up advance and later shortfalls | The 2028-29 shortfall of the durable taxes (A$20.6bn) is advanced by the Budget and repaid, with interest at the debt rate, from the next surpluses (2031-32); a later shortfall, including any never-cut top-up before the guarantee lapses, is met by a levy on market income sized each year to the gap (never above it), first in 2039-40; any advance still outstanding after 2031-32 would be repaid by the levy in equal shares over five years (only in the weak-revenue cases of Table 10.19) | Wealth-tax fund rule |
| Counter-cyclical rule | A displacement is declared from the financial year in which the need test's youth unemployment leg has been met over eight consecutive quarters, as the test is assessed; a one-quarter spike does not declare. Before then shortfalls are levied. Once declared: the dividend is not cut in cash terms before the guarantee lapses; the floor and child payment keep their indexation; durable shortfalls are met first from the reserve, then by a loan from the fund to the Budget at the 10-year bond rate (4.13%), up to 25% of the fund's balance; the levy pays the loan's interest until the first year Stage 2 could start, then repays it in 10 equal instalments whether or not a vote is held, so in both scenarios the loan bridges about one year (Tables 6.51 and 6.53) | Wealth-tax fund rule (rounds 8 to 10) |

*Source: author's design and modelling (supplementary model_output.md, Tables F4-50 and F4-73 to F4-79) [S5].*
