Note: as Table 6.34, with the child-payment rule, its prior-year timing and the response to its tapers shown separately; the timing column includes the income-fall review (A$0.8bn in 2028-29, rising to A$1.2bn) and the response column the payment's income effect. The wealth-tax fund earns 6.5% and pays out 4% of its average balance, growing to A$325.3bn by 2045-46, until the interaction line, charged to its capital after the lapse, exhausts it in 2083-84 on the high reading of its losses (Tables 10.23 and 10.24). With its earnings outside the underlying cash balance, as the Future Fund's are, the balance is A$17.0bn worse than baseline in 2045-46. Finance brings the Future Fund's earnings in once payments begin [S169], and on that basis the balance is A$4.2bn worse, or A$4.0bn better counting all earnings (Table 10.14). Net debt counts only the fund's cash and credit [S132, S168], so it is A$51.9bn below baseline by 2045-46, and net financial worth A$333.3bn above.

The central return of 6.5% includes a premium for bearing risk. At the bond rate of 4.13% the fund holds A$223.1bn in 2045-46 and pays A$383, and at CPI + 3% A$280.4bn and A$421, with the first levy in 2039-40 at every return (Table 10.12). At the central return the fund ends below its real inflows in 53% to 55% of return paths, and before the lapse a levied top-up is needed in 73% to 76% of them (Table 10.13).

AI-driven growth raises the dividend only slowly, through larger durable surpluses, adding A$8 to A$47 to the 2035-36 dividend (Table 10.17).

With the wealth-tax fund, on the 22 October run, uncertainty about Stage 1's costs and most of its revenue moves the levy more than the dividend: at 80% of the costings levies peak at 1.36% of market income and the 2045-46 dividend is A$271 (Table 10.19). The wealth tax's further losses in other taxes fall on the dividend: losses of a quarter of the tax cut it from A$461 to A$352, and losses equal to the tax cut it to A$17 (Table 10.21).

### Who gains and who loses after funding with a dividend

With the wealth-tax fund and its six taxes, only 33% of households are better off in 2028-29 and 67% are worse off on the central allocation (Table 10.26), or about 30% to 70% across the incidence variants. The losers are mostly in the top three deciles, where the taxes fall, but include 2.79 million bottom-half households and 85% of households with a member aged 67 or over. Across the variants that range is 1.15 million to 3.03 million, the lower figures if the capital-type taxes fall only on people with capital income (supplementary model_output.md, Table F4-104). Over 2028-29 to 2045-46, in present value, 34% of households are better off (Table 10.27); at Stage 2, 50% are worse off, and at Stage 3, 60%. With Part 1 alone, 33% are better off in 2028-29 and 32% over the period, so after funding the wealth-tax fund leaves the share better off unchanged in 2028-29 and 2 points higher over the period (Table 6.25).

## 11. Appendix: methodology, assumptions and sources

The costings come from a model that applies published Budget, PBO, ATO and ABS figures to a simple representation of the population and its incomes, and the poverty results from a household model built on the same inputs.

### Methodology

The fiscal model works in the fourteen steps of Table 11.1, and an interactive simulator published with the supplementary files lets a reader change the main levers, such as the floor, the taper and the five taxes' yield, and watch costs and the levy move [S5]. Its checks reproduce the paper's model; they do not test the model's assumptions.

**Table 11.1. The fiscal model in fourteen steps, with the assumptions each uses, 2026-27 to 2045-46 (A$ billion, nominal; per cent; identifiers as in Table 6.55)**

| Step | Method | Assumptions and sources |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Population | The ABS estimated resident population of 27.9 million at March 2026 grows at the Centre for Population's rates to 31.5 million in 2035-36, then at 1.1% and 1.0% a year. Temporary visa holders and 0.52 million adults in the waiting period are not eligible | B12, E10 [S155, S178, S180, S183, S184] |
| 2. Gross cost | Eligible children × the child supplement, less the FTB it replaces, under the child payment's rule; under a dividend option (Section 10), also eligible adults × take-up × the dividend, from 2029-30 (100% of lodgers and 80% of non-lodgers) | Tables 6.1 and 10.9 |
| 3. The floor | Each lifted payment is raised by the ratio of the floor to its single rate, keeping couple relativities and the income tests. JobSeeker-type recipients rise with an elasticity of 0.39, and student and Parenting Payment recipients with e61's job-finding elasticity of 0.21. Applied to PBO costing ECR-2025-3193 (A$88 a day), the method reproduces about 75% of the PBO's 2025-26 cost | C4 to C7, D1, D2, F13, F24 [S113, S137] |
| 4. Payments replaced | Each program's spending from the Budget and the PBO's medium-term outlook is replaced only up to the adult payment, spread by the share on the full rate; for students, only the living allowance of the recipients the floor lifts (C4a) | [S136, S139, S143, S144] |
| 5. Tax and protections | A lognormal curve fitted to ATO 2023-24 taxable income bands is taxed under current rules, with the dividend added to taxable income, but not to any income test, and the threshold and LITO unchanged. Protected recipients' other income is inferred from the income tests and protected, with the offset capped at the tax current rules levy on the payment. The child payment is costed from the household model's shares, with a 1.5-year assessment lag and a response to its tapers | F6, C8, D4, C9 to C15, F14, E12, E14, F15 [S171] |
| 6. Behaviour and the rate rise | Market income falls 16 cents per dollar of net gain from the dividend or the child payment. Taxable income responds to a rise with an elasticity of 0.2 in the standard form ln(M1/M0) = 0.2 × ln((1 − m − t)/(1 − m)), where M is taxable income, m the average marginal rate and t the rise. Each legislated rise is the stage's largest need, rounded up to 0.1 of a point, in whichever of the aggregate and person-by-person forms is larger, with revenue from a per-point method that reproduces PBO costing ECR-2025-2525 | [S82, S140, S154] |
| 7. Funding | Each PBO costing starts in 2028-29, scaled by nominal GDP, and grows with GDP after the PBO's last year, except the wealth tax (nil), the gas measure (−11.2% a year) and the fuel-credit and coal-and-minerals measures, held flat in real terms (B7); the interaction line follows the PBO's year-by-year profile, grows with GDP after its last year and is charged to the fund, and to its capital after the guarantee lapses. For the wealth-tax fund, the fund and the durable taxes follow Table 10.8; the other options are costed as in Tables 10.2 and 10.5 | F1 to F12, F23 [S133] |
| 8. Implementation | Build, running, oversight, trial, evaluation and Community Contribution Fund costs are the author's estimates | Table 6.2 |
| 9. Accounting | Underlying cash, as in Budget Paper No. 1, with interest at 4.13% on the change in debt; the fund is treated as the Future Fund is, with its earnings outside the cash balance, a treatment Table 10.14 tests | [S61, S139, S168, S169] |
| 10. Paths and scenarios | The central path; Stages 2 and 3 on the smallest displacement that meets the need test and on trigger-timed paths; ranges, stress tests and displacement | Tables 6.6 to 6.10 and 6.34 to 6.53 |
| 11. Distribution | Static first-round cash effects per adult and per household, with the revenue measures allocated by decile in each year and in present value over 2028-29 to 2045-46 | Tables 6.14 to 6.27; F4-48 |
| 12. Net benefit | Implementation less administration savings, market income forgone and the excess burden of each financing measure, against the weighted reduction in the household poverty gap | Tables 6.11 and 6.13 |
| 13. Validation and rounding | Mean taxable income is A$77,673 against the ATO's A$78,127; 2026-27 GDP is A$3,093bn against A$3,088bn implied by the PBO; earlier Australian costings range from A$40bn to A$264bn a year. Components are rounded so that rows and totals sum | [S139, S171, S216] |
| 14. Reproduction | partc.py, partd.py, taper_test.py, partE.py, partF.py, the round scripts (partF_r8.py to partF_r13b.py) the household scripts (household.py to household_r13.py) and the scripts for this revision (optionb_hh.py, optionb_fiscal.py, optionb_report.py and optionb_check.py) regenerate every model table, and make_charts.py every chart; check_arithmetic.py re-adds every fiscal table, recomputes the rate-rise revenue and checks the tables against the model output and the red teams' errors | [S5] |

*Source: author's model (supplementary partF.py and the scripts listed in step 14) [S5].*

### The household model

The household model turns the income distribution into 40,000 synthetic households a year, so that poverty can be measured on the relative and the anchored line as ACOSS and UNSW measure it [S37]. Household types follow the 2021 Census [S217, S218, S219], incomes are equivalised with the modified OECD scale [S220], and partners' incomes are paired with an assumed correlation of 0.35. Tenure comes from the ABS imputed-rent study [S177, S193], and Rent Assistance follows current rules [S3, S221]. Three factors are calibrated to the line before housing costs, the 14.2% rate after them and the model's child poverty rate of 15.6%, the published figure [S38]; the last pushes synthetic FTB about 25% above the PBO's path. Take-up is 100% (Table 11.2).

**Table 11.2. Household model: calibration and validation against published figures, 2028-29 baseline (per cent unless stated)**

| Item | Model, 2028-29 baseline | Published figure | Source or status |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Poverty rate after housing costs, all persons | 14.2 | 14.2 (2022-23) | ACOSS–UNSW [S37]; calibrated |
| Poverty line before housing costs, single adult (A$ a week) | 718 | 718 | A$584 in 2022-23 grown 3.5% a year; calibrated |
| Children under 15 in poverty after housing costs | 15.6 | 15.6 (2022-23) | [S38]; calibrated |
| Persons in poverty after housing costs (million) | 3.73 | 3.71 (2022-23) | [S37]; not targeted |
| Lone persons / couples without children / couples with children / sole-parent families in poverty after housing costs | 33.8 / 11.5 / 7.8 / 31.9 | 25 / 10 / 11 / 34 (2019-20) | [S59]; not targeted |
| People aged 67 and over (published: 65 and over) in poverty after housing costs | 11.1 | 14 (2019-20) | [S59]; not targeted; model too low |
| People in households relying on JobSeeker / Youth Allowance / Parenting Payment | 99.4 / 100.0 / 80.8 | 76 / 73 / 71 (2022-23) | [S39]; not targeted; model too high |
| People in households relying on Disability Support Pension / Carer Payment / Age Pension | 63.6 / 86.9 / 23.3 | 56 / 40 / 35 (2022-23) | [S39]; not targeted |
| Gini coefficient, equivalised disposable household income; bottom-quintile income share | 0.325; 7.4 | 0.324; 7.4 (2019-20) | ABS [S222]; not targeted |
| Poverty after housing costs by tenure: outright / mortgage / private renters / public renters | 10.6 / 9.6 / 19.9 / 63.8 | 8 / 10 / 20 / 52 (2019-20) | [S59]; not targeted |
| Housing costs as a share of gross income, all households | 11.8 | 13.6 (2019-20) | ABS [S177]; below it by the calibrated housing-cost factor |
| FTB Parts A and B paid to synthetic families (A$ billion) | 23.2 | 18.6 | PBO path; model about 25% too high |
| Full-rate Age Pension, single, including supplements (A$ a year) | 34,807 | 34,770 (legal maximum) | DSS rates [S3]; within 0.1% after the correction of a double count |
| Take-up of the dividend | 100% (household model) | 100% for lodgers and 80% for non-lodgers (fiscal model) | Paid at assessment; the fiscal model leaves about 0.30 million non-lodgers unpaid |

*Source: author's household model (supplementary household_output_partf4.md, Tables P0, P6 and P9) [S5] and the sources cited. Note: 40,000 synthetic households a year; three parameters calibrated (an income level factor of 1.115, a housing-cost factor of 0.841 and a tilt in partnered parents' incomes of 0.27); partners' income correlation 0.35 assumed.*

Lone persons come out about 9 points too high and people aged 67 and over about 3 points too low. Full-rate pensioners' other income is assumed (Table 6.55), and the relative-line result for people aged 67 and over is sensitive to it: 12.1% to 13.5% across the variants nearest DSS data, with Part 1 at 11.3% to 11.8% (Table 6.23) [S135]. Households relying on JobSeeker, Youth Allowance or Carer Payment come out too poor, so levels for those groups are indicative, though comparisons between packages on the same population are less affected. Tables 10.26 and 10.27 share each decile's revenue measures equally among its adults, which spreads the wealth and superannuation measures more widely than they fall.

### Data gaps and secondary sources

Twenty-eight series, estimates or analyses could not be obtained or done, and Table 11.3 lists each with the proxy used. Secondary sources are marked in the source list; they include the 14% rate from 1 July 2027 and the 2026-27 capital gains tax reform, because Budget Paper No. 2 could not be opened. All web addresses were opened between 14 and 21 October 2026.

**Table 11.3. Data series and estimates that could not be found or opened, and the proxy used, October 2026**

| No. | Gap | Proxy or method used |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ABS population projections by year | Centre for Population path |
| 2 | Published Budget nominal GDP levels | Derived from Budget ratios |
| 3 | Cost of Commonwealth Rent Assistance in 2026-27 | Not needed: Rent Assistance is retained |
| 4 | A PBO labour-supply elasticity | OpenResearch estimate [S82] |
| 5 | ATO total taxable income by band | Lognormal curve fitted to the bands |
| 6 | An official series comparing productivity with real pay after 2021-22 | ABS market-sector growth rates (Figure 2.2) |
| 7 | An official poverty estimate after 2022-23: the ABS did not release its 2023-24 income survey [S45], and the HILDA 2026 poverty chapter could not be read [S223] | 2022-23 rate, with the 2026 poverty line and 2024 rental stress figure |
| 8 | A value of the Low Income Tax Offset in the Tax Expenditures and Insights Statement version opened | ATO and secondary sources [S130, S186] |
| 9 | A published position on a basic income from any state or territory, the Coalition of Peaks or the Regional Australia Institute | Related published positions (Table 9.2) |
| 10 | A youth employment-to-population ratio by occupation exposure | Youth unemployment as a provisional series (Table 9.6) |
| 11 | A PBO or Treasury costing of a uniform rise in every income-tax rate; the PBO's Build your own Budget model could not be downloaded [S224] | Per-point method anchored to ECR-2025-2525 (Table 6.39) |
| 12 | An Australian estimate of who bears an excess profits tax | Alternatives tested (Table 6.17) |
| 13 | Official AI-adjusted employment projections, and polls on a basic income since 2021 | JSA illustrative modelling; 2021 poll |
| 14 | Australian evidence on the price effects of the Coronavirus Supplement | Interest-rate and rent scenario (Tables 6.31 and 6.32) |
| 15 | An excess burden estimate for fuel excise or a wealth tax; the OECD's review of net wealth taxes gives none [S179] | Author's ranges, with Stage 1's net position shown at the ends of the wealth-tax range (Section 6; Table 6.11) |
| 16 | A split of FTB spending by Part A and Part B, or by family type | Stated proxies; top-up from A$1.9bn to A$5.5bn in 2028-29 |
| 17 | DSS data on protected recipients' earnings | Other income inferred from the income tests (C8) |
| 18 | Re-opening PBO costings ECR-2025-3193 and ECR-2025-3901 on 16 October 2026 | Figures and quotations from the pages opened on 14 October 2026 |
| 19 | Department of Social Services data on inflows to and re-entry into working-age payments | A quarter of the extra separations assumed to return to payment (range nil to half; Table 10.19) [S113] |
| 20 | An Australian estimate of the correlation of partners' incomes, and ACOSS–UNSW poverty rates by household type for 2022-23 | 0.35, tested at 0.2 and 0.5; 2019-20 rates [S59] |
| 21 | An RBA or Treasury estimate of the cash-rate response to a fiscal impulse | Stated range anchored on MARTIN, which has no fiscal simulation [S167] |
| 22 | The PBO's component lines for ECR-2025-3193 (re-fetch blocked), needed to place the 25% of its cost the floor method does not reproduce | The remainder is assumed to be partner tapers, waiting periods and indexation; any shortfall brings Stage 1's levy forward (Table 10.19) |
| 23 | The age split of Migration Program grants | Adults assumed to be 70% of grants (55% to 75%) for the waiting-period stock of 0.52 million, and children 30% for the child payment's one-year wait [S124] |
| 24 | ATO tax by bracket for the top tail, and tax-free superannuation pension income by age | Not done: the legislated rise is the larger of the aggregate and person-by-person forms of the response; tax-free pensions remain outside the base |
| 25 | Incomes of temporary residents, incidence by age, consumer incidence of the fuel and gas measures, couples' labour supply and a macroeconomic model | Not modelled: temporary residents are outside the rate-rise base, as legislated; the others are conceded (Table 8.2; Recommendation 13) |
| 26 | Data on within-year falls in family income | Review estimated from divorce, birth and retrenchment counts (supplementary household_output_partf4.md, Table P11); in the net cost; the lag's effect on child poverty bounded crudely (Table 6.24) |
| 27 | De facto separations involving children | Assumed equal in number to divorces |
| 28 | Take-up of the income-fall review, the share of reviews that end in a debt, and the cost per review | Assumed: 60% take-up; 25% (births) and 50% (job loss) end in a debt; A$100 a review |

*Source: author's research log, as recorded in model Tables C0, D0, E0, F0 and F4-0, household_output_partf4.md and the red teams' reviews [S5, S134, S174, S191, S196].*
